**[[Siege of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal]]** was a campaign of the [[Undying Olympian Empire]] fought at Depth 40 of [[Tartarus (Realm)|Tartarus]], on the Cinder Flats above the bend of the [[Phlegethon]]. The Third Siege Army of Tartarus spent ten rotations and five months of front time, eighteen rotations by the theatre's clock, reducing the forge-hold of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal after its Archflamen, Sabinus Orrhae, turned it against the Empire and called up the powers of the deep rivers to defend it. The [[Répertoire du Savoir Athénien]] logged 88.6 million transfers over the course of the siege and entered 4.31 million of them as null. Command passed from Lord-General Aurelian Castorre to Marshal Theron Kastellan and finally to [[Thalia Grace|Lieutenant Thalia Grace]] of the [[Hunters of Artemis|Hunt of the Director]], who was posted to the Flats by [[Artemis|the Director]] as a punishment for the [[Themis Needle]]. In the eighteenth rotation, [[Minerva (AI)|Minerva]] and [[Verrill Chase]] fell onto the Scree in a burning Dominion lantern and turned the final assault, closing the account of the [[AnnChase Pattern|AnnChase]] battleship *Eye of Light* that Minerva had crashed into Tartarus in [[Ch15 - Rex Invictus VIII|Chapter 15]].

## 1 Prologue

> *"He went down into the dark alone. He knows your name. When he comes back up the road you are walking, the deep will be emptied and the vats will close."*
>
> Archflamen Sabinus Orrhae, sermon at Lethe Hold, Rotation Minus Four

### 1.1 The Archflamen of the Deep

After the previous [[Sabinus Orrhae|Archflamen]] of the Tartarean theatre, [[Helvius Strabo]], died at [[Hold Serapis]] in [[Imperial Calendar|Rotation]] Minus Six, following six hundred and four Gaian years of life greatly extended by the physicians of the Apollonian wing, his successor, Sabinus Orrhae, was named by the [[Pontifical College]] within the cycle. Orrhae was forty-one, a [[Legacy|legacy]] of [[Apollo]] on his mother's side and of [[Hestia]] on his father's, and had been a Flamen of the [[Percy Jackson|Lord Imperator]] since his nineteenth year. He had served on the ceiling relays and in the [[Lethe]] holds, and by the reckoning of the College he had buried more soldiers than any priest of his rank. The office he took was an old one and a poor one. The Archflamen's duty was to tour the holds, confirm their Flamens, bless the consecrated ordnance and send the College an account of the faith of twenty-seven million soldiers who mostly had little time for it.

The Tartarean theatre was the largest and the oldest of the Empire's fronts, and also the least regarded by those who did not serve in it. It ran from the ceiling cracks through which the Empire's ships came down into the deep, through the Lethe holds and the [[Acheron]] stairs, to the Fire Steps along the [[Phlegethon]] at Depth 40 and below, and it was held by twenty-seven million soldiers in four hundred sectors against whatever the rivers sent up. Three authorities divided its government between them. The [[Pyrrhus Command]] at [[Watchpoint Hades]] held the armies and decided where they would fight. The [[Répertoire du Savoir Athénien]], the Athenian calculating engine whose verdicts governed supply, [[Transfer (UOE)|transfer]] and discipline across the theatre, decided what each fight was worth and how it would be paid for. The Pontifical College, on the ceiling, held the souls of the soldiers, which in practice meant the Flamens of the holds, the vat-rites and the consecration of ordnance. Of the three, the College had the least power and the longest memory, and its relations with the other two had been poor for centuries. The Command regarded the Flamens as a necessary expense of morale. The Répertoire carried them in its tables as a line of throughput beside nectar and shell. The College had long believed that both had forgotten what the soldiers of the deep were dying for, and it expected its Archflamen to remind them without ever saying so aloud.

Over the course of five rotations, Orrhae undertook a tour of the theatre, his new charge. He travelled with an entourage of a thousand preachers, acolytes, scribes, chamberlains and servants, along with forty [[Vestal Virgins|Vestals]] of the House of the Hearth, armed with short [[Spears|spears]] and Hestian lamps that never went out. The Vestals were a gift from the Virgo Maxima of the House to celebrate his enthronement. The roads between the holds were not safe, and an Archflamen had to be ready to protect his flock with something more than prayer.

Orrhae wished to see the holds he must now serve, and to bring a revival of faith to the soldiers in them. Where he and his entourage passed, he amassed ever more followers. His preaching departed from the liturgy of the College. He taught that the Lord Imperator had gone down into [[Tartarus (Realm)|Tartarus]] alone, before any of his listeners were born, that every soldier who died in the deep and woke in the vats was walking the Imperator's road, and that the Imperator knew each of them by name and would one day return up that road to empty the deep and close the vats. To soldiers who had been dying and waking for years, it was a powerful message. Attendance at the vat-rites doubled in every hold he visited, and soldiers began to answer the rite with the words "He knows me", which appeared in no liturgy the College had approved. Some extremists in the holds began to call him the Imperator's voice. Amongst all this devotion the Archflamen saw, perhaps for the first time, the true power that came with his office. In the deep, far from the College, he felt he could act against the doubters who stood between the soldiers and their salvation.

The College read the reports on the ceiling and was pleased. Faith was falling in every theatre of the Empire, and here was a man raising it in the worst of them. The commanders of the holds were less pleased. Soldiers petitioned for posting to the Archflamen's escort and were refused, and some deserted to join it anyway. [[Commander Thaleia]] of [[Lethe Hold]], whose Sector 74B received him in Rotation Minus Four, logged his visit as a false positive under Code 18 and amended it two rotations later, when thirty-eight of her soldiers requested transfer to his escort, all thirty-eight were refused and nine deserted. Thralls from the ore lifts and pilgrims from the ceiling worlds followed his crawlers on foot. By the third rotation of the tour the thousand had become eleven thousand, with quartermasters and Lictors of its own.

Orrhae voiced his thinking to his advisors, in particular the [[Ilan Dace|Haruspex Ilan Dace]], who had joined the household at Hold Serapis in the second rotation of the tour as an advisor on the Phlegethon holds. Dace was thirty, a legacy of [[Hecate]] and [[Hermes]], and was favoured by the Archflamen as a practical man with knowledge of the deep holds that no priest had time to learn. He was also, as the Hunt's inquiry established long after the siege, a man who had been drinking from the Lethe in measured doses since before his twentieth year, until the waters had emptied him of himself and left room for something else. The inquiry did not name what had filled that room.

Dace encouraged the Archflamen into believing himself the chosen servant of the Imperator, who alone could restore the faith of the deep. He convinced Orrhae that there were those who opposed him and the thousands who followed him. The College was full of men who had never been below Depth 3. The Répertoire counted the faithful as throughput, and the Pyrrhus Command spent them as ammunition. With Dace at his ear, the Archflamen came to trust almost no one. He gathered about him an inner circle of perhaps forty advisors, most of them chosen by Dace and each sworn to him personally on the [[Styx]], and Dace kept a file on every one of them. In private the Archflamen began to speak of a war of faith, a cleansing of the deep led from below by soldiers who knew what they fought for. If that war was ever to come, it needed a hold of its own, apart from the College and the Répertoire, in which to prepare.

After five rotations of touring, during which he was increasingly received as a holy man by the soldiers and thralls of the theatre, the Archflamen went into seclusion at the [[Pyriphlegethon Arsenal]] at Depth 40. The Arsenal was a forge-hold of the Hephaestean orders, eight thousand years old, standing on a spur of black basalt over the bend of the Phlegethon. Its casting lines drew Phlegethon fire through ceramic heat-wells to cast and quench consecrated ordnance, and produced eighty-one percent of the lineage-keyed [[Divine Material Ammunition|divine mortar]] shells and [[Bunker Busters|aedificant]] builder-shells fired in the Tartarean theatre. Its vaults held eight thousand years of the theatre's reserve stocks of ammunition, armour, vehicles and food. It was also the theatre's holiest shrine. Under the cinder cone at its heart lay the [[Katabasion]], the tomb of the First Descent, where the bones of [[Legio Prima Katabasis]], the first Imperial legion to go down into Tartarus, had been laid in a hall of red marble. Over it stood the [[Acropolis (Pyriphlegethon)|Acropolis]], a walled hold on the summit of the cone, with a palace for the Archflamen should he ever visit, the House of the Hearth for the Vestals who kept the reliquary lamps, the [[Tabularium (Pyriphlegethon)|Tabularium]] of the [[Enforcement|Enforcement detachment]] and the [[Echo Mast]], the [[Noosphere|noosphere]] relay spire that carried the Arsenal's traffic to Watchpoint Hades. No Archflamen had visited in three hundred years. Here, Orrhae chose his battlefield.

Though the Archflamen commanded no forces himself, the Arsenal's charter allowed it to raise a militia of its own in time of peril. Nobody had read the charter in three thousand years. Dace had. His preachers moved among the casting lines and the ore lifts warning that the Arsenal was in danger, that the College was jealous of the Archflamen and the Répertoire afraid of him, and that the Pyrrhus Command would one day come to take the Katabasion and the faithful with it. The Archflamen himself lived in the reliquary, ate thrall bread and preached every night to the forge-thralls coming off shift. The thralls were indentured labourers, debt-bonded on the ceiling worlds and shipped down to work the heat-wells for terms few of them would survive. They carried no implants, and when a thrall died at Depth 40 he stayed dead. Orrhae told them that the Imperator knew them too, and they became the most devoted of his followers.

Simultaneously, Dace was building an inner circle of fanatically loyal followers from the eleven thousand and from the thralls, which he named the [[Sworn of Orrhae]]. The commander of the Arsenal [[Arsenal Garrison Cohorts|Garrison Cohorts]], [[Galba Ostorian|Prefect Galba Ostorian]], who had been passed over for promotion three times, was enticed into the circle with a seat at the Archflamen's table. The thrall-masters received promises of their own. When the Steward of the Arsenal, a Hephaestean named Corran Ludd who had no patience for priests, fell down the Number Four heat-well, his deputy, who had sworn on the Styx, took over the casting lines. The loyalty of such men was deemed vital by the Haruspex, and over eleven months the Arsenal changed hands without a shot being fired. Only the Enforcement detachment in the Tabularium, sixty strong under [[Aemilia Rusk|Lictor-Superior Aemilia Rusk]], remained outside the Archflamen's influence.

It was a measure of the theatre's neglect of the Arsenal that none of this was noticed for most of a year. The Arsenal's output reports went up the Echo Mast every cycle and were correct in every particular. Its ration draw rose by a third, which the Répertoire attributed to the Archflamen's pilgrims and approved. Its Steward's death was logged as an industrial accident, of which the casting lines produced several hundred each rotation. The Command had last inspected the Arsenal's garrison in Rotation Minus Nineteen and found it adequate for a hold that had not been attacked in living memory, and the [[Tartarus Front Soldiers|Third Siege Army]], whose line ran north of the Phlegethon bend within sight of the spur, had been told for six centuries that the Arsenal was the Hephaestean orders' business and not its own. The College received the Archflamen's own letters, which spoke of a great revival among the thralls and asked for more preachers. It sent them. By the time Lictor-Superior Rusk's reports began to describe what the revival had become, the Arsenal's chain of command below the Archflamen consisted almost entirely of men who had sworn to him on the Styx, and an oath sworn on the Styx was a matter the Empire's courts did not undo.

### 1.2 The Attempt on the Archflamen (Rotation Minus One, Cycle 312)

The Archflamen's gospel to the debt-bonded and the dying of the Arsenal spread like fire through the casting lines. The promise of the Imperator's return gave them hope of an escape from their brutal and short lives. But it also reached other ears. Lictor-Superior Rusk had been sending reports up the Echo Mast since the Archflamen's arrival, and at Watchpoint Hades the Répertoire du Savoir Athénien cross-indexed them against the attendance reports of the Flamens, the desertion rolls of the holds on the Archflamen's route, the Arsenal's ration draw and the oath-well records at the Styx. The Répertoire had seen it before: priests whose following led them to believe that they spoke for the Imperator himself. It weighed the Arsenal's output, still at ninety-eight percent of quota, against the risk of an apostate in control of eighty-one percent of the theatre's shells, and against the harm the public death of an Archflamen would do to the faith of twenty-seven million soldiers. On the three hundredth cycle of the last rotation before the war, it rendered judgement.

> CODE 31. JUDICIUM PETO. SUBMITTED FROM: TABULARIUM DETACHMENT, PYRIPHLEGETHON ARSENAL.
> VERDICT: ERASURE. SUBJECT: ORRHAE, SABINUS, ARCHFLAMEN. METHOD: SINGLE ROUND, PUBLIC, DURING RITE. FALSE FLAG: PHLEGETHON INCURSION. AVE IMPERATOR.

A marksman of the Enforcement detachment, [[Tertia Vell]], lay in wait for eleven days in a disused cooling flue off the third terrace of the Pilgrim Stair. On the three hundred and twelfth cycle, during the vat-rite before ten thousand thralls, she fired. The round failed to end the Archflamen's tour. He was saved by a Hestian aegis pendant worn under his vestments, the gift of the Virgo Maxima, of which the Tabularium knew nothing. A second shot cracked the pendant without harming him. Vell was taken on the terraces below the flue. Dace told the crowd that the Phlegethon had sent an assassin, and when the Enforcement flash on her sleeve was found, he told them that the ceiling had. The Répertoire's false flag had become the Archflamen's proof.

After the failed attempt, the Arsenal erupted in open revolt. The Tabularium was stormed the same cycle. Rusk's sixty held the gate-towers and the bridge to the inner [[Energy Shielding|ward]] for nine hours before they were overrun. Fifty-eight of them woke in the vats at the ceiling depot and reported what they had seen. Rusk did not wake. Her transfer was logged as [[Null Transfer|null]], the first of the siege. Something had been poured into her mouth before she died, and the Répertoire did not yet know what it was. That night the Archflamen declared the Arsenal the Imperator's Hold, separate from the College, the Répertoire and the Command. He freed the thralls from their bonds, named the garrison the Imperator's legion, and promised that the deep would rise to defend him, since the deep was where the Imperator had gone. The Echo Mast went silent.

The reports of the fifty-eight survivors of the Tabularium were the last account of the Arsenal from within that the Empire would have for many rotations, and they were read at Watchpoint Hades with growing alarm. They described a garrison that had gone over almost entire, a thrall population armed from the vaults within hours of the revolt, and a household around the Archflamen in which the Haruspex gave orders and the Prefect obeyed them. Several of the survivors reported that the Haruspex had gone among the Tabularium's dead and wounded after the storming with a flask, and that Rusk had been carried to him alive. The Répertoire classified those accounts as the products of stress and transfer shock, which they may in part have been, and filed them under a [[CODE 18]] query that was not reopened for many rotations. Rusk's null was entered as an artefact of the Phlegethon's interference with the Arsenal's relay, which was the explanation the Third's technical officers would give for every null of the siege's first rotation. The Mast itself remained intact, and the Third's signallers noted through the following year that it was still drawing power and still transmitting, on bands the Third did not use and could not read.

By seizing the Arsenal, the Archflamen left the Tartarean theatre without its supply of consecrated ordnance. The holds held eleven weeks of stock at the current rate of fire. After that, divine mortar batteries in four hundred sectors would be firing ordinary [[High-Explosive (HE)|high explosive]] at things that ordinary high explosive did not kill. The Arsenal's casting lines could not be replaced. The ceiling foundries could produce keyed shells only by sending each [[Demigod|demigod]] keyer to the forge in person, and their whole output for a rotation would supply the theatre for nine days. The Répertoire calculated that the Lethe holds would collapse first, then the Acheron stairs, then the Fire Steps, and that Tartarus would then push up into the ceiling cracks the Empire used for transit and out into the realspace systems they served. Its first rationing order went out the same cycle.

> SECTOR 72-A, DEPTH 4. AMMUNITION ALLOCATION CONFLICT. VERDICT: REDIRECT ALL 7.5MM SLUGS TO DEPTHS 88B TO 99C. LETHE SECTORS TO DEFAULT TO PLASMA INCINERATION PACKAGES. PRIORITIZE DENSITY OVER KINETIC ACCURACY.

The failure of the verdict produced the first of the siege's recriminations, and they began at once. The College protested to the Command that the Répertoire had ordered the public killing of an Archflamen of the Lord Imperator, during a rite, without consulting the College or informing it, and that the attempt had handed the Archflamen the martyrdom he lacked. The Répertoire replied that the College had been informed of the Archflamen's conduct for three rotations by the Flamens of the holds on his route and had done nothing, and that the method of the verdict had been chosen to spare the College the trial of one of its own. The Enforcement directorate, whose Lictor-Superior had died null at the Tabularium, blamed both. The Command took no position. Its concern was the shells, and the rationing order of the same cycle reached every battery in the theatre before the arguments over its cause had reached the ceiling. Divine mortar batteries were put on a ration of eleven keyed rounds a cycle, and sectors of lesser priority on four. In the Lethe holds, where the deep pressed hardest and the divine batteries were the only weapons that answered it, Commander Thaleia's Sector 74B and its neighbours were ordered to fall back to their second positions to shorten their lines, and did so within the rotation. Nobody in the theatre believed that the rationing could be sustained for long.

The Répertoire deemed that the Arsenal must be returned to the Empire. By the time the order was given it was already too late to do so cheaply. Millions of rebels were digging into prepared positions across the [[Cinder Flats]], and the Arsenal's sixty [[Ceiling-Lance|ceiling-lances]] denied the geometry above it to any ship of the [[UOE Navy|Tartarus Fleet]]. The task fell to the Third Siege Army of Tartarus, which had held the Flats north of the Phlegethon bend for six hundred years, in what would become a war of attrition lasting eighteen rotations by the theatre's clock and consuming more than eighty-eight million transfers.

The choice of the Third was not made without dispute. The College asked that the Archflamen be offered terms before any army moved, and proposed that a delegation of its own Flamens go down the Pilgrim Stair under truce to negotiate the return of the casting lines in exchange for a hearing of the Archflamen's doctrine before the full College. The Répertoire put the delegation's chance of success at under one percent and its chance of adding to the Archflamen's hostages at over sixty, and the Command refused it. The Enforcement directorate asked for a Lictor action to take the Acropolis by stealth, and was told that the Tabularium's fall had shown what a Lictor action at the Arsenal could achieve. The Pyrrhus Command was reluctant to draw soldiers from any other front, for every line in the theatre was already short of shells and would soon be short of soldiers as well, and the Third was the only army in Tartarus that could be committed without moving another. It was already on the Flats and knew the ground, and it had built the Arsenal's inner belt and held the copies of its plans. Above all, its line north of the Phlegethon bend could be thinned to a screen for the duration of the siege without exposing anything the Command considered vital. In the last cycles of Rotation Minus One, the Command assigned the reduction of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal to the Third Siege Army, and the Répertoire began to price it.

### 1.3 Preparations

The Empire's preparations for the siege were simple. The Répertoire's question was how many transfers the Arsenal would cost. The Third had to overwhelm three belts of trenches, destroy the ceiling-lances and eventually storm the Acropolis, and every one of those operations could be reduced to a figure. Everything in the Third was to be an expendable resource. Its soldiers carried implants at the base of the skull that uploaded their patterns at death through the Third's relays to Watchpoint Hades, where the Répertoire wrote them into new bodies grown in vats of nectar. A transfer cost four hundred litres of nectar, eleven seconds of the Watchpoint's bandwidth and nine days of vat time, and the Répertoire priced every metre of the Flats in transfers before it ordered an attack. Regiments would go into battle knowing how many of their number were expected to go up taking their objectives. The order assigning the siege to the Third set no ceiling on transfers. It set one on nulls, the transfers that arrived at the vats empty: none above three percent. The Third was expected to take the Arsenal in four rotations of front time, and to recover the casting lines intact if possible.

On the spur, the Archflamen's victory had escalated into four months of violence among the six million souls who now held it. The thrall gangs settled scores with the thrall-masters and then with each other. The Garrison Cohorts split over whether to follow Prefect Ostorian, and the half that refused were thrown into the Number Four heat-well. Pilgrims who tried to leave by the ore lifts were shot on the platforms. New leaders emerged from the garrison and the thrall gangs. Those who proved their loyalty were raised over their fellows. The best of them went into the Sworn of Orrhae, who wore the Archflamen's mark on their foreheads in ash, or were made [[Lictors of the Flame]], the Archflamen's corps of enforcers, given flame-orange sashes and rods of office and charged with keeping order in the ranks by any means and without appeal. After the bloodshed had ended, the Archflamen's commanders set about their first task, which was to prepare the Arsenal for the Empire's assault. They had to train an army, and they had eight thousand years of the theatre's stores to arm it from.

The Archflamen ordered that the Arsenal be made a slaughterhouse. No defender could expect mercy from an Empire that had sent a killer to his altar before ten thousand witnesses, so every defender must fight or be killed. Those who fought well would be rewarded, and those who failed would answer to the Lictors. The thralls' freedom was short-lived. They were soon put back to work for their new master, digging the Arsenal's defences across the Cinder Flats, where an outer trench belt had been begun a century before against the Phlegethon and never finished. They laid [[Mines (explosive)|mines]] and unrolled razor-wire by the thousand kilometres. The Arsenal's gunners ranged their divine mortars on marker stakes across the Flats until every metre was known, and the garrison trained the thralls with rifles from the vaults. When it came, the battle would be hard fought and long.

The Archflamen also made war with letters. In the months before the Third came down the Stair, the Arsenal's scribes copied his sermons by the hundred thousand and sent them up the ore lifts to the ceiling and out along the pilgrim roads to the holds, together with an open letter to the soldiers of the theatre. It declared that the Archflamen held the Arsenal for the Imperator and for them, that the College had abandoned the deep and the Répertoire had priced it, and that any soldier who laid down his rifle rather than fire upon the Imperator's Hold would be received there as a brother. Few soldiers answered it. The Third's own desertion rate rose by less than a tenth of a percent, and most of those who tried to reach the Arsenal were taken by the river on the way. The letter's effect was felt elsewhere. In the holds along the Archflamen's old route, attendance at the vat-rites fell by half within a rotation, the Flamens reported that soldiers no longer answered the rite at all rather than give the response the Archflamen had taught them, and the College, which had praised his revival for five rotations, found itself unable to explain to the Command why it had not seen what the revival was. The College's standing in the theatre never recovered from the siege, and its loss of influence began here, before a shot had been fired on the Flats.

### 1.4 The Archflamen's Forces

The Archflamen's army was formidable above all for its size. No exact figure was ever available. The Répertoire worked from the Arsenal's last census, eleven months out of date, its ration draw and the noosphere traffic before the Mast went dark, and concluded that about six million, two hundred thousand souls were on the spur. All of them had to be counted as hostile, and by the Archflamen's decree all were his soldiers. Quality varied greatly, from fanatical and well-equipped troops to an untrained mass.

The best were the Sworn of Orrhae. They were the Archflamen's own, chosen by Dace's preachers for devotion and by the Lictors for nerve, and many had served in the holds before following his crawlers. Every one had sworn the Styx oath to the Archflamen personally, and in return they had first pick of the Arsenal's stores. The Répertoire estimated forty thousand. The true figure was nearer ninety thousand, and they would form the disciplined core of the defence. Dace organised them in companies of about three hundred, each under an officer of his own choosing, and distributed them through the belts so that no stretch of the line was held by thralls or garrison alone. Their officers held authority over any garrison or thrall officer in their sector regardless of rank, a practice Ostorian disliked and could not change. In battle the Sworn held the nests, led the counter-attacks and provided the raiding parties that would trouble the Third's sentries for the whole of the siege. They were also the Archflamen's guarantee against his own army, for a thrall cohort that wavered in the line would find the Sworn behind it as well as the Lictors.

After the Sworn in quality came the Arsenal Garrison Cohorts. A forge-hold that made most of a theatre's shells needed a large garrison against the deep, and the Arsenal's was the equal of a ceiling world's planetary cohorts, built around a backbone of retired line legacies and old centurions posted there to finish their service in a quiet place. They lacked the training of a Trench Regiment, but the vaults supplied them with [[Thureos Tank|Thureos]] tanks, [[Hoplon Carrier|Hoplon]] carriers and divine mortars by the hundred. The Répertoire judged them able to man a static line and little more. It was mistaken. The garrison's old centurions had trained it to the standard of the holds, and under Ostorian it would conduct the Arsenal's counter-attacks with a skill that cost the Third dearly in the first rotations of the siege.

The mass of the army was drawn from the forge-thralls. There were about four million of them on the spur when the Archflamen freed them. They had no implants, no home to return to and every reason to hate the thrall-masters and the ceiling that had sold them down, and many had joined the revolt willingly. One in four had already drilled with the militia company the Arsenal's charter required the thralls to supply. Among them were some forty thousand [[Laistrygonian Half-Bloods|Laistrygonian half-bloods]], giant-blooded labourers bred by the Hephaestean orders for the heavy work of the casting lines, three metres tall and kept by the Lictors on combat stimulants and chained in their barracks between battles. Armed from the vaults, the thralls would be capable of holding a trench alongside the garrison.

The last source of manpower was the pilgrims and the Arsenal's own clerks and scribes, who had never held a rifle. Many pilgrims saw the Archflamen as a holy man and had little idea whom they were fighting or why. They had been told that the Empire meant to defile the Katabasion and carry the bones of the First Descent up to the ceiling, and they joined the militia to defend the Imperator's dead. They were given the worst rifles and the last rations.

What made the Archflamen's army dangerous beyond its numbers was its equipment. The Arsenal's vaults had been filled over eight thousand years as the reserve of the whole theatre, against the day a reformation peak broke the line somewhere and an army had to be re-armed from nothing, and they had never been emptied. The vault inventory held rifles for every soul on the spur several times over, armoured vehicles in their thousands, Thureos tanks and Hoplon carriers among them, and divine mortars and their keyed ammunition in quantities the Répertoire's own clerks had not totalled in a century. It held the heat-well tools of the casting lines, which the Arsenal's armourers would turn into weapons as the siege went on, and enough preserved rations to feed six million for a decade at the thrall scale. The Archflamen's army would not run short of shells until long after the theatre outside it had. Its shortage was of soldiers who knew how to use what it had. A thrall could be taught to fire a rifle in a week and to hold a trench in a month, but the Arsenal had few trained gunners outside the garrison, and fewer still who could key a divine shell. The Arsenal's keyers were demigods of the Hephaestean orders, posted there for the casting lines, and the revolt had left the Archflamen about four hundred of them. They became the most valuable soldiers on the spur, and the Lictors guarded them more closely than they guarded the Archflamen.

Behind all of them stood the Archflamen's preachers, who moved through the ranks preaching his gospel at every shift change, and the Lictors of the Flame, who stood behind every company and shot whoever ran. In the pens beneath the Tabularium were also three hundred and eleven unregistered [[Hecatean Strays|Hecatean strays]], children and young people with the witch-blood showing, gathered from the deep holds to await the next Tithe Day. Strays without a registry implant were open doors, and the things of the deep found them easily. The Archflamen called them his children and fed them at his own table. Dace sat with them every night and asked them what they heard.

Command of this army was divided in a way the Third never fully understood until the siege was over. The Archflamen held it in name and showed little interest in its use. He preached, prayed in the Katabasion and received the reports of his commanders as a priest receives confessions, with sympathy and without instruction. Prefect Ostorian commanded in the field. He was a competent officer of the garrison school, careful with his reserves, who knew the Flats as well as any centurion of the Third and had spent twenty years watching the Third fight on them, and his defence of the belts was conducted on sound principles throughout. Dace held no military rank and commanded nothing. He ran the preachers, the Lictors and the Sworn through the officers he had chosen, he controlled the strays, and from the second rotation of the siege onward he controlled the Arsenal's dealings with the river, about which Ostorian was told as little as possible. The Third's intelligence officers spent most of the siege trying to understand Ostorian's decisions. In the end it was Dace's that mattered.

### 1.5 The Third Siege Army

The force sent to reduce the Arsenal was the Third Siege Army of Tartarus. It was a trench army, built to hold a line against the deep indefinitely and to break the reformation peaks that came up out of the Phlegethon every few rotations by digging up to them and shelling them flat. Its soldiers were legacies and [[Mortal|mortal]]-line troops drafted from the ceiling worlds at twelve and thirteen, with a scattering of demigods posted down for their powers, and they were trained to dig before they were trained to shoot. Its motto, cut over the door of every one of its depots, was Quod cadit, surgit. What falls, rises. The soldiers of the Third were loyal, disciplined and long accustomed to death. A regiment could be ordered to take a position knowing that eight hundred of its soldiers would go up taking it, and most of the eight hundred would be back in the line within a fortnight. The Third's officers treated a regiment's dead as a form of supply, and its soldiers had learned to do the same. The system had its costs. Soldiers who went up too many times woke slower, and some woke strange, and a soldier taken by the rivers did not upload at all.

The Third was organised in Phalanxes. Four Line Phalanxes of Trench Regiments held the line, the Second and Fifth in the north and the Seventh and Ninth in the south. Two Assault Phalanxes held the armour, Thureos main battle tanks, [[Colossus Super-Heavy|Colossus]] super-heavies and Hoplon assault carriers able to put fifty soldiers onto an enemy parapet behind a ramp as thick as a bunker door. For a siege the armour would serve only as a breakthrough and exploitation force, and would not man the lines. The Third intended to deny the Archflamen any chance of a war of manoeuvre.

At the outset of the siege the Third's order of battle listed forty-one Trench Regiments, nineteen ordnance regiments and eleven armoured regiments, with the supporting companies of engineers, signallers and medici that a trench army needed to live in the ground for years at a time. A Trench Regiment was a large formation by the standards of other theatres, between one hundred and seventy and one hundred and ninety thousand soldiers at full strength, divided into cohorts of about six hundred and forty, each of nine or ten maniples, and each maniple into sections that dug, ate and slept together. Every regiment kept its own heavy maniples of breachers, soldiers in two hundred kilograms of plate who led assaults on fortified positions and were trained to fight in the galleries beneath them, and its own outrider squadrons, mounted on armoured hounds bred on the ceiling worlds, who scouted, raided and pursued. The engineers held the [[Mattock Crawler|Mattock]] crawlers, broad-tracked machines with dozer blades that filled ditches and cut trenches under fire, and the heat-rods with which the Third found the Phlegethon's veins beneath the ash. The Third's regiments were recruited by region of the ceiling, kept their numbers for centuries, and were replaced as units only when they were destroyed. Their soldiers, when they were transferred, returned to the same regiment, and in most cases to the same cohort, so that a cohort of the Third might hold soldiers who had served in it for thirty years and died in it a dozen times.

Artillery was the heart of the plan, and the siege would be a gunner's war. Each Trench Regiment had its own mortar companies. Behind them stood the divine mortar batteries, whose shells were keyed at the breech by a demigod of the battery, so that a shell keyed by a child of Apollo burst in solar fire, one keyed by a child of Artemis in lunar shards and one keyed by a child of [[Demeter]] in thorns. The aedificant batteries fired builder-shells keyed by children of [[Athena]] and [[Hephaestus]], which could fold a bunker's roof into its floor. Behind everything stood the [[Tenth Ordnance Phalanx]], with its siege guns and the [[Phaethon Heavy Mortar|Phaethon]]-pattern heavy mortars, the largest guns in the theatre. The Third would fight the Arsenal with the Arsenal's own shells until those ran out, and afterwards with whatever the ceiling could make.

Behind the guns stood the system that kept the Third in the field, and it was the Third's true strength. The implants of the Third's soldiers uploaded through a chain of relay pylons that ran across the Flats behind the line and up the shelf wall to the ceiling, from where the uploads passed through the noosphere to Watchpoint Hades. The vats at the Watchpoint could return a transferred soldier to the Third in about nine days and to his regiment within a fortnight. The Répertoire allotted the Third a share of the Watchpoint's vat capacity for the siege, about twenty-four thousand recoveries a cycle, which it considered ample for any rate of loss a trench war could produce. It did not allot the Third any share of the theatre's nectar reserve beyond its peacetime ration, on the reasoning that a siege of four rotations would not exhaust it. Both decisions would be revisited. The Third's supply ran down the relay cables from the ceiling at [[Landing Shelf Kappa]] and down the [[Cinder Stair]], and from the foot of the Stair across the Flats on rail and on the backs of the labour gangs, and every shell, ration and replacement the Third received for eighteen rotations would come to it by that road. The Arsenal had no road at all, except the ore lifts to the ceiling, which the Third closed in the first week, and the river.

The Tartarus Fleet could do little to help. Its ships could hold station in the low geometry beneath the ceiling and lower supplies down the cables, and they could bombard the deep anywhere outside the Arsenal's lance envelope, but within it they could not survive. The sixty ceiling-lances had been sited eight thousand years before to clear the geometry above the Flats of anything that flew, and their engagement envelope covered the whole of the spur, the inner and second belts and most of the outer belt. The Fleet's commander in the Phlegethon reaches, [[Iole Saturnin|Navarch Iole Saturnin]], was ordered to support the siege with a squadron from the [[Cruiser|heavy cruiser]] [[Pallas Ascendant]], to escort the Third's convoys and to stand ready to bring heavy guns down into any corridor the Third opened in the envelope. The destruction of the lances was therefore one of the siege's first objectives, and it would prove far harder to achieve than the Répertoire expected.

The Third knew the Cinder Flats as well as any army in the theatre. They were six thousand square kilometres of black ash and cinder over basalt, on a shelf above the bend of the Phlegethon, lit only by the burning river. Ninety kilometres overhead hung the ceiling, where the Empire's ships held station in the low geometry. There was no rain at Depth 40, only seep, vapour that rose off the Phlegethon and came down every few cycles as a storm of hot black water that turned the Flats into a grey paste a metre deep. To the south and east of the spur the shelf broke into the chasms of the [[Mora Cut]], the [[Lethe Cut]] and the [[Demus Gorge]], impassable to an army on foot, so that any attack had to come across the Flats from the north and west.

That was where the Archflamen's commanders had dug. The outer belt ran in an arc of a hundred and ninety kilometres from the Mora Cut in the north to the edge of the Demus Gorge in the south-west. It was the youngest and weakest of the three, and it was unfinished when the revolt began. The second belt lay inside it at about thirty kilometres from the spur. The inner belt was six hundred years old and in places eight kilometres deep, and the Third had dug it itself, in the days when the Arsenal was the Third's anchor against the Phlegethon. Its plans were in the Third's own archive. Inside it rose the [[Cinder Wall]], thirty metres high, ringing the base of the spur, and above the Wall the cinder cone and the Acropolis. The sixty ceiling-lances in their silos across the spur and the inner belt were the reason the Empire could not simply bring a fleet down on the Arsenal. They were Phlegethon-fed beams built eight thousand years before to keep [[Monster|monsters]] out of the geometry above Depth 40, and they could reach anything holding station over the Flats. The Arsenal would have to be taken from the ground.

Command of the Third was held by [[Aurelian Castorre|Lord-General Aurelian Castorre]] from his headquarters on [[Quarterdeck Nineteen]], in the ceiling above the Flats. Castorre was a nobleman of a Pyrrhus Command family and an Athenian by training, taught the Tactica Olympica at the Academy on [[Callistus]], with forty years on the Tartarus front and a reputation as a careful commander who did not waste soldiers he could not replace. His chief of staff, [[Leda Corvis]], was an Athenian of the same school. The Répertoire's plan suited them both. It was plain and methodical, gave great attention to the supply of the army and little to its price, and assumed that the Third would advance by sapping and shelling, belt by belt, as it always had. Castorre did not expect to need more. On the seventieth cycle of Rotation Zero he signed the first order of the siege, and the Third began to come down the Cinder Stair. It had eleven weeks before the shells ran out in the Lethe holds.

To find the soldiers for the siege, the Third thinned its own line north of the Phlegethon bend to a screen of six regiments and handed its watch over the river to the holds on either side. Each of its Line Phalanxes was commanded by a Phalanx-General of long service. [[Oren Callix|Phalanx-General Oren Callix]] of the Second, the senior of them, was a mortal-line officer who had risen from the ranks of a Trench Regiment and was known in the Third for the care he took over his soldiers' nulls, which he counted personally at the end of every rotation, and for his doubts about any plan that set no limit on transfers. The Fifth was regarded as the Third's best phalanx in the attack and the Seventh as its best in defence, and the Ninth, in the south, held the hardest ground on the Flats along the rim of the Demus Gorge. The Assault Phalanxes and the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx answered to Castorre directly. The Third had not fought as a whole army in more than two centuries, and its staff had never moved more than four phalanxes at once. The descent of the whole Third down a single road from the ceiling, under the guns of an enemy who knew the road, was the largest movement it had attempted in its history, and the Répertoire gave it eleven months.

## 2 The Killing Fields

> *"Dig, then shoot. Shoot, then dig. The ground keeps whatever you give it."*
>
> Centurion Ilsa Marrow, Sixth Cohort, Forty-Fourth Trench Regiment

### 2.1 The Descent to the Flats (Rotation Zero, Cycle 70 to Rotation One, Cycle 38)

On the seventy-fourth cycle of Rotation Zero, one hundred and eighty-six thousand soldiers of the [[Twenty-Second Trench Regiment]] began the descent of the Cinder Stair, the switchback road of cut basalt that ran nine hundred metres down the face of the shelf wall from the ceiling relay at Landing Shelf Kappa to the Flats. They were the first soldiers of the Third Siege Army to set foot on the ground the siege would be fought over, and they were only the beginning. Lord-General Castorre remained on Quarterdeck Nineteen, in the ceiling above the Flats, from where he would direct the campaign for its first eight rotations. His presence on the Flats was not thought necessary. The Répertoire's plan required a commander who could manage the flow of soldiers and shells down the Stair, and that work was best done from the ceiling. Castorre assumed, as did his staff, that the outcome of the siege was not in question and that only its timetable remained to be settled.

The Archflamen's gunners did not fire on the Stair during the descent, though the Twenty-Second spent three cycles strung out along a road one cohort wide on the face of a cliff. The Third's officers assumed that the enemy was waiting for the army to reach the Flats before revealing his guns. The truth was that the Archflamen had forbidden his gunners to fire on the Third until the Third fired first. He intended that the Empire should strike the first blow against the Imperator's priest in front of six million witnesses, and Dace's preachers read the order aloud at every shift change in the Arsenal for six weeks so that none of them would be in any doubt about it.

The Third's transports came down through the ceiling in seventeen convoys of relay barges, ore-haulers and troop lighters, escorted by a squadron of the Tartarus Fleet under Navarch Iole Saturnin from the heavy cruiser Pallas Ascendant. The ceiling-lances denied the geometry over the Flats to any ship, so the convoys did not land. They held station eleven kilometres north of the lance envelope and lowered their cargo down the relay cables to the shelf at Kappa, from where it was carried down the Stair. For five months the cables ran without pause, carrying rations, water, nectar for the forward vats, timber and steel for revetments and dugouts, and above all shells. The Third's quartermasters had been ordered to stock the Flats for a war of four rotations. They stocked it for six, and the Répertoire allowed the excess, since the shells had been drawn from the Arsenal's own lists before the Mast went dark.

The scale of the undertaking was without precedent in the theatre. By the Répertoire's ledgers, the cables carried down some eleven million tonnes of stores in the first five months, of which shells and propellant made up more than half. Each Trench Regiment required about nine hundred tonnes of rations and water a cycle in the line, each ordnance regiment several times that in ammunition when firing, and the forward aid stations, where the wounded were stabilised and the dying were given the last rites before their implants fired, required nectar, dressings and Flamens in proportion. The Third laid down its stocks in great depots at the foot of the Stair and along the railheads, dug into the ash and roofed against the Arsenal's guns, and by the end of the descent the Flats behind the Third's line held more ordnance than any place in Tartarus except the Arsenal itself. The rationing order of Rotation Minus One did not apply to the Third. Its guns were the theatre's means of ending the rationing, and the Répertoire allowed them to fire as the plan required.

The cables could not carry the heavy guns. A Phaethon heavy mortar weighed eleven hundred tonnes in its cradle, and the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx would have to bring its guns down the shelf wall on rail. To lay that line, and the lines that would carry men and shells across the Flats to the gun positions, the Command sent down a labour corps of four hundred and twenty thousand bondsmen from the debt-rolls of the Lethe Hold. They were not soldiers and carried no implants, and the Third's soldiers called them the [[Quiet Ones]]. Over the following ten rotations they laid four thousand kilometres of track across the Cinder Flats and buried sixty thousand of their own number beside it. Their work is commemorated by a memorial on the ceiling at Landing Shelf Kappa.

It took eleven months of front time for the whole of the Third to be put in place. When the work was done, the Third's forward line ran in an arc of one hundred and sixty kilometres, about forty kilometres out from the outer belt, from the edge of the Mora Cut in the north to the rim of the Demus Gorge in the south-west. Behind it, in ranks of emplacements eighty kilometres deep, stood the guns.

### 2.2 The Campaign Plan

The Third's plan for the reduction of the Arsenal was drawn up by the Répertoire and signed by Lord-General Castorre. Rather than concentrating for a single thrust and allowing the Archflamen to move his reserves along the shorter and better-roaded inner side of his belts to meet it, the Third would press along the whole of its front at once and keep pressing, so that the Archflamen's army would be bled at every point of the arc until it gave way somewhere. The terrain dictated the shape of the attack. The Mora Cut and the Demus Gorge were impassable, so the Third would have to come from the north and the west, where the Archflamen's commanders had concentrated their belts in anticipation.

In the north, the Second and Fifth Line Phalanxes would advance on the outer belt where it ran along the edge of the Mora Cut. The Fifth would then swing east along the Cut and around the end of the outer belt to strike the northern spur, an arm of the second belt that the Arsenal had begun to dig along the Cut a century before and never completed. If the Fifth moved quickly enough, it might enter the second belt from the north without having to fight for the outer belt at all. In the south and west, the Seventh and Ninth Line Phalanxes held the line from the Lethe Cut to the rim of the Demus Gorge, and their orders were to break the outer belt on the western Flats. With the outer belt broken in the west and turned in the north, the arc would contract. As it did so, the Third's front would shorten, its regiments would become less vulnerable to counter-attack, and its guns could be massed more densely against each kilometre of the enemy's line. The outer belt therefore had to be overwhelmed quickly, whatever it cost.

Once before the second belt, the Line Phalanxes could proceed more carefully, sapping forward and probing for weaknesses before committing to any major assault. Once the second belt was broken, the ring would close upon the inner belt, and the Acropolis would come within range of the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx's Phaethons for the first time. The Third could then afford to wait. The Archflamen's army would be confined within a ring of trenches the Third had dug itself six centuries before, with no hope of relief and nowhere to shelter from the guns, and the Third would reduce the cinder cone to gravel around it.

The Répertoire estimated the cost of the outer belt at nine hundred thousand transfers. Castorre approved the estimate, and on the same cycle approved a request from the Pontifical College to hold a liturgy of thanksgiving for the Third's victory in the chapel of the Pallas Ascendant, on a date in Rotation Four.

The plan's chief assumption was that the Third's guns would prevail. The Répertoire allotted the siege a daily expenditure of four hundred thousand shells in the preparation of an assault and ninety thousand in the ordinary business of the line, and expected that the Third's nine hundred and forty guns, with the Arsenal's own keyed ammunition behind them, would silence the Archflamen's three hundred divine mortars within the first rotation. Its second assumption was that the vats would carry the cost. A soldier transferred on the Flats would be back in his cohort in a fortnight, so that the Third's strength in the line would fall only by the soldiers in transit, and the Répertoire's estimate of nine hundred thousand transfers for the outer belt amounted, in its tables, to a standing reduction of about thirty thousand soldiers. On paper the Third could not be worn down. The plan made no provision for nulls beyond the three percent ceiling set in the order of assignment, and it did not consider what the Archflamen might do with the river, because neither the Répertoire nor the Command believed that a forge-hold of the Hephaestean orders had any dealings with it.

### 2.3 The Beginning (Rotation One, Cycle 41)

The Répertoire dates the beginning of the siege to the forty-first cycle of Rotation One, at the change of the second watch, when nine hundred and forty guns of the Third opened fire together on the outer belt. The trench mortars of the forward regiments fired first, on the wire. The divine batteries followed within the minute. The batteries of the children of Apollo burst their solar shells a hundred metres over the Archflamen's forward trenches and lit the Flats gold for the first time in six hundred years, while the batteries of [[Ares]] fired shells that burst in rings of iron, and the aedificant batteries fired builder-shells keyed by the children of Athena and Hephaestus that folded the concrete of the bunkers in upon itself. Behind them, from eighty kilometres back, the Phaethons of the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx fired upon the resistance nests of the outer belt, the hardened points where the Archflamen's thralls had poured six metres of concrete over a gun pit. The first Phaethon shell of the war landed on a nest on the western Flats designated Gamma 14 and removed eleven metres of basalt from beneath it. The bombardment continued through the second and third watches, until by the fourth the barrels of the forward divine batteries glowed red and their crews were pouring condenser water over the breeches to keep the keyed shells from cooking off as they were loaded.

At the start of the fifth watch, the Arsenal's gunners replied. The Archflamen's army held about three hundred divine mortars in the outer and second belts, drawn from the Arsenal's own vaults, and its gunners had spent nine months ranging them on marker stakes across every metre of the Flats from the outer belt to the foot of the Stair. They did not know where the Third's guns were and fired upon their flashes. The first shells fell short, and then wide, and then came steadily closer. At the fifth bell a shell keyed by a child of Hephaestus landed in the ready pit of Battery Twenty-Six of the Third Ordnance Regiment, setting off forty keyed rounds stacked there and destroying Gun Four with its crew of eight and the battery's keyer. The solar fire burned in the pit for six hours. The nine woke in good order at Watchpoint Hades eight days later and were back with their battery within the month, but the gun was not replaced until Rotation Two. It had been a lucky hit at extreme range. The Répertoire nonetheless revised its estimate of the artillery duel. The Archflamen's gunners had located a battery thirty-one kilometres behind the Third's line within four hours of their first exchange, and the counter-battery war that the Third's planners had expected to win in weeks was now considered an open question.

While the artillery duel began, the [[Twenty-Ninth Trench Regiment]] advanced. It went forward with the barrage on a front of nine kilometres in the north-west quarter of the Flats, one hundred and seventy thousand soldiers in open order with rifles slung and picks in hand. They were not to attack. The guns would need weeks to soften the outer belt, and the Twenty-Ninth's task was to advance under the cover of the bombardment, which kept the enemy's infantry in their shelters and his mortars occupied with the Third's batteries, and then halt and dig. The regiment covered fourteen kilometres in six hours and halted with the outer belt twenty-six kilometres ahead. By the end of the seventh watch, the first rifle pits of the siege had been dug along nine kilometres of the Flats. The Archflamen's gunners found the range of the Twenty-Ninth before the night was out and killed three hundred and eighty of its soldiers in their pits. The regiment's centurions logged the transfers and kept digging.

### 2.4 Digging In

Over the following cycles, each rifle pit was joined to the next by a narrow trench, so that each section had a single trench of its own. Section trenches were joined into maniple trenches, maniple trenches into cohort trenches, and cohort trenches into those of the neighbouring cohorts. Within five cycles the Twenty-Ninth held a continuous line nine kilometres long and a metre and a half deep, and its reserve had begun on the second. The Third had been digging on the Cinder Flats for six hundred years and worked from a manual that had not changed in four of them. Fire bays were cut every eight metres, with traverses between them so that one shell could not kill the soldiers of two bays. Dugouts were cut six metres down and roofed with steel and basalt, and communication trenches ran back to the support line in zigzags. At night, wire teams went out in front of the trenches to lay three belts of razor-wire against patrols and raiders, and the heavy weapons teams were brought forward and dug in. The work was methodical and efficient, and it was the work the Third had trained for on the Flats in peacetime.

The Flats resisted it. The cinder slumped when it was wet and ran when it was dry, and the seep-storms that came down every few cycles filled the trenches to the knee. Beneath the ash ran heat veins from the Phlegethon, and a trench cut across one would boil. On the eleventh cycle, sappers of the Twenty-Ninth's Fourth Cohort cut into a vein six metres down a dugout shaft, and a maniple was lost when it came up the shaft at a thousand degrees. Thereafter the Third's engineers walked every line with heat-rods before it was dug, and the Third's trench maps of the campaign mark the veins in red.

Despite the Third's methodical approach, the Archflamen's army did not come out to contest it. Pinned under the bombardment that fell upon it every watch, it remained in the outer belt, offering little resistance beyond harassing mortar fire on the Third's working parties and the attentions of marksmen who shot the careless at four kilometres from nests in the slag heaps. While the Third dug, the Archflamen's thralls manned the positions opposite, and the two armies faced each other across twenty kilometres of ash that neither entered.

The outer belt was a formidable obstacle. Where complete, it was nine kilometres deep. The Archflamen's thralls had dug it to the Arsenal's own manual, which was older and better than the Third's, and supplied it from the Arsenal's stores, which were for practical purposes inexhaustible. Its front was a belt of slag-cast tank traps a hundred metres deep, behind which lay an anti-tank ditch eight metres wide and five deep, and behind that belts of razor-wire too dense for shellfire to clear. Under all of it lay the mines. The Répertoire estimated, from the Arsenal's inventory, that between three and four million had been laid in the outer belt alone, by hand and to a plan drawn up by the Arsenal's ordnance clerks, who had been making mines for eight thousand years. Behind the wire lay the trenches, and beneath them shelters cut sixteen and twenty metres into the basalt, deep enough to protect their occupants from all but the heaviest shells. In places the trench lines were gathered into resistance nests of poured concrete under six metres of fill, so arranged that each nest could fire into the ground in front of its neighbours, and a soldier crossing the outer belt would be under the fire of at least two of them at any point. The belts also grew stronger as they approached the spur. The outer belt was the youngest and least complete. The second was older, deeper and better built, and the inner belt was the Third's own work, whose plans the Third's engineers had read in their own archive and found deeply discouraging. Each belt would have to be broken in turn, and each would cost more than the last.

### 2.5 The First Assault on the Outer Belt (Rotation One, Cycle 79)

The Répertoire selected [[The Drum|Leaf 22-14]] on the western Flats for the first assault, where the outer belt crossed a low cinder rise known as the Drum. It had been built in haste and had the fewest nests per kilometre of any leaf on the belt, and the Répertoire assessed that it might be broken there. The Thirty-Seventh Trench Regiment of the [[Seventh Line Phalanx]], under Colonel [[Petra Anselm]], would make the main attack. On its left and right, the Twenty-Second and Thirty-Third would make strong supporting attacks to pin the defenders before them and draw the Archflamen's reserves away from the Drum. Behind the Thirty-Seventh, the [[Fourth Assault Phalanx]] brought forward the [[Sixteenth Armoured Regiment]] and held it ready. If a breach was made, the Sixteenth would drive through it and make for the second belt before the Archflamen could react.

The assault was planned in three waves over two cycles, with four hundred and sixty thousand soldiers across the three regiments. The first wave was not expected to make much progress. Its task was to probe the whole front of the three regiments in strength and find the weak points. The second wave, led by the Thirty-Seventh's breachers in their heavy plate, would strike those weak points, gain a foothold in the outer belt and hold it until the second cycle, when the third wave would pass through them and widen the breach. Once the breach was wide enough, the Sixteenth Armoured would be released from reserve.

All of this would be preceded by the heaviest bombardment the Third had ever fired. Every gun that could be moved was brought to the western Flats. The forward regiments' trench mortars would provide plunging fire into the enemy trenches. Behind them, the heavy mortars and quad-launchers of the second line would engage the enemy's identified heavy weapons, and further back the [[Kyklops Siege Carriage|Kyklops]] siege carriages, twelve-wheeled crawlers each carrying a single short howitzer, would fire directly upon the bunkers and nests. Behind the Kyklops, the divine batteries would lay a rolling barrage ahead of the infantry to keep the thralls in their shelters and then lift onto the Archflamen's guns. Finally, the Phaethons would add their weight to the rolling barrage before lifting onto the nests deeper in the belt. Three million, six hundred thousand shells had been stockpiled for the attack, to fall upon three leaves of the Flats.

The Second Ordnance Phalanx opened fire on the seventy-fourth cycle in support of the regiments' own guns, and fired for five cycles without pause. The ground between the lines was turned over so thoroughly that no trace remained of the roads that had once crossed it, and the outline of the Archflamen's forward trenches on the Drum disappeared from the spotters' periscopes. On the evening of the fourth cycle, Colonel Anselm reported to the Seventh Line Phalanx that she did not expect her first wave to meet much resistance.

On the seventy-ninth cycle, at the fourth bell, the barrage lifted to the rear and the first wave went over the parapet. The thralls had sat out five cycles of bombardment twenty metres down in the basalt, and when the barrage lifted they came up their shafts and manned their guns. The Thirty-Seventh's cohorts had advanced barely twenty metres when the nests on the Drum opened fire, from positions the spotters had marked as destroyed. Machine-gun fire from the nests crossed in front of the advancing lines at waist height, and the Archflamen's gunners, who had staked every crater on Leaf 22-14 in Rotation Zero, brought down their shells in patterns that walked from the Third's wire to the enemy's and back again. The soldiers of the Third had been trained to advance in line, and they did so, at an even pace with rifles at the port, and the fire took the lines apart as they came. As it grew heavier, sections went to ground in the craters, and the Archflamen's mortars followed them there. Officers who rose to lead their maniples forward were killed in turn. Of the Fourteenth Cohort's six hundred and forty soldiers, who went over in nine maniples, five hundred and seventy-one had been transferred or wounded by the end of the first watch, including every officer. At roll that night its survivors in the forward trench numbered sixty-nine, fewer than a single maniple at full strength. No soldier of the Thirty-Seventh reached the enemy wire on Leaf 22-14 that cycle.

Elsewhere on the Drum, a few sections reached the outer belt, crawling through the wire where the guns had thinned it and fighting the thralls hand to hand in the fire bays of the forward trench. The thralls fought hard. They were forge-workers who had first held a rifle the year before, and they carried no implants, so that a thrall killed on the outer belt was gone for good. They held their bays to the last, and when the bays fell, the Archflamen's mortars were turned upon them, killing the Third's soldiers who had taken them along with any thralls who remained.

The second wave followed on schedule at the sixth bell. The breachers had watched the destruction of the first wave through their periscopes, and they went forward across the same ground and were destroyed upon it in the same manner. Their plate stopped rifle fire but offered no protection against shells or the heavy guns of the nests. The attack continued until the end of the seventh watch, when it was halted. Throughout the night the wounded made their way back to the Third's trenches, while the Archflamen's gunners fired flares over Leaf 22-14 at intervals and engaged whatever they saw moving. Some of the Thirty-Seventh's sections remained out in the craters a hundred metres short of the outer belt, and fighting broke out again wherever they attempted to advance.

The cost had been terrible and the gains were nothing. On the second cycle, at the fourth bell, the third wave went in and paid the same price for no ground. The breach the Répertoire had planned for never came. The outer belt on the Drum had been completed in the eleven months the Third had spent putting its army in place, and the thralls had built more nests into it than the Arsenal's plans recorded. The Sixteenth Armoured Regiment never left its reserve.

On the eighty-fifth cycle, the Arsenal's Garrison Cohorts counter-attacked. Prefect Ostorian had watched the three waves fail on his wire and judged that the Thirty-Seventh had been bled white, and he sent nine thousand of his garrison soldiers across the ash behind a barrage of their own and a screen of Hoplon carriers to take its forward trench. He was correct about the Thirty-Seventh's infantry. He had not considered its heavy weapons, which had never been committed to the assault and remained dug in where they had been placed in the nights after the regiment came forward, with the same crews and their ammunition beside them. The garrison advanced into crossing fire of the kind that had destroyed the Thirty-Seventh, and within a minute the heavy mortars and quad-launchers of the second line had lifted their fire onto it. By the end of the watch the counter-attack was spent, and the garrison's dead lay upon the Thirty-Seventh's between the lines. The garrison's dead did not return. Both armies were back where they had started. The artillery duel continued, but the first assault on the outer belt was over. It had cost the Third one hundred and eighty-four thousand transfers in seven cycles.

On the spur, the first assault was received as a deliverance. The Archflamen's preachers told the thralls that the Imperator had turned back the Empire at the Drum, and the Arsenal's casting lines, which had been working at half their rate since the revolt, were restored to full production within a month. Ostorian drew more practical lessons. He concluded that the Third would always attack behind a barrage, that its soldiers would always advance in line, and that its breachers would follow its first wave onto the same ground, and he reorganised the outer belt's defence on that basis, thinning his forward trenches and putting more of his soldiers into the deep shelters, with orders to come up only when the Third's guns lifted. He also learned the price of his own counter-attack. Nine thousand of the garrison had gone across the ash, and fewer than four thousand returned, and the garrison dead did not come back from any vat. For the rest of the siege Ostorian counter-attacked only where his guns could cover the ground and where he could expect to hold what he took, and the Third's officers, who had expected the garrison to waste itself in such attacks, found that it seldom did.

Two hundred and six of those transfers were null. The soldiers' implants had fired, and their patterns had entered the noosphere, but the noosphere had not delivered them to Watchpoint Hades. The Third's technical officers attributed the losses to the Archflamen's [[Electronic Attack (EA)|jammers]], which had been broadcasting on the noosphere bands since the first barrage, and to the Phlegethon, which had always claimed a small number of uploads each rotation at Depth 40. Their explanation was only partly correct. Haruspex Dace had been listening on those bands since the Tabularium fell.

The Répertoire's report on the first assault was issued on the ninetieth cycle. It found that the bombardment had been adequate in weight and inadequate in effect, that the depth of the Archflamen's shelters had been underestimated by about half, and that the first wave had been sent forward at the moment the barrage lifted, giving the defenders the time they needed to man their guns. It recommended that future barrages lift in stages, with feints, so that the defenders could not tell when the infantry were coming, and that the breachers be held back until a lodgement had been made. It assigned no fault to the commanders. Colonel Anselm retained the Thirty-Seventh, which was rebuilt from the vats within two months, and Lord-General Castorre informed the Command that the Third had tested the outer belt and found it stronger than expected, and that the plan would proceed with adjustments. It was a fair description of what had happened, and it left out what mattered. The Third had sent four hundred and sixty thousand soldiers against three leaves of an unfinished trench belt and had not reached its wire, and the Archflamen's gunners had shown that they could find and destroy the Third's batteries at extreme range. Neither fact appeared in the covering note to the Command.

### 2.6 Life in the Trenches

Along the whole of the arc, both armies dug in deeper. Over the following months a maze of trenches spread across the Cinder Flats in a band that grew from three kilometres in depth to eleven. For months at a time the line did not move, and it appeared that neither side intended to move for a long time. Every regiment nonetheless took its turn at patrolling and raiding. At night patrols went into the ground between the lines to lift the enemy's mines and lay their own, or to find gaps in the wire for raiding parties to exploit, and raids to take prisoners went out from every regiment on the line twice a week. The Archflamen's raiders came the other way, most often from the Sworn of Orrhae, and took the heads of the sentries they killed. Without any regiment making an attack, the Third lost an average of two thousand three hundred transfers a cycle along the arc. Such losses had been planned for. The vats at Watchpoint Hades ran without pause, and the cables brought down replacements every cycle, a third of them soldiers returning from their own transfers, so that the line remained at strength.

The war of the trenches was also a war of signals. Every soldier's upload passed through the chain of relay pylons that the Third's signallers had planted across the Flats behind the line, tall steel masts of the old noosphere pattern, each serving about four kilometres of front, from which the uploads were handed up the shelf wall to the ceiling and on to Watchpoint Hades. The pylons were the Third's most important targets after its guns, and the Archflamen's gunners learned to find them early. A pylon struck in the night left its stretch of the line uploading through its neighbours, at longer range and through more of the Phlegethon's interference, and the Third's signallers spent the siege replacing them, as many as forty in a single cycle. The Arsenal's jammers worked on the same bands from the second belt, and the Third's technical officers attributed much of the siege's early null rate to the combination. Their figures showed that nulls clustered on stretches of the line where pylons had been lost, and they asked for more pylons. The Command approved the request and the Quiet Ones laid the cable for them, and the null rate did not fall.

Conditions on the line were wretched. The seep-storms turned the trenches to streams every four or five cycles, and between them the ash fouled weapons, food and eyes alike. Rations came forward cold, and unboiled water from the Flats sent a soldier to the vats within a week. In the second month the Phlegethon rats arrived, animals the size of dogs that had come up the river with the heat and fed on the dead in no-man's land and the rations in the dugouts. The Archflamen's divine mortars fell at random, at any watch and on any cohort, and the Third's soldiers learned to tell the different shells apart by their sound, and to judge which left them time to reach a dugout.

The vats restored a soldier's body. They left him what he remembered of his death, and the repeated transfers of the trench war began to take a toll the Répertoire had not priced. Most soldiers returned from a first transfer fit for duty. By the fourth or fifth, many returned slowed, or with tremors, or unable to sleep without a light, and were posted to the support line for a month to recover. Soldiers who had been transferred forty times or more were known on the line as [[Old Ladder|Old Ladders]], and their cohorts kept them out of the forward bays and put them on the wire teams, where their steadiness was valued. The proportion of such soldiers in the Third rose with every rotation of the siege, and the Third's medical officers raised it with the Command repeatedly. The Command took note of it.

The first rotation of the siege passed. The outer belt had held against the first assault, and both armies were content to wear down the other in a stalemate of slow destruction. The Archflamen's dead remained on the Flats. The Third's did not.

### 2.7 The Battle of Redoubt K-19 (Rotation Two, Cycle 302)

Six months after the first assault, with the line unchanged along the whole of the arc, the [[Second Line Phalanx]] was ordered to make a probing attack along its entire front. Its commander, Phalanx-General Oren Callix, expected little of it, and neither did the Répertoire, whose order had been written chiefly to keep the Archflamen's troops in their shelters while the Third's engineers began a new sap in the north. The regiments of the Second each selected a few points on their fronts and sent their breachers against them under heavy bombardment and [[Smoke (SMK)|smoke]].

The [[Forty-Fourth Trench Regiment]] chose [[Redoubt K-19]], a resistance nest four kilometres north of the Drum, where the outer belt descended into a slag gully and climbed out again. Built of the Arsenal's best concrete, it had been struck by the Phaethons nineteen times without being cracked, and it anchored eleven kilometres of the outer belt. The task fell to the Sixth Cohort, under [[Ilsa Marrow|Centurion Ilsa Marrow]], a mortal-line officer of twenty-nine drafted from the ceiling world [[Tamaris]], who had been transferred twice and had a reputation in the Second for her trench work. Covered by smoke from the preliminary bombardment, the Sixth Cohort crossed the ground below K-19 at a run and was in the wire before the redoubt's defenders saw it. The Archflamen's gunners, firing blind into the smoke, shelled the gully, the cohort and the redoubt's own forward trenches without distinction. Marrow lost a maniple in the wire but carried the rest of the cohort into the forward trench beneath K-19, where they found and seized the entrance to a personnel shelter.

The Archflamen's jammers had been active on the Second's relay bands since the bombardment began, and the cohort could raise no one, so Marrow sent a runner back with a written report that the Sixth held the forward trench under K-19 and the head of a shelter, and needed breachers and engineers. The runner took four hours to cross eleven hundred metres of ash and was wounded twice. The report reached the Forty-Fourth's command post at the eighth hour of the watch, and Phalanx-General Callix at the start of the next. By then the Archflamen's gunners had found the trench. They had orders to shell any part of the outer belt taken by the Third, with their own soldiers in it if need be, and they shelled the Sixth Cohort's trench for nine hours. Marrow took the cohort down into the shelter, and beneath the redoubt it fought the thralls through a warren of galleries cut into the basalt, with grenades, bayonets and entrenching tools in passages too narrow for two soldiers abreast, until it held the first gallery and its junction with the second.

On the second cycle, Callix committed his breachers, the Forty-Fourth's heavy maniples together with every breacher the neighbouring regiments could spare, and they worked their way up the gully toward K-19 trench by trench against the redoubt's guns. On the third cycle the Fourth Assault Phalanx came forward with three companies of the Sixteenth Armoured Regiment, forty-two Thureos tanks in all, and the Colossus super-heavy [[Lady Hestia]], which had come down the Stair on rail in Rotation Zero and waited nine months in a revetment for a target worthy of her. With them came eleven Mattock engineering crawlers, broad-tracked machines with dozer blades the width of a road, to fill the anti-tank ditch before K-19 under fire. Four Mattocks were knocked out in the ditch. The other seven filled it in eight hours, pushing the slag of the redoubt's own glacis down into it, and the tanks crossed behind them. At four hundred metres the Lady Hestia put a shell through the redoubt's forward embrasure and out through its rear wall.

Below ground, meanwhile, the defenders attempted to burn the Sixth Cohort out of the galleries with flame lances brought up from the heat-wells. They were tools, made to clear slag from the casting lines with jets of river fire, and in the confines of the galleries they killed forty of the cohort's soldiers within an hour. The breachers forced the redoubt's main access shaft from the surface at the end of the third cycle, fought their way down through its gun decks and cleared the galleries passage by passage, narrowly avoiding an exchange of fire with the Sixth Cohort, which took the sound of their plate for the garrison's, before the regiment's challenge was given and answered. Eleven soldiers of the Sixth remained standing in the gallery they had held.
