When he turned back she was gone, through an open culvert grating where she had fallen.

The ambush cost the Cohort dearly, but the Forsworn could not hold the slipways alone and did not try. They followed Hesper into the ground, and the Cohort hunted down the remnants of the dock garrison. Two hundred and twelve of the Cohort's sworn were transferred in the battle for the docks. Eighty-eight had been killed by Styx-iron and did not reach Watchpoint Hades. The Cohort had failed to take a single one of the Forsworn alive, though the Inexorable's holds carried three thousand captured thralls of the dock garrison for Dask's questioners at Rhamnous. After ten cycles the docks were in the Cohort's hands, and its engineers spent two more demolishing them beyond repair, dropping the quays into the river and bringing down the barge hangars. The Cohort of Nemesis then returned through the ceiling and left Tartarus. Ardent sent the Third a single signal under the Cohort's seal, stating that the Phlegethon Docks had been destroyed and the Arsenal cut off from the river, and that the Cohort would be back for the Legate. Castorre asked Corvis which Legate.

The battle for the Phlegethon Docks was a costly success for the Cohort and a considerable one for the Empire. In ten cycles, with a fraction of the Third's strength and none of its trenches, the Cohort of Nemesis had closed the Arsenal's only door to the river, destroyed most of the dock garrison and drawn the Archflamen's commanders into a defence of their rear that they would maintain until the end of the siege. It had also failed in the purpose its Prefect cared for most. Hesper was alive, the Forsworn had withdrawn into the ground beneath the spur, and the Cohort had learned only what the Ninth Styx Legion's records had already told the Command. The Third's commanders were left to deal with the consequences of a battle in which they had played no part. They had not been consulted about the docks and had not been told of the Cohort's departure, and they had no plan to exploit the docks' fall because nobody had told them it was coming. The opportunity the Cohort had created on the second belt was taken up only at the end of the rotation, when the Third's own spotters discovered it.

### 4.9 The Null List (Rotation Seven, Cycle 50)

The four hundred and nine nulls of Leaf 52-33 forced the Third's medical officers to look again at a problem they had been recording since the first barrage. A null was an upload that reached the vats empty or did not reach them at all. There had always been a few, from soldiers taken by the rivers, from failed implants, and from the handful of uploads the Phlegethon claimed each rotation at Depth 40. The deaths caused by Styx-iron could be explained from the Ninth Styx Legion's records. The black blades cut what the implant was trying to save, and the implant uploaded an empty pattern. The rest of the list could not be explained so easily. By the fiftieth cycle of Rotation Seven it ran to eleven metres. When the Styx-iron deaths, the river and the failed implants had been removed from it, there remained more soldiers than the Third had ever lost in a rotation, killed by ordinary shells in ordinary trenches, whose implants had fired and whose uploads had been logged leaving the relays and had arrived at Watchpoint Hades empty. Most had died near relay pylons. The study was led by Medicus-Centurion Hanno Ricci, who had been taken off the Flats in Rotation Five after nineteen transfers in two rotations and given charge of the ward at Watchpoint Hades for soldiers who came back wrong. He concluded that something was taking the uploads in the air, during the seven seconds they spent in the open between the pylons and the ceiling. The technical officers suspected the Echo Mast, which had been broadcasting down the river since Rotation Six and which they now believed was also listening.

The conclusion reached the line before it reached the Command's agenda. Centurion Marrow, sent up for a month's rest after Leaf 52-33, returned to the Forty-Fourth on the eightieth cycle and asked to be posted to a cohort holding a stretch of trench as far as possible from the nearest relay pylon. By the end of the rotation a third of the Forty-Fourth's centurions had made the same request.

Ricci's study was sent to the Command at Watchpoint Hades in the sixtieth cycle of Rotation Seven with a recommendation that the Third's relay pylons be moved back from the line, that soldiers in the forward bays be ordered to keep their distance from them and that the Echo Mast be treated as the Third's principal target. The Command referred the first two recommendations to the Répertoire, which judged that moving the pylons would lengthen the time uploads spent in the open and increase the losses it was meant to prevent. The third recommendation it had already made itself. None of them was acted upon in Rotation Seven. The study did have one effect, which was to make the null rate a subject the Third's soldiers discussed openly. A transfer had always been regarded on the Flats as a delay. From Rotation Seven onward the soldiers of the Third began to speak of death on the Flats as something that might be permanent, and their officers began to report that assault waves went forward more slowly than they used to.

### 4.10 The Thinning of the Second Belt (Rotation Seven, Cycles 51 to 400)

The Third did not understand what the Cohort of Nemesis had done for it until the end of Rotation Seven, when its spotters counted the Archflamen's cohorts on the second belt and found thirty thousand fewer than at the rotation's start. With the docks gone, the Archflamen had no way to bring anything up the river, and whatever came to help him would now have to climb the Phlegethon's cliffs onto the spur itself. He placed his best troops on the spur to receive it, and more around the Acropolis, in the vaults and in the lance silos against the Cohort's return, and he found them by stripping the second belt. The Third's centurions felt the difference before the Répertoire measured it. The counter-attacks on the second belt came later and lighter than before. The barrage still fell on any trench the Third took, but the cohorts behind it were thinner and slower, with more thralls and fewer garrison soldiers among them. Twice in the last month of Rotation Seven a regiment of the Third took a nest on the second belt and held it overnight, which no regiment had done since the Saturation. The Third's commanders had wanted a breakthrough for three rotations. On the first cycle of Rotation Eight, with the second belt thinner than it had ever been and the Eighth Line Phalanx fresh on the western Flats, the Répertoire informed Castorre that he could have one.

The stripping of the second belt was the subject of the first serious quarrel among the Archflamen's commanders. Prefect Ostorian had held the belts since the start of the siege and regarded the second belt as the Arsenal's true defence, since every rotation the Third spent before it was a rotation the inner belt did not have to fight. Hesper regarded the belts as a means of killing the Third's soldiers in places where the Mast could hear them, and held that the spur, the vaults and the lance silos mattered more than any trench on the Flats. The Archflamen sided with Hesper. Ostorian kept his command, but from Rotation Seven the defence of the Arsenal was directed in fact from the Katabasion, where Hesper and Dace attended the Archflamen daily and Ostorian attended when summoned. The garrison cohorts that had formed the backbone of the second belt went to the spur, and their places on the belt were taken by thrall cohorts trained by the Forsworn and stiffened by detachments of the Sworn. The arrangement suited the Forsworn's methods and the Mast's purposes. It did not suit the defence of the second belt, which was now held by the least capable part of the Archflamen's army against the largest concentration of the Third's strength since the siege began.

The Eighth Line Phalanx took its place in the line in the same period. Castorre placed it on the western Flats between the Seventh and the Ninth, on the leaves facing the stretch of the second belt the Répertoire judged weakest, and gave it the remainder of Rotation Seven to dig in, learn the ground and be filled out from the depot at Kappa. Its three regiments were of uneven quality. The Fiftieth and Fifty-Second were veteran regiments of the Tartarus front with long experience of reformation peaks. The Fifty-Fourth was the Lethe Hold drafts, under officers chosen for their availability, and Castorre kept it on the left of the Phalanx's line, against the edge of the Lethe Cut, where he judged it least likely to be tested. The Répertoire's plan for Rotation Eight assigned the Eighth the main effort.

## 5 The Big Push, and What Came Up the River

> *"Give it everything. It keeps what you give it."*
>
> Colonel Damaris Lowe, Fifty-Second Trench Regiment, Eighth Line Phalanx

### 5.1 The Big Push (Rotation Eight, Cycle 1)

The Third had been on the [[Cinder Flats]] for eight rotations of front time. By the [[Répertoire du Savoir Athénien|Répertoire]]'s plan it should have been dug in before the inner belt four rotations earlier, with the [[Acropolis (Pyriphlegethon)|Acropolis]] under its heaviest guns, and it was still in front of the second belt. It had been obliged to ask for more soldiers and for help against the [[Echo Mast]], and the Command had sent it a Line Phalanx and a pure-blood cohort it had not requested, which had gone home without speaking to it. The Cohort of Nemesis had nonetheless left the second belt thinner than it had ever been, and [[Aurelian Castorre|Lord-General Castorre]] saw in it the chance to redeem the failures of the previous rotations with a decisive breakthrough. By his chief of staff's reckoning he had four rotations of the Command's patience left, and perhaps fewer.

Castorre ordered the three regiments of the Eighth Line Phalanx, together with every regiment already in the line, to plan and execute a general offensive on the second belt along its whole frontage. Sustained pressure would draw in the [[Sabinus Orrhae|Archflamen]]'s reserves, which would then be destroyed by the guns or in the infantry's attacks, and the belt would eventually break somewhere. When it did, the Fourth Assault Phalanx would be ready to drive through the gap toward the inner belt. If it moved quickly enough, it might gain a foothold in the inner belt before the Archflamen could man it. The Répertoire named the offensive [[Operation Antilochus]], after a son of Nestor who had died saving his father. The Third's soldiers called it the Big Push before the orders had reached the regiments, and nobody on the Flats ever used the official name.

### 5.2 Plans and Forces for Operation Antilochus

The offensive was the largest the [[Tartarus Front Soldiers|Third]] had mounted since the start of the siege, and the Répertoire planned it on the scale of the Saturation with a fire plan the Saturation had lacked. Every Line Phalanx was to attack on its own front. The Second and Fifth in the north would fix the Archflamen's cohorts along the old northern spur and the [[Mora Cut]], the Seventh and Ninth would attack on the western Flats, and the Eighth, fresh and at full strength, would make the main effort in the centre on the leaves the Répertoire judged weakest. The Fourth Assault Phalanx, rebuilt after the loss of its reserve to the Wedge in [[Imperial Calendar|Rotation]] Four, was held behind the Eighth with three armoured regiments, two hundred and sixty tanks and a hundred and ten [[Hoplon Carrier|Hoplons]], ready to exploit wherever the belt broke. The [[Tenth Ordnance Phalanx]] and the [[Divine Material Ammunition|divine batteries]] of the Line Phalanxes were given eleven cycles of preparatory fire, the longest bombardment of the siege. The quartermasters had spent two rotations stockpiling for it and had nine million shells on the Flats, a third of them keyed, which was more than the Third had fired in the whole of its first three rotations on the Flats.

The Répertoire's estimate for the offensive was one million, eight hundred thousand [[Transfer (UOE)|transfers]] over thirty cycles. [[Watchpoint Hades]] was instructed to clear its vats for the Third, and the Command suspended the posting of returned soldiers to other theatres for the duration, so that every soldier of the Third who went up on the Flats would come back down the Stair to his own regiment. The vats were nonetheless the offensive's limiting factor. They could return about forty thousand soldiers a cycle at full capacity, nine days after their deaths. An offensive that transferred more than that each cycle would consume the Third's strength faster than the vats could restore it, and Castorre's staff calculated that the Third could sustain the losses of a full general offensive for no more than ten cycles before its forward cohorts began to run out of soldiers. If the second belt had not broken by then, it would not break.

The plan was sound in its arithmetic and conventional in everything else. It assumed that the second belt, thinned by the Cohort of Nemesis, would give way under enough weight, and that the Archflamen's army would respond to the Third's offensive as it had responded to every other, by counter-attacking locally and dying in the attempt. It made no allowance for the Forsworn, whose presence on the second belt the Third could not measure, and none for the Echo Mast, whose effect on the [[Null Transfer|null]] rate the Command had not acknowledged. Castorre approved it on the sixtieth cycle of Rotation Eight. He told the Command that he expected to be before the inner belt by the end of the rotation, and he moved the College's liturgy of thanksgiving to Rotation Ten.

The Archflamen's army could not fail to see the preparations. Its sentries on the second belt watched the Third's trenches fill, its stockpiles grow in the support lines and new gun pits being dug behind the old ones, and the Arsenal's gunners shelled the Third's lines every watch to disrupt the work. The Third's gunners replied against the Arsenal's guns, since every gun destroyed now would be one fewer firing when the infantry went over. On the eighty-ninth cycle of Rotation Eight the preparatory bombardment began. Every gun in the Third fired, and for eleven cycles the barrage went on through every watch, lighting the Flats from the Mora Cut to the [[Lethe Cut]] while the Archflamen's thralls sat it out in their shelters beneath the second belt. In the dark watch of the hundredth cycle the first wave moved forward into the fire bays, with the second packed in behind it in the support line, loaded with grenades, ammunition and three cycles' rations. At the first bell of the hundred and first cycle the barrage stopped.

### 5.3 Day One (Rotation Eight, Cycle 101)

The Third went over the parapet on a front of one hundred and nine kilometres, four hundred thousand soldiers in a single wave. The Archflamen's thralls were already coming up out of their shelters to their guns, and they opened fire low along the ground into the smoke before the attackers were in sight. Behind the machine guns, the Arsenal batteries the bombardment had missed brought down their fire among the waves.

On Leaf 50-27, before Ceiling-Lance Seven, the attack of the Twenty-Ninth Trench Regiment collapsed. Its soldiers had looked every morning for two rotations at the thirty-one breachers on the Arsenal's wire, and they now advanced into a barrage the Arsenal's gunners had been ranging on that leaf since the raid. Within the first hour the leading maniples had gone to ground and the following maniples had gone to ground among them, and then soldiers began to come back. Each cohort went into the attack with a provost-optio, a Command officer carrying the authority of the Répertoire, whose task was to prevent exactly this. On Leaf 50-27 three provost-optios shot soldiers who were falling back, and in two of the Twenty-Ninth's cohorts the retreating soldiers shot their provost-optios. At the parapet the returning first wave met the second wave going forward, and fighting broke out in the fire bays between the two while the Arsenal's barrage fell on both. Within two hours the attack had become a rout. The Command's verdict came three cycles later. The regiment's colonel and his staff were arrested and sent up the Stair under guard to Watchpoint Hades, where they were tried and sentenced under CODE 31. The Répertoire carried out the sentence in the vats, and seven officers were transferred by order and arrived null by order. The Twenty-Ninth Trench Regiment was disbanded, its colours burned at the depot at Kappa and its survivors sent to the [[Forlorn Cohorts]] on the Cocytus front, where the Empire sent its condemned. The soldiers who had shot the provost-optios were never identified.

The fate of the Twenty-Ninth was the first collapse of a regiment of the Third in the siege, and the Command treated it with a severity intended to ensure it would be the last. It is doubtful that it achieved its purpose. The Twenty-Ninth had been on the Flats since the first rotation, had dug the first rifle pits of the siege and had lost the raid on Lance Seven through no fault of its own, and its soldiers had spent two rotations watching the consequences of that raid on the Arsenal's wire. The sentence passed on its colonel was read aloud in every regiment on the Flats. It taught the Third's soldiers that the Command would not tolerate a regiment that broke. It also taught them that the Command could make a soldier null by order, which the soldiers of the Third had believed only the Archflamen could do, and the Third's provost reports from the remainder of Rotation Eight record a sharp increase in soldiers found in the support lines without orders.

Elsewhere the day had more to show. In places the Third reached the second belt and carried its forward trench with the bayonet, and some regimental commanders reported good progress, while others reported failure and losses their staffs had not finished counting. The second wave was ordered forward along the whole front regardless. By the end of the cycle it was clear that most of the Archflamen's cohorts had held, and that those driven out had simply withdrawn to positions further inside the second belt and manned them. The greatest success came in the south, on the front of the Thirty-Ninth Trench Regiment of the Ninth Line Phalanx on Leaves 51 and 52. There the Thirty-Ninth's gunners had won the counter-battery duel outright and destroyed nearly every Arsenal gun that could reach their front, so that when the infantry went over almost nothing fired on them from behind the wire. The Thirty-Ninth went through the forward trench of the second belt and was still advancing in the dark watch. The Third's staff on [[Quarterdeck Nineteen]] made its front the point of main effort for the rest of the offensive. The Fourth Assault Phalanx, held in reserve all day, was ordered to attack through the Thirty-Ninth with its tanks and Hoplons. It would take a cycle to bring into position, and the Thirty-Ninth would have to hold its gains through the second cycle.

The first cycle had cost more than anyone had planned. Many cohorts were already badly understrength, the vats at Watchpoint Hades were full, and the first transfers of the offensive would not return to the line for a week. The cohorts that had entered the second belt would have to hold there until relieved. The second cycle would be one of holding and of meeting the counter-attacks that were certain to come, and the third would bring the next great effort. The gains so far were small, and the Archflamen's army had fought hard and in places fanatically to keep its ground. The attack had to continue.

### 5.4 Day Two (Rotation Eight, Cycle 102)

The second cycle was a cycle of consolidation and scattered local attacks. Each cohort that had reached the second belt tried to secure its position or tried again for the objective it had missed on the first cycle, and beneath the artillery duel the infantry fought a hundred separate battles in the craters and in fragments of captured trench. By its end, several of the Third's colonels were asking for the offensive to be called off or reduced. At the rate they were losing soldiers their regiments would cease to exist within the week, and the vats could not refill them in time to matter. The Third's command would not consider it, and Castorre could not. The offensive was too important to be abandoned for its cost, however high, and speed mattered more than anything, since every cycle's delay gave the Archflamen more time to dig in again. There would be more attacks on the third cycle. The Third was bleeding badly, and it assumed the Archflamen was bleeding too.

### 5.5 Day Three (Rotation Eight, Cycle 103)

On the third cycle the fighting continued along the whole front as ordered, with the weight of the effort in the south on Leaves 51-41 and 52-41, where the Fourth Assault Phalanx went in through the Thirty-Ninth. Its tanks advanced slowly across the craters and deployed into line, with the Hoplons behind them carrying fifty soldiers each. The Archflamen's positions before them were by then a loose line of smashed bunkers, walls and shell-holes that could not long withstand the weight of an Assault Phalanx. The Hoplons dropped their ramps on them and put their breachers in, and by the middle of the cycle the Fourth's tanks and infantry were moving forward again. A breakthrough at the second belt appeared close.

The Archflamen's commanders had recognised the danger of the Thirty-Ninth's gains on the first cycle and used the second to reinforce the sector. On the third they counter-attacked with [[Thureos Tank|Thureos]] tanks and Hoplons of the Arsenal's own, new divine mortars on crawlers from the vaults, Kyklops siege carriages and a squadron of twelve Lance-Hunters, the long-barrelled tank [[Destroyer|destroyers]] the Arsenal built for the deep holds. As the Fourth's leading tanks advanced, resistance grew in front of them, and by the middle of the afternoon watch the Fourth had been halted and the Arsenal's armour was pushing it back. Its tank losses mounted through the watch until its commanders called for a defensive barrage to cover their withdrawal. The breakthrough had been stopped. There was better news only at the far eastern end of the attack, where the Twenty-Sixth Trench Regiment of the Fifth Line Phalanx took its first-cycle objectives on Leaf 62-38, two cycles late, and prepared to push again on the fourth with the support of the tank companies of the Fourteenth Armoured.

### 5.6 Day Four and After (Rotation Eight, Cycles 104 to 124)

The Third could not abandon the gains of the first three cycles, however small, and it appeared to be prepared to beat itself to death against the second belt to keep them. Its whole strength was committed, and its only reserves were cohorts assembled from the survivors and stragglers of the first cycle and soldiers returning from the vats a few thousand at a time. As cohort commanders fought for their local objectives, the offensive lost momentum. The shell stockpiles were falling, the [[Phaethon Heavy Mortar|Phaethon]] shells worst of all. Cohorts were reduced to the size of maniples, and maniples to the size of sections. The offensive had run for seven cycles without a breakthrough when the Fifty-Second Trench Regiment of the Eighth Line Phalanx made its attack on Leaf 57-40.

The Fifty-Second was a new regiment in its first battle, and its colonel was new to command. [[Damaris Lowe]] was thirty-four, a [[Legacy|legacy]] of [[Athena]] on her mother's side, from the ceiling world Ostia Minor, which had sent soldiers to the Third for four hundred years. She had come down the Stair three months before and spent those months in the forward bays of Leaf 57-40 studying the second belt. Amid the confusion of the offensive she proposed a night attack by the whole regiment at once on the trench line before her, in place of the piecemeal pushes that had won almost no ground for an enormous number of transfers. The Eighth Line Phalanx at first refused, judging the risk too great. A regiment caught in the open in front of the second belt at night would be stranded there. Lowe argued that if the guns were strong enough they could keep the Arsenal's guns quiet even if the attack failed, and a regiment brought back under the cover of its own guns would not cost much. The Phalanx gave way and allowed her to try.

On the dark watch of the hundred and sixth cycle, eleven cohorts of the Fifty-Second went out into no-man's land on their bellies, with Lowe at the head of the first. The Third had taught its regiments for six hundred years to advance at a walk in parade order. Lowe had spent two months of nights teaching hers to crawl, in silence and without halting for the flares, since the Arsenal's sentries watched for movement that stopped and a regiment that froze at every flare took twice as long to cross. As the regiment crawled, every gun it had, and every battery Lowe had been able to borrow from the Phalanx, opened fire on the second belt behind the trench line, then dropped onto the trench line itself at the planned minute, and then lifted and rolled forward. The Fifty-Second rose from the craters and charged. The thralls had sheltered through the barrage as they always did and came up their shafts to their guns when it lifted, to find the Fifty-Second already on their parapet. Few reached their guns. The regiment went over and along the trench line, and the thrall cohorts below came up their shafts one at a time into its bayonets. Lowe herself was killed crossing the first trench by a Laistrygon that came up out of the support trench in front of her. Her upload was logged arriving whole at Watchpoint Hades ninety seconds later, and she woke in the vats eight cycles afterwards. Her regiment knew none of this until later and went on without her. At the first bell the second wave passed through the first into the support trench and beyond it. The second belt had been broken.

### 5.7 The Folding of the Second Belt (Rotation Eight, Cycles 107 to 160)

News of the Fifty-Second's success reached Quarterdeck Nineteen within the watch, and reinforcements were moving to widen the breach within two. The first to arrive was a battery of quad-launchers, towed across no-man's land by tractors, which came into action beyond the second belt's forward trench and broke up the Arsenal's last attempts at a counter-attack. Tanks, Hoplons and heavier guns followed, and the Third's outrider squadrons came through on their armoured hounds and wheeled east and west along the inside of the second belt to take its cohorts from behind. As the outer belt had done six rotations earlier, the second belt now collapsed in the cycles that followed. The Archflamen's commanders left rearguards to die in the nests and withdrew the rest of their army, and one by one the Third's regiments advanced into the second belt, in places unopposed. Any of the Archflamen's cohorts that failed to escape before the ring closed were surrounded and destroyed.

The Third was too badly hurt to press on immediately into the inner belt. It needed time to rebuild and to plan its next move, and the cost of the breakthrough had been far too high. The Big Push had cost two million, nine hundred thousand transfers in sixty cycles, of which one hundred and eighty thousand were null. It had taken eight rotations of front time to reach the inner belt, rotations of slaughter as bad as anything the theatre had seen since the Second Grand Offensive, and by the Répertoire's count the Third had now transferred more than twenty-three million soldiers on the Cinder Flats, six hundred and eighty thousand of whom had not come back. Yet for the first time the end seemed within reach. The Archflamen was confined behind the inner belt and the [[Cinder Wall]], and the front had shortened to sixty-one kilometres, on which the Third could mass more guns and soldiers to the kilometre than ever before. On the hundred and sixty-second cycle of Rotation Eight, a Phaethon of the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx fired from a new pit on the old second belt, and forty seconds later its shell landed on the lower terraces of the Acropolis. For the first time in the war, the Third's guns could reach the Archflamen. Colonel Lowe returned to her regiment on the hundred and twentieth cycle.

Judged against its object, the Big Push succeeded. Judged against its plan, it was a failure the Third could not afford to repeat. It had cost more than half as much again as the Répertoire's estimate, run for twice the time and broken the second belt in the one place the plan had not chosen, through the initiative of a regimental colonel in her first battle whose proposal the Eighth Line Phalanx had at first refused. The main effort through the Thirty-Ninth had been stopped by an armoured counter-attack the Third's intelligence had failed to detect, and the Fourth Assault Phalanx had lost a third of its tanks without reaching the inner belt. The Command noted the cost and the success together and did not trouble to reconcile them. Castorre received the thanks of the [[Pontifical College]] and a request from the Command for an explanation of the transfers. He sent both to his chief of staff. In the Third, the reputation of the Fifty-Second and of its colonel was made, and the method of Leaf 57-40, the night crawl under a barrage timed to the minute, was taught to every regiment on the Flats before the end of the rotation.

The Archflamen's losses in the offensive could only be estimated. The Répertoire put them at three hundred and twenty thousand dead, almost all thralls and garrison soldiers, with a further eighty thousand left behind on the second belt when the ring closed. It was the heaviest defeat the Archflamen's army had suffered, and it was suffered at a time when the army could least replace it. The Archflamen had no implants and no vats. Every soldier the Third killed on the second belt was a soldier the inner belt would lack, and from the end of Rotation Eight the Archflamen's commanders could no longer man every trench they held. They would come to rely more and more on the allies the Mast had called up out of the river.

### 5.8 The Kindlers (Rotation Eight, Cycles 170 to 200)

Facing defeat, the Archflamen showed no sign of concern. He told his commanders that the end he had preached was at hand and that the slaughter must continue. He no longer spoke of victory or defeat, only of the dark, of going down into it and of the Imperator coming back up through it, and every cycle he led the [[Katabasion]]'s procession out onto the terraces of the Acropolis under the Phaethon fire with the bones of the First Descent carried before him. By now Hesper had become the closest adviser of the Archflamen and of [[Ilan Dace|Dace]] alike. She told the Archflamen after the fall of the second belt that the war was not lost. The Empire's army was closing its ring, but there were powers in the river greater than any army with guns, and they would stand with him if he held firm and knew how to call them. She knew how. Asked what it would cost, she said that the Kindlers would name the price, since it was theirs to name.

Hesper brought the Kindlers up from the ruins of the docks shortly afterwards, by the cliff stair the Arsenal's smugglers had used. There were nine of them, [[Cinderborn]] priests of the Red Furnace, the [[Phlegethon]]'s own congregation, which had lived at the bend for as long as the Arsenal had stood on the spur, their skin burned and healed so many times that it had the texture of slag. They could call a reformation. Every soldier of the Third knew what a reformation was. The [[Monster|monsters]] of [[Tartarus (Realm)|Tartarus]] did not stay dead, and whatever the Third killed went back into the deep to be made again and sent back up within a rotation or a century. A reformation peak occurred when the deep did this faster than usual and sent many at once, and the Third existed to meet such peaks. Nobody in the Third had known that they could be summoned. The Kindlers went into the heat-wells beneath the Acropolis with Dace, and over nine cycles, by the evidence later recovered from the Arsenal's records and from six [[Vestal Virgins|Vestals]] of the House of the Hearth found alive at the end of the siege, they fed into the Number Four heat-well four thousand thralls, the forty [[Laistrygonian Half-Bloods|Laistrygons]] of the Arsenal's last breeding pen and every one of the [[Hecatean Strays|Hecatean strays]] except the girl Mirren, whom Dace kept.

On the hundred and ninetieth cycle the Third's sentries on the old second belt reported that the Phlegethon was rising. The river below the shelf, which had burned red at the edge of the Flats for six hundred years, turned orange and then yellow, and its heat came across the Flats in waves. Red and black cloud, the first ever seen on the Flats, gathered beneath the ceiling over the spur and turned slowly over the Acropolis. The Répertoire read the heat curves and the soundings of the river bed and issued a reformation warning for the bend at Depth 40 to every hold in the theatre. It assessed the peak as the largest of the Third's six hundred years on the Flats, and assessed that the Third could not meet it with what remained after the Big Push. The Command sent the Third a reserve.

### 5.9 Segment Twelve (Rotation Eight, Cycle 201)

The reserve was sent to [[Ch19 - Rex Invictus XII|Segment Twelve]], the last segment of the Third's arc, at its extreme south-western end, where the trenches ran along the rim of the [[Demus Gorge]] and stopped. It had been a fringe position for two hundred years, held by a single battalion of the Ninth Line Phalanx under a Major, and its name came from the ration post and from the augur overlay that stamped the same identifier on its soldiers' visors whenever the alarms sounded. It was there that the reformation came up first. The Répertoire had predicted the peak at the bend. The Kindlers had called it beneath the Demus Gorge, nine kilometres down, and on the two hundredth cycle of Rotation Eight it climbed the Gorge walls in the dark watch and came over the rim into Segment Twelve.

Segment Twelve held. Its walls had been dug millennia earlier by soldiers who knew the work, and its battalion held behind them against the first wave and the second, with rifles, keyed mortars and a legacy of [[Poseidon]] who flooded the kill corridor with brine. It held when a breach opened in front of it, an absence where none should have been, with forms marching through it almost in order. It held when something tall and armoured came out of the breach and raised its hand and folded the ground before the parapet upward, turning the range stakes to dust. The segment's centurion then triggered the emergency beacon.

The reserve answered. Two ships of [[UOE Navy|the Fleet]], the Axis Dawn and the Warbringer Fury, had come down through the ceiling and were holding station over Segment Twelve in the low geometry, within the reach of the [[Ceiling-Lance|ceiling-lances]] and against every rule the Fleet had for Depth 40, since the reformation warning had stated that the reserve was needed at once. They carried four million soldiers raised for the peak, with their armour, guns and [[Titan (Mech)|Behemoths]] and the supplies for a theatre. The Axis Dawn's augur placed her at twenty-three percent operational after the passage through the ceiling, and the Warbringer Fury at forty-one. The heavy batteries of both ships fired on the breach, and the breach and what was in it were taken out of the world. With the ships came the reserve's commander. The Command had sent its most senior available field commander to take the peak in hand, a Senior Commander of the [[Waves of Poseidon]] ranked nine thousand and fifty-first on the Unified Leaderboard, callsign [[Dulaberre-Six]]. She was [[Marien Phyrrus|Marien Pyrrhus]], daughter of [[Stephanos Pyrrhus]], and she came down to Segment Twelve with the first pallets to see the ground for herself.

She was on the landing shelf behind the segment with [[The Major|the Major]] when the shelf ceased to exist, from its edge inward, into an absence that took the light and returned nothing. Marien Pyrrhus fell into it while pulling soldiers clear, and her bodyguard went in after her. Sector Command ruled that if Dulaberre-Six went in, the ships could not leave her. The Major put his battalion and its wounded into the ships' holds while the absence consumed the rest of the shelf, and the Axis Dawn and the Warbringer Fury went in after Marien Pyrrhus. The Répertoire's log for Segment Twelve ends with four lines.

> DULABERRE-SIX. NO SIGNAL.
> UOE AXIS DAWN. NO SIGNAL.
> UOE WARBRINGER FURY. NO SIGNAL.
> RESERVE: NOT AVAILABLE.

The Répertoire classed the event as a subreality shear and the loss as total, and struck the reserve, its officers, its four million soldiers and its guns from the Third's books. It would be a long time before the Third learned where they had gone. The reformation peak came up the Demus Gorge that night onto a front with nothing behind it, and while the Third's attention was fixed on the Gorge, the river at the bend began to give up its dead.

### 5.10 On the Tides of the River (Rotation Eight, Cycles 201 to 204)

The Echo Mast had been calling down the rivers for two rotations, and most of what heard it had already come up in small numbers. The Kindlers' peak brought the rest, and with it the drowned fleet. For as long as the Empire had flown ships in Tartarus, the Phlegethon had been taking them. A ship that went down over the bend did not burn out in the river. The river kept it, hot, whole and dark, on its bed, with its dead crew still at their stations, and there were hundreds of such hulls under the bend at Depth 40. For two rotations, since the Echo Mast first called, the [[Frost Masons]] of the Cocytus had been working on some of them, coming up the river beneath the ice they made around themselves and sealing the breaches with Cocytus ice that did not melt in the Phlegethon. The Kindlers' peak lit the hulls' reactors with river fire, and on the two hundred and fourth cycle of Rotation Eight they began to rise.

The first came up out of the river at the bend in the dark watch and climbed on its old engines into the low geometry over the Flats, inside the lance envelope. The lances did not fire on it, since the Arsenal's gunners had been told what to expect. It was twelve kilometres long, a hull of bronze-green divine steel with a prow carved into the figure of [[Apollo]] a kilometre high, half the god's face burned away and Cocytus ice packed into a split that ran along a third of the hull. The Répertoire identified it at once. It was AC-09-1642, the Eye of Light, which the Order of the Hollow Crescent had captured and the Empire recovered at Luna, and which Minerva had crashed into the Tartarus front at Grid Delta in Rotation Four as the waypoint for CODE 78E (see Rex Invictus VIII, Chapter 15). After the Wedge was driven in, its wreck had lain half-buried at the river's edge at Grid Delta until the end of Rotation Five, when the river rose at Depth 38 and the wreck was gone when it fell again. The Wedge's engineers had logged it as lost in the Phlegethon. The river had carried it along its bed for two rotations to the bend at Depth 40, where the Frost Masons found it. Hesper Calloway had gone down to it on the first cycle it was sealed and taken it for her own, and her Forsworn now crewed it from its old stations alongside its drowned crew. Behind it came the rest of the drowned fleet.

### 5.11 Imperial Fleet Presence Over the Flats

The Tartarus Fleet's squadron over the Flats had been on station for nine rotations, since it came down with the Third's first convoy under [[Iole Saturnin|Navarch Iole Saturnin]] in the [[Cruiser|heavy cruiser]] [[Pallas Ascendant]]. Its task was to guard the convoys from the ceiling cracks down to [[Landing Shelf Kappa]] and to hold station north of the lance envelope to keep the ceiling clear of winged things that came up the river. The Pallas Ascendant was one of four hundred of her class in the Fleet and could outfight anything the deep had sent up through the ceiling at Depth 40 in six hundred years, and the Third's soldiers, who called her the Old Owl, looked up at her running lights from the trenches every night. Since nobody had expected the river to produce anything she could not handle, the Fleet had withdrawn most of the squadron for other work over nine rotations. By Rotation Eight Saturnin had the Pallas Ascendant, three Lantern-pattern [[Frigate|frigates]] overdue for refit, three ceiling monitors fit to fight but short of crew, two armed haulers, a troop lighter and three fire barges, old ore-haulers packed with keyed charges for use against things too large to shoot. She had reported to the Fleet every rotation that the ceiling over the Flats was secure. When the Eye of Light rose, she sent her frigates north and east as pickets, with orders to find what else was coming, avoid action and return at full speed. The frigate Iris Four returned first, having found three drowned raiders of a pattern two thousand years old, which had come close enough to read the squadron and then withdrawn into the river cloud without firing. Saturnin recognised a probe. The deep now knew her strength, or her lack of it, and would come in enough strength to overwhelm her.

### 5.12 The Battle of the Low Geometry (Rotation Eight, Cycles 210 to 211)

On the two hundred and tenth cycle the pickets counted fourteen hulls rising from the river at the bend and climbing the ceiling geometry toward the squadron in two lines. Saturnin met them with everything she had. The Fleet sent a cruiser group from the ceiling cracks at Depth 36 under [[Cassia Thorne|Navarch Cassia Thorne]] in the cruiser [[Themis Unbowed]], with the cruiser Hyperion, the old grand cruiser [[Patience of Hera]] and a squadron of escorts, but it arrived several hours behind the drowned fleet and could not prevent several drowned ships from passing into the geometry over the Flats. The drowned fleet divided. Five hulls led by the Eye of Light swung around the northern end of Saturnin's line, while nine more, led by a drowned heavy cruiser the Répertoire could not name and the Third later called the [[Cinder Dawn]], passed down its southern side, and the two lines went past each other at close range in a gun battle that wrecked both. The Eye of Light raked the length of Saturnin's line as she passed. Her batteries had been built to fight the Princes, and the river had not harmed them. They stripped the squadron's shields and buckled its armour. The frigate Iris One was crippled and left drifting, two monitors were destroyed, and the troop lighter Long Labour lost three reactors to the Cinder Dawn's attack craft and was left without thrusters. Saturnin had to abandon her, and the Long Labour's crew kept her guns firing. The Pallas Ascendant had been hit repeatedly and was listing, but she was still fighting, and her guns had damaged a drowned transport hulk and destroyed one of the raiders. For reasons nobody in the squadron understood, two drowned cruisers in the southern line had not fired at all, and as Saturnin watched they turned away. Their guns could have finished the Pallas Ascendant and every ship with her. Saturnin did not know why they held their fire, nor that Thorne's cruisers were coming, since most of her relays had been shot away.

The Eye of Light and the drowned transport pressed on toward the Flats to land their cargo, while the Cinder Dawn launched boarding boats at the crippled Long Labour. Saturnin sent in her fire barges. The crew of one, ordered to steer it onto its target and abandon it, rode it into the stern of the drowned transport instead, and the explosion of its keyed charges sent the transport down. Its drowned crew brought the descent under some control, and it came down on the [[Shale Plateau]] beyond the head of the Demus Gorge, a hundred and forty kilometres behind the Third's line in the south-west, where it tore a furrow three kilometres long and lay without moving, its holds shut. While the transport fell, the Eye of Light descended into the lowest geometry over the Flats, inside the lance envelope where no ship of the Empire could follow, and opened her holds. The allies the Forsworn had gathered in the river for two rotations came down onto the Cinder Flats in their thousands, in drowned landing craft, boarding boats and the ship's own Lanterns, and from her lower holds she lowered on her cargo cables four drowned Behemoths, walking engines of the Forge of [[Hephaestus]] lost in the river over the millennia, which stood up on the ash before the Mora Cut and began to walk.

Navarch Thorne knew she was too late to prevent the landing and came on in the hope of catching the Eye of Light as she climbed back out. In the fighting that followed her escorts were cut to pieces and the Pallas Ascendant's engines were wrecked, and the battle turned decisively against the Fleet. The drowned fleet still had four capital ships in action, the Eye of Light and the Cinder Dawn among them, and the Fleet had lost the low geometry over the Flats. Saturnin, in the last relay she could raise, asked the captain of the Patience of Hera to report her own view to the Fleet's board of inquiry, which was that she had failed in her duty. She did not expect to keep her command. Thorne would not go home beaten while one chance remained. She ordered the Themis Unbowed to ramming speed against the Eye of Light. The ram is still taught at the Fleet's school at [[Callistus]]. It was brave and it failed. The Themis Unbowed struck the Eye of Light a third of the way along her length, below the burned face of the god, and broke herself on a hull that the Cocytus ice held together. Thorne's crew of eleven thousand were transferred in the next four seconds, and ninety percent of them returned. Thorne did not.

The battle left the low geometry over the Flats a field of wrecks. The Fleet lost or abandoned eleven ships, the Themis Unbowed, four frigates and escorts, two monitors, the Long Labour and the three fire barges, and every other ship engaged was damaged. The Pallas Ascendant barely survived and would need two rotations in the yards. The Hyperion was badly hurt, and only the Patience of Hera came out fit to fight. Of the drowned fleet, the Fleet could confirm the destruction of the transport on the Shale Plateau and four raiders. The rest had withdrawn into the river cloud or into the geometry over the spur, where nothing could reach them, and the drowned now held the low geometry over the Arsenal and could land whatever they wished.

The Fleet's board of inquiry sat at Callistus in Rotation Nine. It found that Navarch Saturnin had been left with a squadron unfit for the station she held and had reported it as fit for nine rotations, and that the Fleet had withdrawn her ships in the belief that the river could produce nothing she could not meet. It found both errors to be the Fleet's and Saturnin's together, and it declined to accept her own verdict on herself. She was relieved of the station and given the Pallas Ascendant again when the yards had finished with her. Navarch Thorne was commended posthumously for the ram. The inquiry's harshest findings were reserved for the Fleet's intelligence officers, who had held the Répertoire's reformation warning for Depth 40 for nine cycles without connecting it to the hulls on the river bed, which had been charted for two thousand years. A drowned fleet had been assembled under the bend for two rotations, within sight of a Fleet squadron, and nobody in the Fleet had thought to ask what the Frost Masons were doing there. They had failed in their duty, and the Third paid for it.

The strategic consequences were grave. With the low geometry lost, the Third's supply rested entirely on the relay cables to Landing Shelf Kappa, which the drowned could now threaten from above, and the Fleet could no longer promise to keep the ceiling over the Flats clear. The Répertoire's plan for the inner belt had assumed that once the ceiling-lances were reduced the Tartarus Fleet would bring its heavy cruisers down over the Arsenal to shell the Acropolis. That assumption had to be abandoned. Whatever the lances did, the Fleet could not come down into a geometry held by a drowned battleship of the [[AnnChase Pattern|AnnChase]] pattern with her batteries intact. The Arsenal would have to be taken by the Third alone, against an enemy that was now reinforced from the river faster than the Third could kill it.

The Cinder Dawn landed her cargo the next cycle, ten thousand Cinderborn of the Red Furnace, the Phlegethon's fighting congregation, under their war-priest [[Draugos the Kindled]], who had burned and healed for four hundred years and whose followers held that he could not die by fire. From the river cloud, the drowned ship the Third came to call the Rimebound lowered onto the spur the Frost Masons of the Cocytus, a warband of siege engineers in ice-white armour who would build for the Archflamen defences the Third had never seen. By the Répertoire's estimate more than sixty thousand new enemies came down onto the Flats in the eleven cycles after the battle of the low geometry. None carried implants, and all had come to kill the Empire's soldiers in a way that would last. In ten cycles the Third had lost its reserve at Segment Twelve and its fleet over the Flats. It stood before the inner belt of the [[Pyriphlegethon Arsenal]] on sixty-one kilometres of front, with a reformation peak climbing the Demus Gorge on its flank, four drowned Behemoths walking toward it from the Mora Cut, and nothing coming down the Stair behind it.

## 6 Breaking the Deadlock

> *"There is no after. There is the Flats, and then there is the next segment of the Flats."*
>
> Optio Hale Tarrant, Sixth Cohort, Forty-Fourth Trench Regiment

### 6.1 The Greatest Obstacle (Rotation Eight, Cycle 160)

The breaking of the second belt by the Fifty-Second Trench Regiment brought down the whole of the Archflamen's second line. With the Third advancing along the length of the arc, the cohorts on the second belt had either to withdraw or be surrounded, and they withdrew to the inner belt, leaving rearguards in the nests to delay the pursuit. The inner belt encircled the spur on a front of sixty-one kilometres. It was a zone of works eight kilometres deep, thicker than the outer and second belts combined, and after the Arsenal itself it was the oldest structure on the Flats. The Third had dug it six hundred years before, when the Arsenal was the Third's own anchor against the Phlegethon at Depth 40, and every Archflamen and Steward since had extended it as the vaults went deeper and the Arsenal grew. Its plans were in the Third's archive, and the Third's engineers regarded them as the most discouraging documents it held.

The inner belt comprised trench lines, bunkers, pillboxes, gun pits sunk into the basalt and roofed with it, and redoubts with their supporting works, all sited so that every approach was a killing ground swept from three directions. Beneath it ran galleries and shelters too deep for the Phaethons to reach, and before it lay the wire and the [[Mines (explosive)|minefields]]. Unlike the outer belts, it was also covered by the ceiling-lances. Sixty of them stood in their silos across the inner belt and the spur, and although they had been built to keep the deep's flying things out of the ceiling geometry, the Arsenal's gunners had discovered in the first rotation of the siege that a lance could fire flat across the Flats, crossing a regiment's front from end to end in a second. The Phaethons could now reach the lance silos but could not break them, since the silos had been built to withstand anything that came down out of the ceiling and the Third possessed nothing on the ground as heavy. The Archflamen's army had had eight rotations to prepare the belt, and every gun in it had been ranged on every metre of its approaches.

Before the revolt, the Arsenal had been considered impregnable, and it had been built to be. Only the Empire, with its vats and the inexhaustible drafts of the ceiling worlds, could have contemplated attacking it directly, and even the Third had needed eight rotations and twenty-three million transfers to reach the inner belt. Many of the Third's officers believed that the worst now lay before them. Inside the inner belt stood the Cinder Wall, and inside the Wall the cinder cone and the Acropolis, but those were fixed targets that the Phaethons could grind down in time. The inner belt presented ten thousand separate targets, each of which would have to be taken a few hundred metres at a time, by attrition and at great cost. Once the Third was through it, the staff on Quarterdeck Nineteen believed, the Wall and the Acropolis would fall quickly. They still believed it on the hundred and sixtieth cycle of Rotation Eight, before the drowned came down.

The Archflamen's dispositions on the inner belt reflected the losses of the Big Push and the new strength the river had brought him. The thrall cohorts that had escaped the second belt were placed in the forward works, where they would absorb the first weight of any assault, and behind them the garrison cohorts and the [[Sworn of Orrhae]] held the redoubts and the lance silos. The Forsworn held no sector of their own. They were distributed through the belt in small parties, at the junctions and in the deep galleries, where they could direct the counter-attacks and deny any lodgement the Third made. The Frost Masons of the Cocytus, landed on the spur by the Rimebound, began within a few cycles of their arrival to rebuild the works the Phaethons had damaged. Their ice set harder than the Arsenal's concrete and did not melt in the heat of the Flats, and a bunker roofed with it shed a Phaethon shell as a parapet shed rain. By the end of Rotation Eight the Third's spotters had logged more than four hundred new or rebuilt works on the inner belt faced in white, and the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx's fire on them produced no effect its observers could measure.

The ceiling-lances were the inner belt's chief strength and the Third's chief problem. They could not be reached by the Fleet, which had lost the low geometry, and they could not be broken by the Phaethons. The Répertoire's plan for the inner belt required them to be taken from the ground, silo by silo, as the [[Twenty-Ninth Trench Regiment|Twenty-Ninth]] had tried and failed to take Lance Seven in Rotation Five, and each silo was now sited inside the strongest works on the Flats. Until they were taken, any attack the Third made on the inner belt would be swept by fire against which no trench and no armour gave protection, and every regiment that went forward would do so knowing that a single lance could cross its front from end to end in a second.

### 6.2 Small Gains (Rotation Eight, Cycles 160 to 270)

The Command's original allowance of four rotations had been extended twice since the start of the siege, and by Rotation Eight Lord-General Castorre had been given twelve rotations in all to retake the Arsenal, with the full support of the Dionysian Logistics Directorate in shells and soldiers. His plan had been the Répertoire's, methodical, well supplied and indifferent to its price, and he had four rotations remaining. No one on his staff believed the timetable could be kept, and no one knew what would follow when it expired. Most assumed it would mean failure: the Directorate would reduce the Third's priority, regiments would be withdrawn for other fronts, and the Third would be left before the Arsenal in a war it could not finish. If the Archflamen held for four more rotations, he would have won, whether or not he understood it. His army had suffered as heavily as the Third, and more, since its dead did not return, but it had held for eight rotations by fighting a defensive war that bled the Third while conserving its own strength. It still had the stores of the vaults and the soldiers to use them, and it now had the river as well.

Castorre's difficulty was a simple one. He was short of time and needed a new offensive at once, and his commanders on the ground told him that the Big Push had worn their regiments to nothing. The losses had been staggering, the vats at Watchpoint Hades would need weeks to clear them, and the Archflamen's cohorts were still fighting hard, some of them with the zeal of fanatics. An attack on the inner belt in that condition would be futile and would cost transfers for no gain. The regiments needed time to refill and rebuild, and until then they would harass the inner belt with guns, raids and limited attacks while the general offensive waited. Every soldier on the Flats understood that the same pause gave the Archflamen time to rebuild his own strength.

The Third therefore advanced by the slow method, digging. Its regiments came up to the inner belt and dug in facing it, and the ground between the old second belt and the inner belt filled with saps, dugouts and communication trenches running back to the second belt, which spared the Third's columns the old dock roads, ranged so precisely by the Archflamen's gunners that a column on a road was destroyed before it heard the shells. Harassing fire fell on the working parties every watch, and the work continued under it.

The pause also exposed the Third's command to criticism it had so far escaped. The Big Push had broken the second belt, and for some cycles after it the Third's reputation stood higher in the Command than at any time since the siege began, but the price of the offensive was now known in every hold of the theatre, and so was the fact that it had been won by a colonel the Eighth Line Phalanx had overruled. Castorre's Phalanx-Generals were men and women of his own school, Athenians of the Academy on Callistus, chosen for their method and their care with supplies, and the Big Push had shown that none of them could break a belt by their methods alone. [[Oren Callix|Callix]] of the Second, whose probe had taken [[Redoubt K-19|K-19]], was the only one who had won a battle the Répertoire had not planned for him, and he was ill regarded by the staff on Quarterdeck Nineteen for it. Castorre did not replace any of them. He trusted their competence, and he had four rotations left, and he did not believe a new commander could learn the Flats in less.

### 6.3 The Battle of Gallows Rise (Rotation Eight, Cycles 270 to 278)

On Leaf 55-47, the Fifty-Second Trench Regiment of the Eighth Line Phalanx, under Colonel Damaris Lowe, held the right flank of the Eighth's line where it met the south-western shoulder of the inner belt. Its front ran along a shallow valley in the ash, overlooked two kilometres away by a low ridge that formed part of the inner belt, and its forward cohorts were under plunging fire every watch. Lowe could withdraw them out of range and leave a wide no-man's land that would later have to be recrossed, or she could take the ridge. The Eighth Line Phalanx urged her to take it. From Leaf 55-47 a series of low ridges ran south-east toward the spur, each of which gave the Archflamen the best observation and firing positions for kilometres and would have to be taken in turn. The Fifty-Second named the first of them [[Gallows Rise]], for the bodies hanging on the wire along its crest.

The Fifty-Second was in no condition for a set-piece attack. It was at half strength after the Big Push, and the vats were returning its soldiers only a few hundred at a time. Lowe's objectives were accordingly limited. She did not intend to drive deep into the inner belt, only to throw the Archflamen's cohorts off the high ground above her, while the regiments on either side made diversionary attacks to prevent the enemy moving reserves along the belt to meet her. She would attack on a front of four kilometres with five cohorts in the first wave and two in reserve, and two more cohorts would make a supporting attack on the left, none of them at full strength and some below half. The regiment's tank company could field four serviceable Thureos for the attack, and the Eighth Line Phalanx released two [[Colossus Super-Heavy|Colossus]] super-heavies from its reserve for the hardest objective. The key to the attack was the artillery. Three hundred and sixty guns would fire, most of them in a box barrage around the ridge to keep reinforcements out, but they were short of shells and in poor repair after the Big Push, with worn barrels and failing recoil pistons, and the heavier pieces were throwing their shells wide. After the barrage the mortars would lay [[Smoke (SMK)|smoke]], behind which the cohorts and tanks would advance up the bare slope while the heavy weapons in the forward bays fired at high angle over their heads onto the Archflamen's heavy weapons on the reverse slope.

The Archflamen's works on Gallows Rise were strong. A forward trench was backed by a solid line with pillboxes for heavy guns, dugout shelters beneath it and hardened bunkers within it, and wire, tank traps and mines on the slope in front. On the left of the attack, where the ridge turned, stood a knot of works and gun pits designated Bastion G-70, and in the centre a second strongpoint the Fifty-Second called the Gallows. On the far slope stood the Archflamen's guns, ready to lay a counter-barrage into no-man's land. The Second Cohort was given the Gallows. The Eighth Cohort, under Centurion [[Ostap Lerche]], a veteran of three regiments who had been on the Flats since Rotation Three and had been transferred eleven times, was given Bastion G-70, with the four Thureos and the two Colossi in support.

The barrage opened on the two hundred and seventy-seventh cycle and rolled over Bastion G-70 and the far slope through the night, until the mortars began to fire smoke and the smoke mixed with the ash into a grey fog over no-man's land. On the two hundred and seventy-eighth cycle the Fifty-Second went over the parapet behind it, and as it advanced its own guns fell silent and the Archflamen's opened. On the right, the Eleventh Cohort went furthest, past the Gallows, before it was caught in a crossfire of heavy guns from its left and went to ground in the shell-holes, its centurion transferred in the first salvo. The Second Cohort, attacking the Gallows itself, came under the heaviest fire on the ridge, including incendiary mortar rounds that threw a gel of river-slag able to burn through armour, and its leading maniples went to ground. In the centre the Seventh Cohort crawled forward under the same fire.

The Eighth Cohort's front before Bastion G-70 had taken the heaviest of the barrage and was nothing but craters. One Thureos bogged down in the first of them and served for the rest of the action as a fixed pillbox, firing up the slope at the bastion. When the cohort emerged from the smoke, the bastion's pillboxes and bunkers opened fire. The first Colossus was struck repeatedly by rounds and lance-bolts as it swept the trenches with its twin main guns. The second lost its right track to a heavy shell and sat immobile in no-man's land. A second Thureos struck a mine and burned, and its crew were killed as they escaped. On the left, the supporting attack climbed the steepest part of the ridge slowly under mortar fire, while the two reserve cohorts came forward into the forward trench and a squadron of outriders waited on their hounds for a breakthrough.

The battle continued throughout the cycle with the artillery of both sides engaged overhead. In the centre, the Seventh Cohort reached the Archflamen's forward trench and cleared it, the defenders appearing to have withdrawn into their strongpoints, and more maniples were sent forward to hold it. A battery of quad-launchers ordered across no-man's land in support was caught by a barrage on the way and its tractors destroyed. The expected counter-attack came out of the inner belt behind the ridge in a wave of thralls and Sworn, who carried the trench hand to hand. The Seventh's centurion was transferred in the fighting, and those of its soldiers who did not escape were killed or taken.

On the left, Lerche's Eighth Cohort closed on Bastion G-70 crater by crater, aided by the surviving Colossus, which broke open one of the bastion's bunkers with a direct hit. With a few more sections Lerche judged he could take the position, and with his signaller wounded by a mine he reported to the regiment's command dugout that Bastion G-70 would fall if the second wave were sent at once. The second wave was still being organised and supplied when the order came, and it went over the parapet across ground swept by every gun on the ridge. It reached the forward elements of the Eighth, which had a foothold in the bastion's forward trench, and carried the attack into the bastion with the bayonet. Lerche led the assault on the breached bunker himself, clearing the approach trench with grenades, ordering a flame section to burn out the breach, and entering it at the head of his soldiers. The defenders were killed hand to hand. Lerche, wounded in the wrist and burned, organised the bunker's defence and refused evacuation. The remaining bunkers of Bastion G-70 fell one after another, each after hard fighting, and the last Colossus was driven into the bastion to become part of its wall. With the Eighth Cohort now clearing the ridge's trenches from the flank, the fire on the other cohorts slackened, and the attacks that had gone to ground were renewed. The Seventh and the Second soon reported their objectives taken. The forward works on Gallows Rise belonged to the Fifty-Second.

By the standards of the inner belt the capture of Gallows Rise was a model action. A regiment at half strength, with worn guns and too few shells, had taken a fortified ridge on a front of four kilometres in a single cycle, and its losses, eleven thousand transfers, were below the Répertoire's estimate for the first time since the siege began. The Eighth Line Phalanx reported it to Quarterdeck Nineteen as proof that the inner belt could be broken by limited attacks of the same kind, ridge by ridge, until the spur was reached, and the staff began to plan the next of them before the Fifty-Second had finished consolidating. The method was sound. It was overtaken within the hour.

### 6.4 The Drowned Land (Rotation Eight, Cycle 278)

As the Fifty-Second consolidated its objectives, a seep-storm broke over Gallows Rise, and out of the low geometry, where the Eye of Light and the Cinder Dawn had held station since the battle with the Fleet, came the landing craft of the drowned fleet. The drowned ships bombarded the Flats first. The Eye of Light's batteries had been built for the House of Apollo to fight the Princes of the deep, and their solar lances turned the ash to burning glass while her heavy shells, each the weight of a Phaethon round, left craters the size of the Arsenal's heat-wells. The Third had no means of reply, since no counter-battery fire could reach a ship in the low geometry. Trench sections were destroyed by single hits and Phaethons overturned in their pits. A single shell from the Eye of Light's main battery landed eleven kilometres behind Gallows Rise, and the Fifty-Second's own forward trench at the foot of the ridge took a direct hit.

The landing craft followed the bombardment down through the storm, burning from the river fire in their old hulls, and burst open on the ash to release the Cinderborn of the Red Furnace. Most of them had been aimed at the Third's forward line before the inner belt, and the Cinderborn went into the Third's trenches wherever they found them and killed everyone within. The Third was wholly unprepared for an assault from above. Its strength was in the forward bays facing the inner belt, and its gun pits, supply trenches and command dugouts were attacked from behind and overrun. The Third had built its entire war around trench lines and set-piece attacks, and its methods offered no answer to an enemy that struck from every direction at once. Reports came in from sectors far behind the line that no one had believed the Archflamen could reach.

On Gallows Rise, a warband of the Cinderborn fell upon the Eighth Cohort in Bastion G-70. Slag-gel and solar shells did not harm them, since they had been made in the river and its fire ran in them, and the Eighth had little chance against them. Many of Lerche's soldiers broke and went back down the slope. The Cinderborn climbed the last Colossus, tore open its hatches and killed its crew, and stormed the bunker Lerche held. He was killed in the bunker by a champion of the Red Furnace. His implant fired, and the Répertoire logged the upload as null. Draugos the Kindled had blessed his warbands' blades in the Phlegethon at the bend before they came up, and a blade of the river severed what the Third sent upward as cleanly as Styx-iron. The landing threw the Fifty-Second off Gallows Rise and returned the ridge to the Archflamen.

### 6.5 Death from Above (Rotation Eight, Cycles 279 to 300)

The arrival of the drowned cost the Third the war it had been fighting. For eight rotations the Third had chosen where battles would be fought, and it could no longer do so. The Cinderborn warbands moved through the trench lines and attacked the Third's soldiers wherever they were found, while the Forsworn from the Eye of Light went through the Third's rear areas in the Empire's armour and entered its command dugouts using the Empire's codes, so that the Third's soldiers could no longer distinguish friend from foe until the shooting started. Where the drowned struck hardest they put the Archflamen's line back where it had stood before the Big Push, and they ensured that the siege would continue.

The Répertoire's reconstruction of the landings of the two hundred and seventy-eighth cycle counted forty-one separate descents along the Third's arc in the first six hours, from single landing craft carrying a warband of a hundred Cinderborn to the Eye of Light's own Lanterns, which put down two thousand Forsworn and drowned soldiers behind the Fifth Line Phalanx at Ore Head. Nine of the Third's regimental command dugouts were overrun in that time, and two Phalanx headquarters were attacked. The heaviest blow fell on the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx, whose gun lines eighty kilometres behind the front had never been attacked by anything and were guarded by their own crews. The Cinderborn came down among the Phaethon pits in the third hour and destroyed eleven of the great guns before the gun crews and a regiment hurried back from the line drove them off. The Third's transfers in the first cycle of the landings came to two hundred and four thousand, more than on any single cycle of the Big Push, and nineteen thousand of them were null. The proportion was the highest the Third had recorded. Most of the nulls fell to the blades of the Red Furnace and to the Styx-iron of the Forsworn, and the rest to whatever had been catching the Third's uploads on the Flats since the first barrage, which was now catching more.

The Third's response was improvised and, given its training, creditable. Its regiments cut their trench systems into separate keeps that could be held independently, and turned their support lines to face outward. The Phalanx-Generals pulled cohorts back from the forward bays to form local reserves able to counter-attack a landing within the hour, and the outrider squadrons, which had had little to do since the outer belt fell, became the Third's fastest means of reaching a landing before it could dig in. Within ten cycles most landings were being destroyed within a watch of coming down. The drowned adapted in their turn, landing at night and in the seep-storms, and in greater numbers.

A slow siege of attrition had become a fluid war. The Cinderborn and the Forsworn could descend on the Third's rear wherever they chose out of the low geometry that the Fleet had lost. The Third recovered from the first shock. The drowned had come down without heavy guns or armour, the Third's trenches were deep, and the raiders could not hold what they took. In some places the front had been broken and the Archflamen had followed through to push it back, while in others it had held. Over the following weeks, the drowned ships brought down heavier equipment, and the Archflamen's cohorts came out across no-man's land to join the attacks on the Third's weakened forward positions and retake ground lost the year before. Where a clear ring of trenches had surrounded the spur, there was now a ragged line that bulged forward in some sectors and fell back in others.

The gravest consequence was to the Third's supply. Its supplies came down the relay cables at Landing Shelf Kappa from convoys in the low geometry that the Fleet had guarded for nine rotations, and with the Fleet driven from the low geometry the convoys could not come down. The shell stocks on the Flats remained large, thanks to the quartermasters' decision in Rotation Zero to stock for six rotations, but they could not now be replaced. Neither could the soldiers. The vats at Watchpoint Hades could still write the Third's transfers into new bodies, but the bodies could not be brought back down the cables, and by the end of Rotation Eight three hundred and ten thousand soldiers of the Third were waiting at the ceiling depots for a way down. Castorre's staff set to work calculating, from every quartermaster's return they had, how long the Third could fight on its dwindling stores.

Their answer was nine months of front time at the rate of fire the Third had sustained since the Big Push, or fifteen at half that rate. The Third chose the second figure. From the two hundred and ninetieth cycle the divine batteries were rationed to forty shells a gun a cycle and the Phaethons to six, harassing fire on the inner belt was suspended except on leaves under attack, and every keyed shell on the Flats was placed under the control of the Phalanx-Generals. The Third had fought for eight rotations in the knowledge that its guns would always have more shells than the Archflamen's. From Rotation Eight it no longer did. The Arsenal's vaults still held eight thousand years of stock, and the Archflamen's gunners, who had never rationed anything, fired more in the last months of Rotation Eight than at any time since the first barrage.

The Third also had to learn to fight under an enemy sky. It had no anti-air guns on the Flats beyond the heavy weapons of its regiments, since the ceiling-lances had kept the geometry clear for six hundred years, and its trenches had been dug against shellfire from the inner belt rather than attack from above. The quartermasters converted quad-launchers to fire upward and sent them forward to the gun lines. The engineers roofed the communication trenches near the command dugouts with steel and basalt, and the regiments began to post sentries facing the ceiling. None of it was enough. The drowned ships could land a warband anywhere on the Flats out of the river cloud, and the Third could do no more than make each landing more expensive than the last.

Word of the situation on the Flats reached the Command at Watchpoint Hades, which was alarmed. After eight rotations, the reduction of the Arsenal was in danger of complete failure. The Command ordered an inquiry, led by the Dionysian Logistics Directorate under the senior Logothete [[Irene Vasska]], with the power to remove the Third's command if it found that the command had failed in its duty. Word of the impending defeat also had to go up to the Directorate's masters on the ceiling worlds, and in time to the Pontifical College and to [[Pyrrhus Command]], whose anger at the waste would end many careers and send many officers to the Cocytus front or the Forlorn Cohorts. None of the politics did anything for the Third's soldiers before the inner belt.

### 6.6 The Lament of Ixion (Rotation Eight, Cycle 300)

The worst news came on the three hundredth cycle. The drowned transport that the fire barge had driven down onto the Shale Plateau during the battle of the low geometry had lain there for ninety cycles without moving, one hundred and forty kilometres behind the Third's line in the south-west, beyond the head of the Demus Gorge. The Third's outriders had observed it from a distance and found its holds shut, and the staff had marked it on their maps as the Lament of Ixion, from the name on its hull in the Fleet's old script, and treated it as a wreck. On the three hundredth cycle its holds opened. The crash had killed much of what was inside, but much more had survived and spent the ninety cycles preparing, and it now came out onto the Shale Plateau and formed up, far behind the Third's line. Among the drowned host, out of the transport's lower holds, came drowned Behemoths.

All the Behemoths of the Empire had once been loyal. The Forge of Hephaestus had built them to walk the deep holds and crush what rose from the rivers, and for eight thousand years they had done so, but over the millennia the rivers had taken some of them in floods and lost battles. A Behemoth that went down into the Phlegethon did not die there. The river preserved it with its crew in the cradle, as it preserved the ships, and in time the crew ceased to be the crew and the engine walked for the river. Nine of them came out of the Lament of Ixion, the tallest sixty metres high, its plating black for a thousand years. They had one hundred and sixty kilometres of the Flats to cross with nothing in their path, and the Third first learned where they had gone when they came around the head of the Demus Gorge behind the Ninth Line Phalanx, advancing north along the old second belt. With the rear of the Ninth under direct threat, the [[Sixth Assault Phalanx]] was ordered out of the line at once, to take whatever additional tanks it could from the Fourth and meet the drowned host.

### 6.7 The Battle of the Demus Rim (Rotation Eight, Cycles 319 to 358)

The Sixth Assault Phalanx began to move on the three hundred and nineteenth cycle. By the time it was in position to attack, the drowned host had advanced a further fifty kilometres north and occupied the old second belt as it came. The strategic position was grave. The Archflamen's army was attacking the Third's front, while the drowned host in its rear threatened to cut the Eighth and Ninth Line Phalanxes off from the rest of the Third. Only the Sixth, assembling to attack on Leaf 61-29 on the rim of the Demus Gorge, stood in the way, and if it failed the Third would pay heavily.

The battle began on the three hundred and fifty-eighth cycle. The Sixth's tanks advanced first by companies in line abreast, at a walking pace in parade order, and with them went the phalanx's few Behemoth-hunters, the long [[Gigantomachy Lance|Gigantomachy lances]] on Colossus hulls built to kill engines like those before them, together with a company of ordinary Colossi. Behind the tanks came the infantry of the Sixth's Trench Regiments on foot, including the Thirty-Seventh, lent to the Sixth for the purpose, and behind them the field guns, still on their tractors.
