Heavy artillery was scarce, since there had been no time to bring forward the Phaethons or their shells, and there had been no time for reconnaissance either. It was a war of movement, and the Third had not been built for one.

The drowned host was a horde of the river's leavings, drowned soldiers and sailors in the rotted armour of a dozen centuries alongside creatures of the deep for which the Third had no names, led and herded by the Forsworn and driven on by the dirges broadcast from the Behemoths' war-horns. The first blow fell from above. Stymphalian swarms came down out of the river cloud in their thousands, throwing their bronze feathers like darts, and with them came the drowned ships' old Lanterns, flown by drowned crews. The Sixth's tanks had no weapons able to engage them, and as the formations broke and scattered under the attack, tank after tank was set burning. Nothing had attacked the Third from the air over the Flats since the first rotation, when the ceiling-lances had cleared the Stymphalians from the dark as fast as they came. Now the Archflamen held the lances and the drowned held the low geometry, and within an hour the Third had lost control of the air over the whole of the southern Flats.

The leading tank companies pressed on and reached the drowned host's first line, where the Forsworn had dug in to receive them, and the field guns came into action behind them. The first positions appeared to have been overrun. By then, however, the Behemoths had the Sixth's range and began to destroy it, tank after tank, with Phlegethon-cannon that threw the river itself and with drowned versions of the Forge's own hammer-guns. The Sixth's return fire broke harmlessly against the Behemoths' Aegis-[[Energy Shielding|wards]]. Even the Gigantomachy lances proved unequal to the fight. One of them found a firing position on a cinder bank and put three beams into the tallest Behemoth, all of which were turned by its wards, and was destroyed by the reply. On the left, the drowned host's own armour, Thureos and Hoplons of a pattern the Forge had not built in a thousand years, came into the fight under Forsworn command, and the Sixth's stalled attack began to come apart. After two hours the attack had clearly failed, and the drowned host was advancing again behind the Behemoths. The Sixth's commanders ordered a withdrawal and sent forward a reserve company of sixteen Thureos to cover it. None of them returned.

Through the night the ground on the Demus Rim was covered with wounded and with tank crews trying to find their own lines, while the drowned host dragged off every damaged tank it could salvage. The Thirty-Seventh's Fourteenth Cohort, which had gone in with two hundred soldiers, returned with eleven. The Sixth Assault Phalanx lost seventy-four tanks on the Demus Rim, twelve of them Colossi, and twenty-nine field guns, and its infantry losses came to thirty-one thousand transfers, four thousand of them null. None of the drowned Behemoths had fallen.

The defeat on the Demus Rim was the worst armoured defeat in the Third's history, and it was the product of decisions made in haste by commanders who had never fought a battle of movement. The Sixth Assault Phalanx had been sent against an enemy whose strength nobody had measured, without reconnaissance, without heavy guns and without any means of fighting the swarms and Lanterns that came down on it from the river cloud. Its tanks had advanced in parade order across open ground because that was how the Third's tanks advanced against a trench line, and against the Behemoths it was the worst formation they could have chosen. Its Gigantomachy lances, the only weapons the Third possessed that had been built to kill a Behemoth, had been committed piecemeal in the first wave and lost in the first hour. The Third's commanders had not underestimated the drowned host so much as failed to estimate it at all. The Sixth had been ordered to meet it, and the Sixth had done what it was told.

The consequences for the Third were immediate. The drowned host now stood on the old second belt behind the Eighth and Ninth Line Phalanxes, with nothing between it and the Ninth's rear, and the Third had no armoured reserve able to stop it. The Behemoths could not be killed with anything on the Flats. The only engines that could meet them were the Empire's own Behemoths, and those walked for the Forge of Hephaestus, which had never sent a cohort of them below Depth 30.

### 6.8 Retreats and Withdrawals (Rotation Eight, Cycles 360 to 380)

After the defeat on the Demus Rim, the Third could no longer hold its ring around the inner belt. The Eighth and Ninth Line Phalanxes were ordered to abandon their positions on the southern face of the inner belt and swing west, and even then they could hardly hope to escape what was advancing behind them. The southern half of the ring was given up. To save the rest of the two Phalanxes, one regiment would have to stay behind as their rearguard. The order amounted to a sentence. The rearguard would be caught against the [[Lethe]] Cut, which could not be crossed, and was to fight to the end as a pocket that would hold the drowned host for as long as it survived while the other regiments turned to meet it. Once cut off it could not be supplied, and the drowned host would overrun it before long, sooner if it brought up the Behemoths.

The Eighth Line Phalanx chose the Fifty-Fourth Trench Regiment. It was the Eighth's freshest regiment, raised from the ration rolls of the [[Lethe Hold]] in Rotation Two when the shells stopped coming, and it had been on the Flats for less than a rotation. Its colonel, [[Corin Vadas]], a Lethe Hold administrator given the regiment when no one else would take it, asked how long he was to hold and was told to hold until relieved. When he asked when relief might be expected, he received no answer.

The Fifty-Second and the Fiftieth left their trenches on the three hundred and sixty-third cycle and withdrew north-west into the positions of the [[Seventh Line Phalanx]]. The Seventh fell back in turn to a new line anchored on the ruins of Ore Head, with its front along the old ore road to the south-east, and the remains of both Assault Phalanxes were placed behind it as the Third's last reserve. Hundreds of thousands of soldiers, with their tanks and guns, were on the move across the Flats at once, digging new lines as fast as they could while the drowned host advanced toward them. The Third's regiments, which had faced a single front for eight rotations, now faced in every direction, and the southern Flats were in pieces. Any chance of taking the Arsenal within the Répertoire's twelve rotations was gone. It would take everything the Empire could send merely to prevent the reduction of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal from ending in defeat.

The withdrawal was carried out with more skill than the battles that had made it necessary. The Third had spent six hundred years learning to dig, and its regiments dug the new line from Ore Head to the Seventh's old positions in eleven cycles under the guns of the inner belt and the raids of the drowned, while the Fifty-Fourth held the Lethe Cut behind them. The Eighth and Ninth Line Phalanxes brought out most of their guns and nearly all their soldiers. They abandoned their stores on the southern Flats, two rotations of shells and rations among them, and the Third's engineers set charges in the dumps before they left. The drowned host came up to the abandoned works on the southern face of the inner belt on the three hundred and seventy-fifth cycle and found them empty. The Archflamen's cohorts came out of the inner belt to meet it, and for the first time since the [[Tabularium (Pyriphlegethon)|Tabularium]] fell, the Arsenal's army and its allies from the river stood together on the Cinder Flats in the open.

The Fifty-Fourth Trench Regiment dug in along the lip of the Lethe Cut as the last of the Eighth's soldiers passed through its lines. Its position was a pocket nine kilometres wide with the Cut at its back, held by soldiers who had been ration clerks and ore-lift hands two rotations before, under officers who had never commanded in battle. It was given the stores the departing regiments could not carry, a battery of divine mortars without keyers and the promise of relief. The drowned host did not attack it. For the rest of Rotation Eight it passed the pocket by on its march north, leaving pickets to watch it and nothing more, and the Fifty-Fourth's reports to the Eighth Line Phalanx recorded only the mist that came up out of the Lethe Cut below its trenches every dark watch, thicker each night than the last. The Eighth logged the reports and did not reply to them. It had nothing to send.

### 6.9 The Command at Watchpoint Hades (Rotation Eight, Cycle 380)

The Command's alarm at the events of Rotation Eight was matched by its confusion about what to do. The Third had been the Empire's anchor at Depth 40 for six hundred years, and in a single rotation it had broken the second belt, lost its fleet, lost its reserve and been driven from half its ring by an enemy nobody in the Command had believed existed. The Pontifical College demanded that the Archflamen be destroyed whatever it cost, since an Archflamen allied to the drowned dead of the Phlegethon was now a matter discussed in every chapel of the theatre. The Directorate demanded an accounting of twenty-three million transfers. The Répertoire reported that the drowned host's advance on the southern Flats, if unchecked, would reach the relay pylons at Landing Shelf Kappa within a rotation, after which the Third could neither be supplied nor reinforced and every soldier it lost would be lost for good. It also reported that the null rate on the Flats had risen to one transfer in eighteen. The Command did not need the Répertoire to tell it what that meant for the Third's soldiers. It asked the Répertoire what would be needed to save the siege, and the Répertoire's answer, delivered on the three hundred and eightieth cycle, was a new commander for the Third, with the Behemoths of the Forge of Hephaestus behind him. The Command began to seek both.

On Quarterdeck Nineteen, the Third's staff counted what remained. The Third had lost the southern half of its ring, its fleet over the Flats and its reserve at Segment Twelve, and its supply now depended on cables it could no longer protect. It still had more than two million soldiers on the Flats, nine months of shells at the rationed rate and a line that held. It had no plan for winning the siege. The Command at Watchpoint Hades had begun to draw one up, and Castorre was not consulted.

## 7 On the Cusp of Defeat

> *"The Third will pay what it costs. Tell me the price, and then stop telling me."*
>
> Marshal Theron Kastellan, on taking command

### 7.1 A New Commander (Rotation Nine, Cycle 1)

News of the Third's perilous situation on the Flats reached the Lord Marshal of the Tartarean Theatre at Watchpoint Hades at the turn of Rotation Nine. From the command deck at Hades she had a view of the whole of the theatre, every hold and every front from Depth 3 to the Cocytus. She commanded more armies than her staff could readily count and held a direct line to the Senate on [[Olympus Primaris]], and it was her staff, together with the Répertoire, that had drawn up the plan for the reduction of the Arsenal and appointed Aurelian Castorre to carry it out. Now it fell to the same staff to rescue it. She had already empowered Logothete Irene Vasska of the Dionysian Logistics Directorate to inquire into the Third's command, and Vasska's first recommendation was that it be replaced. It had failed in its duty and failed the Imperator, squandering shells, nectar and vat time that the Directorate had placed at its disposal in quantities never before given to a single siege.

Vasska's report ran to some four hundred leaves, and its findings were damning. The Third had been given four rotations and the shells for six, and after eight it stood further from the Acropolis than it had at the end of Rotation Three. Castorre had treated the null rate as a technical matter for the Répertoire's officers and had mentioned it to the Command only in the covering notes to his revised timetables, though by Rotation Eight more than one transfer in thirty from the Flats arrived at Watchpoint Hades empty, above the ceiling the Command had set at the start of the siege. He had sent two regiments to [[Wedge (Grid Delta)|the Wedge]] at [[Grid Delta]] without protest and had never pressed for the return of the Thirty-Third. He had given the Third no defence against attack from the air, on the assumption that the ceiling-lances which kept the Fleet from the Flats would also keep the air clear for the Third, and when the drowned fleet rose the Third had nothing with which to answer it. Above all, the report found, the Third's command had gone on planning the reduction of the Arsenal as a siege of forge-thralls and garrison cohorts for five rotations after the Legates of the deep rivers had come up to fight beside them. Vasska did not accuse Castorre of cowardice. She accused him of the more ordinary failing of keeping to a plan after the war it had been written for had ended, and the Lord Marshal accepted her finding.

News of his dismissal made little impression upon Lord-General Castorre. He received it calmly at his terminal in the command ring of Quarterdeck Nineteen, in the full knowledge that a man of his house could not fall far. A post was already waiting for him on the staff of the Second Ceiling Army on Callistus, then mustering against the reformation peaks at Depth 12. It was for those beneath him to bear the weight of the failure. Officers who had not already found posts elsewhere were sent to the Forlorn Cohorts or to field commands on the Cocytus front, where the ice took soldiers and did not return them. For most it amounted to a death sentence, and on the Cocytus the phrase was meant literally. [[Leda Corvis]] was posted to the Cocytus, went down the cables on the twentieth cycle of Rotation Nine, and was not heard of again by the Third.

With the Third's command being replaced, a vacancy opened at its head, and there were powerful voices ready to fill it. On the ceiling world of Olympia Sacra, the Pontifical College declared that the war had failed for lack of faith. The reduction of the Arsenal should be a crusade to free the Katabasion and the bones of the First Descent from the apostate, and since Orrhae had been one of the College's own, it was the College's forces that should bring him to justice. The College could send its temple legions to steady the line on the Flats and avert defeat, but only on condition that it named the Third's commander. Enforcement backed the College. Its Lictor-Superiors were ready to assume command, and it proposed to place the whole of the Third under Enforcement's authority with a Lictor at the head of every regiment. It was a powerful alliance, and the lobbying for control of the Third was ferocious. In the halls of Watchpoint Hades, the Third had become a prize that many wished to hold.

The College's temple legions were a considerable prize in themselves. The College maintained eleven of them on the ceiling worlds, about four hundred thousand soldiers in all, recruited from the faithful of the holds and armed from the College's own foundries, and it had never yet committed one to the Tartarus front. Their soldiers carried implants and were transferred like the Third's own, and their devotion was beyond question. Their training and equipment were another matter, and the Lord Marshal's staff judged them fit to hold a quiet stretch of trench and little more. Their real value was political. A College that had sent its legions to the Flats would have a stake in the outcome of the siege and a voice in its conduct, and the College intended to have both.

The Directorate would hear none of it. The Third must remain under the Directorate's control, since it was the Directorate's supply lines and stores on which the Third depended. If the Directorate could not name the commander, Vasska warned, the priority of the war would have to be reviewed, and whoever took the Third without the Directorate's support might find that shells were scarcer than before and that the vats at Hades had other fronts to fill.

As commander of the theatre, the Lord Marshal held that the appointment was hers to make, whoever petitioned her, and reconciling the parties was a difficult political task. In the end she reserved the right to name the commander in the field and allowed the College and Enforcement to place their own officers on his staff and in his administration. Few were satisfied with the outcome. The new command would be under pressure from its first cycle to transform the campaign, and the College in particular continued to press its case and to withhold its temple legions until its influence was sufficient to justify sending them.

The officer chosen to take up this poisoned cup was [[Theron Kastellan|Marshal Theron Kastellan]] of Pyrrhus Command, a hard-fighting general with a long record of victories. Although now old, he was regarded by the Lord Marshal as a safe pair of hands. Like Castorre he was a patrician, of a house with a long history in the upper ranks of the theatre. Among his large staff was his personal flamen, a sign of the seriousness of his devotion. He was a man of strict routine and fixed ideas about how a war should be fought, and he had the experience to fight this one. He had commanded the Sixty-Third Trench Regiment of the Second Siege Army through the twelve-rotation siege of [[Hold Charybdis]] on the Cocytus, and it was his victory there that recommended him for the Arsenal. He had also served for forty years under Stephanos Pyrrhus. With her letter of appointment, the Lord Marshal gave him a second letter under the Pyrrhus seal, which he did not show his staff. It asked him to discover, if he could, what had become of Stephanos Pyrrhus's daughter at Segment Twelve.

Kastellan's staff reflected the bargain that had produced him. His chief of staff was a Directorate officer seconded from Vasska's own inquiry, whose first duty was to keep the Third's accounts in a form the Directorate could read. The College placed a Flamen-Inquisitor in his headquarters with authority over every chaplain on the Flats, and Enforcement a Lictor-Superior with a cohort of Lictors and the right to try any soldier of the Third for heresy. Neither had any authority over the conduct of operations, and both reported to their own masters on Olympia Sacra by every courier. Kastellan treated them with formal courtesy and told them nothing he had not already decided.

His first orders on taking command at Quarterdeck Nineteen on the forty-first cycle of Rotation Nine were defensive. All attacks along the arc were cancelled, and the Ordnance Phalanxes were ordered to conserve their shells for counter-battery work and the breaking up of enemy attacks. Every regiment was required to report its nulls separately from its transfers at every watch, the first time the Third's command had asked for the figure, and the Répertoire was instructed to map them leaf by leaf. The map it produced showed the nulls concentrated in two places. One was a ring about the Acropolis within the reach of the Echo Mast. The other lay in the south, where the grey had begun to spread off the Lethe Cut. Kastellan did not yet understand the first. He had already seen enough of the second.

Kastellan's first task, once his staff was assembled, was to gather a force to relieve the Third. He needed to break the drowned fleet's hold on the low geometry, put fresh soldiers and supplies onto the Flats, and reopen the supply lines on which the Third's eventual victory depended. His appointment marked a change in the Empire's approach to the Arsenal. In his view the siege had already lasted far too long, and it was time to commit enough strength to finish it. The Third had done well in wearing the Archflamen down, and fresh forces and a fresh approach would now break him.

He went first to the [[Forge of Hephaestus]], which had a long association with the Third and had built most of its tanks and all of its Phaethons. The drowned Behemoths would have to be met and destroyed, and only Behemoths could do it. The lords of the Forge agreed to send the [[Forge Cohort Talos]], the oldest of the Forge's walking cohorts. Its engines were made ready in the depths of the Forge, the rites were held and the cradle-crews briefed and blessed, and the feet of every Behemoth were anointed with [[Ichor|ichor]] from the Forge's altars as a sign that the engines would lay the river's dead at the Imperator's feet. With the rites complete, the Behemoths were embarked in their forge-lighters for Tartarus. The Third also needed soldiers in greater numbers than ever before, above all sappers, and the drafts of the ceiling worlds were called upon again. The Forge sent nine regiments of Hephaestean sappers with the Behemoths, engineers trained to dig beneath fortresses since before the Empire had a name for them, and further soldiers, guns and tanks were embarked for Depth 40.

The Tartarus Fleet would provide the ships and escort for the relief convoy, and placed two [[Wrath-Pattern Strike Wing|Wrath-pattern strike wings]] at the Third's disposal, one of interceptors and one of bombers, with Lantern escorts. They would contest the ceiling geometry over the Flats that the drowned had taken, and give the Third a long-range striking arm it had never possessed. The slow war of attrition was becoming total war, fought with almost every means the theatre could bring to bear.

In all, the relief force assembled for the Arsenal over the first three hundred cycles of Rotation Nine comprised the twenty-two engines of the Forge Cohort Talos with their cradle-crews and the Hephaestean servicing companies that maintained them; nine regiments of Hephaestean sappers, about one hundred and eight thousand soldiers, with forty boring engines; eleven new Trench Regiments raised from the drafts of the ceiling worlds, about one million, eight hundred thousand soldiers, to refill the Line Phalanxes; two armoured regiments of Thureos tanks and a company of Colossi for the [[Fourth Assault Phalanx]]; four ordnance regiments, two of them divine batteries keyed by children of Apollo and [[Ares]] drawn from the holds of the Fire Steps; the two Wrath wings of the Tartarus Fleet; and nine hundred thousand tonnes of shells, nectar and stores. It was the largest single convoy sent to the Tartarean theatre in a century, and the Directorate let it be known on the ceiling worlds that it expected a return on it.

### 7.2 The Last Stand of the Fifty-Fourth (Rotation Nine, Cycles 1 to 15)

None of these preparations could help the Fifty-Fourth Trench Regiment. Left on the edge of the Lethe Cut as the rearguard of the Eighth and Ninth Line Phalanxes, it was surrounded, with its back to a drop of seven kilometres into the grey mist at the bottom of the Cut. Its sacrifice had saved the rest of the two Phalanxes, but that was little consolation to the Lethe Hold drafts in its trenches. The instrument of its destruction came up out of the Cut. The [[Unremembered]] had come up the Lethe when the Echo Mast called, travelling beneath the grey mist from the confluence of the Lethe and the Phlegethon at Depth 43, and had waited since the rising of the drowned fleet for the Archflamen's war to give them the ground they wanted. They were the Lethe's own: soldiers of the Empire and of the older wars before it who had drunk from the Lethe, or been made to drink, and had forgotten who they were, and had then been given something else by the river to be instead. Their Legate called herself [[Ysander Pale]], and she had used no other name for nine hundred years. The southern Flats, where the dead lay unburied in the craters and the ground was soured by eight rotations of shellfire, were the kind of battlefield they favoured, and with the Fifty-Fourth trapped at the edge of their river, they gave it their full attention.

The Third's commanders had suspected since the first rotation that the Arsenal held proscribed weapons. For six thousand years its vaults had contained a store of distillates that the Empire had forbidden and never managed to destroy, and these had passed to the Archflamen when the Tabularium fell. Chief among them was a distillate of the Lethe itself, the seventh of the nine grades refined from the river by the old Hephaestean chemists before the Senate put a stop to their work, which the Arsenal's inventory listed as [[Grey Seventh]]. It was a heavy grey vapour that clung to the ground and passed through skin, eyes and respirator seals alike, as the Lethe passed through a hull. A soldier exposed to it forgot, the last hour first and then the last day, and then his name and his training, in about the time it took to recite the motto of the Third. Those who received a light dose recovered within hours with a gap in their memory. Those who received a heavy dose did not recover. They rose from their trenches and wandered, and if they were killed while empty, their implants uploaded nothing, so that the transfer arrived at Watchpoint Hades as a body's worth of absence. Until now the Archflamen had kept his Grey Seventh in the vaults as his last weapon, for the day when all seemed lost.

The coming of the drowned had made the Archflamen's army stronger and the Archflamen himself weaker. Many of his new allies had no intention of obeying him. The Cinderborn and the Unremembered had come for reasons of their own, and where those reasons diverged from his, they ignored him. He had no hold on the warbands that now moved where they pleased across the Flats. Even Hesper had little influence over them and made little attempt to use it, since to her the Archflamen's revolt was only an opportunity to do the work of something larger. The allies had all come to destroy the Third, and each had its own idea of how it should be done. Orrhae was shut away in the Katabasion, under the Phaethon fire every night, receiving reports from his councillors, chief among them Dace, who now commanded the Sworn, and he had little understanding of what was happening beyond the Wall.

Unlike the Cinderborn, the Unremembered had not plunged straight into battle on their arrival. They had gathered their strength and chosen their first victims with care, and they chose the Fifty-Fourth. What they intended was an experiment, a rite and a preparing of the ground, fought for no strategic purpose. They took the Grey Seventh from the vaults by force, from under the guard of the Sworn, and the Archflamen did not prevent them.

The first attack came at the change of the first watch on the eleventh cycle of Rotation Nine, without a barrage. The Fifty-Fourth's sentries saw a pale light staining the mist in the Cut below them, and the mist began to climb the face of the Cut, thick and heavy, until it came over the lip, lay down on the ash, filled every crater and entered the trenches. The Fifty-Fourth had been trained, like every regiment of the Third, to fight through a gas attack, and its respirators and seals were of no use against Grey Seventh. Where the mist was thin, soldiers sat down in the fire bays and recovered an hour later with no memory of the cycle. Where it was thick, it took everything, and whole maniples put down their rifles, removed their helmets or climbed out over the wire into no-man's land and wandered among the craters. The first assault came out of the mist while they stood in it.

It was led by engines out of the grey, drowned Hoplons and Thureos and assault carriers of a kind the Forge had not built in a thousand years, their hulls rotted black with Lethe-water, which crossed no-man's land against almost no resistance and lowered their ramps to release the Unremembered. Their armour had rotted on them over the centuries, and the river had grown into the gaps like fungus on wet timber. They had forgotten their faces, and the river had not troubled to restore them. They had not forgotten how to fight. They went into the Fifty-Fourth's trenches with the discipline of the old legions, sweeping the fire bays clear and throwing grenades of Grey Seventh down the dugout stairs, and in close combat their long grey blades went through the Fifty-Fourth's armour while the Fifty-Fourth's bayonets turned on theirs. The Fifty-Fourth stood and fought to the last. No regiment of the Third could have withstood Grey Seventh and the Unremembered together any better, and it could not withstand them for long. In desperation its centurions called down their own guns on their own trenches, and the shells fell on both sides alike. As the mist thinned, a second wave of thralls and Sworn came across no-man's land through the counter-barrage in the wake of the Unremembered, entered the trenches, drove the survivors back along the communication trenches and took the support line.

No help could be sent, and with nowhere to withdraw to, the Fifty-Fourth had no choice but to make its last stand. Its trenches fell one by one, its field guns were abandoned as their ammunition ran out, and its last tanks fought hull down from their pits until the drowned carriers drove over the trenches and collapsed the walls upon whoever was beneath, and the tanks were knocked out one after another.

### 7.3 The Toxic War (Rotation Nine, Cycles 11 to 15)

The fighting continued through the night. Colonel Vadas reported to the Eighth Line Phalanx that he could hold for one more cycle, after which his ammunition would be exhausted, his last guns overrun and his regiment scattered. His soldiers held for three more cycles, to the last of them and the last round, as the line contracted toward the lip of the Cut. The Unremembered released Grey Seventh upon them again and again. They did not take the Fifty-Fourth's dead away but gathered them into heaps in the craters and left them in the mist, and the soldiers who had breathed the vapour and lived they did not kill at all, leaving them to wander the southern Flats among the dead. All contact with the Fifty-Fourth ended at the fourth bell of the fifteenth cycle, when its last pocket was broken.

The Unremembered left behind them a charnel ground, with the grey still lying in the bottoms of the trenches and the deepest craters. Leaves 61-24 and 62-24 had been made into ground that belonged to the Lethe, and creatures out of the river could be seen moving in its trenches and dugouts, among which the empty soldiers of the Fifty-Fourth walked unharmed. The regiment had entered the pocket with one hundred and forty-one thousand soldiers. The Répertoire logged one hundred and nine thousand as transferred during the four cycles of the stand, and every one of them arrived at Watchpoint Hades null. The remaining thirty-two thousand, the empty, were never transferred, and since their implants still reported a heartbeat the Répertoire could not list them as dead. It entered them as absent. It was the heaviest loss of the siege in a single action, and the first occasion in the war on which a regiment was lost entire without a single soldier returning.

The Fifty-Fourth had been raised from the ration rolls of the Lethe Hold, and its loss fell upon the Hold with particular weight. [[Commander Thaleia]] of Lethe Hold, who had signed the regiment's draft orders in Rotation Two and had known Vadas when he ran the Hold's ration board, received the Répertoire's list three hours after it was issued, with NULL entered against one hundred and nine thousand names and ABSENT against thirty-two thousand more. She ordered that every family be informed, including those of the absent, who were to be given that word and no other. She entered the loss on the Hold's register under [[CODE 44]], the code for a regiment lost entire with no return, which had not been used in her time.

### 7.4 The Grey

For the Unremembered, the Fifty-Fourth had been a test of Grey Seventh, and the test had succeeded. From then on they used it wherever they went, and the Third's soldiers learned that a grey light in the craters meant an attack within the hour. Wherever the Unremembered passed, they left the ground grey behind them and spread their river further into the Flats. This was deliberate. The Unremembered had no interest in the Archflamen's war of attrition and were indifferent to whether the Arsenal stood or fell. Their purpose was the Lethe's, which was for the Flats to forget that they had ever been held by anyone, and they were preparing the southern Flats as ground fit for the things in the river that had not yet come up. It was a rite, carried out with care, that made way for something worse, and it bore no resemblance to war as the Third's commanders understood it. The Third's soldiers called the southern Flats the Grey thereafter. The trench maps of the Arsenal campaign show the Grey in a pale wash from the Lethe Cut almost to the Demus Rim, spreading from map to map and rotation by rotation.

The Third had no defence against Grey Seventh, and its attempts to devise one in the rotations that followed met with little success. The quartermasters issued sealed hoods of the pattern worn against Lethe seep in the holds, which delayed the vapour by about a minute. The Apollonian physicians at Watchpoint Hades tried draughts of nectar taken before an attack, which allowed some soldiers who had breathed the vapour to recover more of what they had lost, though never all of it. The only measure that worked reliably was distance. Regiments on the edge of the Grey were ordered to keep their forward trenches thinly manned and to hold their soldiers in the support line until an attack had shown itself, and the Unremembered's attacks thereafter fell on emptier trenches. The cost of the method was ground, since a thinly held trench was easily taken, and the Third gave up four kilometres of the southern Flats in the rotation after the Fifty-Fourth was lost.

### 7.5 The Second Battle of the Low Geometry (Rotation Nine, Cycles 30 to 300)

While the command was being changed, the Third's regiments still had to face the enemy before them and keep their soldiers and stores alive while they did so. All attacks were halted, and the object of the siege was set aside for the object of survival. The Archflamen and his allies now held the initiative, and with the drowned behind them they attacked in greater strength than ever before. The Third's guns were rationed to a few rounds per gun per watch and could no longer break up attacks before they arrived, and many of the attacks took and held the Third's trenches. Salients were driven deep into the Third's line, and the Third was pushed back a metre at a time from ground that had cost it millions of transfers. For almost a rotation it suffered nothing but defeats and withdrawals, and as its soldiers and stores ran down it appeared to be on the edge of losing the war.

Kastellan worked without pause, and on the three hundredth cycle of Rotation Nine his convoy was ready to descend to Depth 40, with its transports, supply ships and the forge-lighters of the Forge Cohort Talos under strong escort. The Fleet had not forgotten the first battle of the low geometry, and the drowned [[BATTLESHIPS|battleship]] could not be ignored. Its first move was to send the three Lantern destroyers of Picket Ithaca down through the ceiling cracks to locate the drowned fleet. They found the wrecks of the first battle drifting in the low geometry, the Themis Unbowed and the Long Labour among them, but met no enemy, and reported all quiet. No one knew where the drowned fleet or the [[Eye of Light]] had gone, and an attack had to be expected. The Fleet assigned the twin heavy cruisers [[Lysander's Oath]] and [[Kallisto]] and a full squadron of eight Lantern frigates to the convoy, which descended in good order with the transports at its centre, the Lysander's Oath leading and the Kallisto in the rear.

Most of the drowned fleet had in fact returned to the river after its victory. The Eye of Light had not. She had lain for most of a rotation among the wrecks of the first battle, with her reactors banked and her drowned crew at their stations, giving off nothing the Fleet's augurs could read. The Forsworn had served aboard the Empire's ships for six hundred years and knew what a convoy escort looked for. As the convoy passed over the wrecks, she struck. Her first broadside destroyed two of the Lantern frigates and her second disabled the Lysander's Oath's starboard thrusters and steering, while a transport loaded with Phaethon shells was crippled and left drifting. The cruisers replied, the Kallisto turning to fire a wide spread of torpedoes and closing on the battleship, and while they fought, the transports made for the cables at Kappa, with the remaining frigates placing themselves between the cargo and the Eye of Light's guns at the cost of two more ships crippled.

At close range, even a battleship could not ignore two heavy cruisers. The Eye of Light took the Kallisto's torpedoes as she turned her guns on them, and fires broke out within her along the old wound in her hull, where Cocytus ice had been packed. Her batteries left the Lysander's Oath burning and venting atmosphere, but the Kallisto held her course and tore open the battleship's flank below the burned face of the god. With her prey escaping to the cables and her own damage mounting, the Eye of Light broke off and withdrew among the wrecks over the spur with two drowned escorts. The Kallisto, alone, did not pursue. The Lysander's Oath was beyond saving, and her captain ordered her abandoned and left the fires on her lower decks to burn her out, which took several weeks. Her surviving crew crossed to the Kallisto, which continued down to Kappa, and the low geometry over the Flats acquired one more wreck. Four escorts had been lost and hundreds of the Lysander's Oath's crew killed, but the convoy had got through. The two cruisers had saved it, and perhaps the siege.

The battle confirmed what the first had suggested, that the low geometry over the Flats could no longer be treated as safe water. The Fleet responded by placing a standing squadron over Kappa, two heavy cruisers and their escorts on permanent station at the edge of the lance envelope, and by routing every later convoy down the cables under its guns. It was an expensive commitment. The Tartarus Fleet had other charges in the deep, and every cruiser held over the Flats was one fewer on the [[Acheron]] stairs and the Fire Steps. The Fleet's admirals accepted it with reluctance and reminded Watchpoint Hades of it at every [[Conclave at Watchpoint Hades|Conclave]] thereafter. The Eye of Light was not seen again for most of a rotation, and the Fleet's officers argued over whether the Kallisto's torpedoes had finished her. They had not.

### 7.6 Planetfall (Rotation Nine, Cycles 300 to 330)

Most of the convoy's supplies reached the Flats, and all of the Behemoths. Soldiers came down the cables at Kappa by the thousand, tanks by the hundred and shells by the thousand tonnes, and everything was sent forward to the front at once. Among the cargo landers, one of the forge-lighters settled on its own cables onto the Flats at the foot of the [[Cinder Stair]] and released the Behemoths of the Forge Cohort Talos under the oldest cradle-master of the Forge, First [[Doros Varne|Cradle-Lord Doros Varne]]. There were twenty-two of them in honour banners bearing the eagle of the Empire and the bronze hammer of Talos: ten Brontes-pattern engines eighty metres tall and twelve lighter, faster Steropes-pattern hunters. The Forge Cohort had come to meet the drowned Behemoths and take up a war that the Forge had fought against the river for as long as there had been Behemoths, since the first engine went down into the Phlegethon and came back walking for something else. Against orders, the Third's soldiers came up out of their trenches along the line to watch them pass, and no one prevented them. For many soldiers who had been on the Flats for nine rotations, it was the first time they believed the Third might win.

The convoy's arrival transformed the Third's position. Within thirty cycles the Line Phalanxes had been brought back to strength, the gun lines had been restocked to a level they had not held since [[The Saturation|the Saturation]], and the Ordnance Phalanxes were permitted to fire freely for the first time since the drowned had cut the convoys. The new soldiers were posted to the Line Phalanxes by cohort rather than as whole regiments, so that every cohort on the line received its share of drafts alongside its veterans, a practice the Third had abandoned in its first rotation for administrative convenience and Kastellan restored on his own authority. The [[Quiet Ones]] laid a second rail line from the foot of the Stair to the southern gun lines, and the Hephaestean servicing companies built a cradle-yard for the Forge Cohort on the old outer belt below [[The Drum|the Drum]], where the Behemoths would be rearmed and repaired for the rest of the siege.

### 7.7 Back from the Brink (The Kastellan Offensive, Rotation Nine, Cycle 330 to Rotation Ten, Cycle 240)

Even while the Fifty-Fourth was dying on the Lethe Cut, Kastellan had been planning the Third's next attempt on the inner belt. After a long period of bitter defence, the Third was being refilled and resupplied every cycle, and new attacks were planned for every regiment along the whole front. The Third would no longer simply hold its trenches. The preparations were lengthy. Every regiment had to absorb new soldiers, arm them and post them to the cohorts. The stocks of shells, fuel and everything else had to be built up, cohorts and batteries had to be moved into position, and the colonels worked through every watch on their attack plans, fire plans and reserves. The Kastellan Offensive was set to begin on the two hundred and fortieth cycle of Rotation Ten. Its aim was to recover all the ground lost since Gallows Rise and to break the inner belt so that the Third could reach the Cinder Wall. It was an ambitious plan, and it took most of a rotation to make the battered regiments ready.

Kastellan's most important decision was where to employ the Behemoths. The lines of command did not permit him to give orders to Cradle-Lord Varne, and the deployment of the Forge Cohort depended on his reports to the lords of the Forge. After careful review, all twenty-two engines were committed to a main effort on the front of the Seventh Line Phalanx, alongside its four regiments: the [[Twenty-Second Trench Regiment|Twenty-Second]], the first regiment of the Third to descend the Cinder Stair; the Thirty-Seventh, which had made the first assault on the outer belt; the Forty-Ninth; and the Twenty-Ninth. The Twenty-Ninth had been disbanded after the Big Push and its colours burned. Kastellan raised it again, recalling its eleven thousand surviving soldiers from the Forlorn Cohorts on the Cocytus, giving them new colours and a new colonel and filling the regiment from the drafts. He informed its officers that the Twenty-Ninth had a debt on Leaf 50-27 and that he expected them to pay it. The Fourth Assault Phalanx would support the Seventh with its Colossi.

The first phase of the plan was a prolonged bombardment. The new shells went first to the Ordnance Phalanxes and the mortar batteries, and with the low geometry now held by the Wrath wings and convoys reaching Kappa every week, millions of shells were carried forward and stacked at the gun lines. The barrage built slowly over the weeks before the attack, harassing the Archflamen's lines and making it dangerous to bring anything forward to them, and in the final cycles it rose until the guns were firing without pause, their barrels glowing and their breech blocks and recoil pistons wearing out under the strain.

On the two hundred and fortieth cycle of Rotation Ten, the guns fell silent and the assault cohorts went over the top along the arc. On Leaf 54-45, Colonel [[Imre Saldane]] of the Twenty-Second Trench Regiment went over with his soldiers, beside the regimental standard that had come down the Cinder Stair in Rotation Zero. Behind the infantry advanced the Thureos and the Colossi, while the mortar batteries laid smoke and [[High-Explosive (HE)|high explosive]] to keep the defenders' heads down, and behind them all the Forge Cohort Talos walked into the battle, led by Varne aboard the Brontes-pattern [[Anvil of Talos]], to which he was joined through the old Hephaestean cradle links. For the first hours the battle appeared to be following the familiar pattern of heavy losses for small gains, but on the front of the Seventh Line Phalanx the attack went well. Nothing in front of the Behemoths stopped them. Their Aegis-wards held against the Archflamen's fire while their own weapons smashed a road through the inner belt, and when the Archflamen's cohorts counter-attacked on Leaf 54-45 with tanks and infantry, they could not halt Varne's advance. The Wrath interceptors came over low to add their bombs and cannon to the fire falling on the inner belt. The forward trenches were overrun, and the Third's cohorts and breachers pressed on into the maze of trenches and dugouts behind them.

By the end of the first cycle the Twenty-Ninth had retaken Leaf 50-27 and recovered the remains of the thirty-one breachers from the wire before Ceiling-Lance Seven, which it carried back across no-man's land itself. Every regiment that had fought alongside the Behemoths reported good progress. Across the rest of the Flats, results were mixed. Some regiments had been stopped by the defensive fire or thrown back across no-man's land by heavy counter-attacks, and wherever the Cinderborn, the Forsworn or the Unremembered entered the fight, the Third fared badly. On the edge of the Grey, the Fiftieth Trench Regiment was struck with Grey Seventh and lost heavily, all of its transfers null, and requested permission to reply in kind. The Third had captured stocks of the Arsenal's own Grey Seventh shells in the outer stores in Rotation Three, and they had been held in a sealed magazine behind [[Ore Head]] ever since. Against the advice of much of his staff, Kastellan granted permission, and the Fiftieth's guns were soon soaking the Archflamen's lines on the edge of the Grey in the same vapour. The Fiftieth's soldiers watched the Archflamen's thralls rise empty from their trenches and wander, and did not suspect that every shell they fired was doing the Lethe's work by putting more of the river into the ground.

Elsewhere on the arc the offensive went less well. In the north, the Second and Fifth Line Phalanxes attacked the inner belt along the edge of the Mora Cut without the support of the Behemoths and gained between four hundred and nine hundred metres on most of their leaves, at a cost of six hundred and seventy thousand transfers over five cycles. The [[Ninth Line Phalanx]], opposite the Demus Rim, made diversionary attacks as its orders required and lost a further eighty thousand. The Eighth Line Phalanx was still being rebuilt after the Lethe Cut and took no part in the offensive.

The second cycle opened with further strikes from the air, but this time the drowned contested them, and the Wrath interceptors fought the [[Stymphalian Swarm|Stymphalian]] swarms high over the Flats beneath the ceiling. On Leaf 57-48, two waves of Wrath bombers struck the trenches of the inner belt, and with that support the Fifty-Second Trench Regiment under Colonel Lowe gained more ground in a cycle than it had in a rotation, a result that persuaded Kastellan to ask the Fleet for more bombers. As the Behemoths advanced again under a seep-storm, the Anvil of Talos destroyed a column of eleven Arsenal tanks moving up as reinforcements within a single minute, and its augurs then detected the drowned Behemoths approaching. The Archflamen's allies had answered the Forge Cohort with twelve engines of their own, drawn across from the Mora Cut and up from the Demus Rim. Varne made them his first targets, and while the infantry battle continued at their feet, the Behemoths of Talos and the drowned Behemoths of the river fought their own war, evenly matched, their wards flaring and their reactors straining under the demands of their weapons.

The first to fall was the Brontes-pattern Steadfast. Raked by the drowned hammer-gun the cradle-crews called the Smith's Laughter, its last ward failed, and before it could be raised again a Phlegethon-cannon put a jet of river-fire through its command deck and killed the whole cradle-crew within seconds. With its higher mind burned out, the Steadfast stopped where it stood with its guns raised and silent, and it remained standing for the rest of the war, the tallest structure on the Cinder Flats outside the Acropolis. At the Forge of Hephaestus a bell was rung for it, and would be rung again for each of its brothers that fell at the Arsenal. The duel of the engines continued throughout the cycle, and without the Behemoths' fire the infantry advance slowed. Little further ground was taken on the second cycle, and the fighting resumed on the third and fourth.

By the fifth cycle both sides had lost heavily. Of all the regiments in the offensive, the Twenty-Second had gone furthest, driving a salient into the inner belt on Leaf 54-45, and Varne resolved to throw his weight there, either to force a breach or to draw the drowned engines onto his own and allow another regiment to break through elsewhere. Rearmed and patched overnight by the Hephaestean crews that accompanied the Forge Cohort, the remaining Behemoths advanced among the Thureos and their escorting infantry through the Archflamen's first barrages. After five cycles of fighting the Archflamen's line was thin. The drowned Behemoths had also lost engines and alone had been holding the line together, and now, outnumbered and scarred, they fell back to regroup. The Archflamen's cohorts could not stand against the Forge Cohort alone. Their one success was a Steropes brought down by concentrated divine mortar fire. Some of the thralls fought with fanatical determination, while others broke under what the Third's soldiers called Behemoth-shock and fled. The Twenty-Second advanced, the tanks and outriders of the Fourth Assault Phalanx joined the pursuit, and one more cycle of fighting saw the inner belt breached and fresh tank columns pouring through the gap. The Kastellan Offensive had broken the inner belt, and the Behemoths had won the battle for it.

This time, however, the Archflamen's army did not abandon its works when the belt broke, as it had at the outer and second belts. Over the rotations of the siege, the ground between the inner belt and the Cinder Wall had been covered with trenches, pillboxes and gun pits, lacking the deep shelters and careful siting of the inner belt but offering ample positions from which to contest every metre. The Archflamen's commanders knew they could not afford to fall back to the Wall and dig in again, since close to the spur any further withdrawal would leave them nowhere to go. There was therefore no clean breakthrough and no rapid advance, only a slow crawl forward against attacks and counter-attacks every cycle. On Leaf 54-45 the Archflamen's strength was spent, and the Twenty-Second made further large gains before it too had to halt to avoid outrunning its supplies and guns. The Behemoths were withdrawn into reserve for repair. They had made the difference, at a cost of four Brontes and seven Steropes that would have to be replaced from the Forge, and the Third's staff credited them with six drowned Behemoths destroyed.

The battle for the ground before the Cinder Wall was far from over, and Kastellan was already preparing the next phase of his offensive, but a long step had been taken toward victory. The Archflamen had lost heavily, and while the Third's replacements would keep coming down the cables, his could not. Kastellan understood that a siege of attrition always favoured a besieger who was properly supplied. For the first time in four rotations, the Third could say that it was winning the reduction of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal.

The Répertoire's figures for the offensive bore out that judgement. Transfers had been lower than in any previous offensive of the siege, and so had nulls, except on the Fiftieth's front on the edge of the Grey, where they ran at forty percent. On the Répertoire's map of the Flats, the pale wash of the Grey had spread a further eleven kilometres north since the Fiftieth began firing. Kastellan asked his staff for everything the Third held on Grey Seventh. They could give him only the Arsenal's old inventory, which recorded that the distillate was proscribed and nothing more, since no chemist in the Third knew what it was.

Kastellan's decision to fire the Arsenal's Grey Seventh back at the Archflamen was the most criticised of his command. The College's Flamen-Inquisitor protested it to Olympia Sacra within the cycle as the use of a proscribed weapon by an army of the Imperator, and Enforcement's Lictor-Superior joined the protest, though neither proposed any other remedy for the Fiftieth. Kastellan defended the decision on the grounds that the regiment had been losing every soldier it sent forward without return, and that the vapour was already on the field. On the evidence available to him the defence was reasonable, and the Lord Marshal accepted it. The ground the Fiftieth's shells had soured remained the Lethe's for the rest of the siege.

## 8 The War Beneath, and the War Above

> *"Under the ground there are no relays. Dig as if you meant to stay."*
>
> Saying of the Hephaestean sapper regiments

### 8.1 The Plan under the Ground (Rotation Ten, Cycle 250)

The inner belt had been broken four rotations later than the Répertoire's first plan had allowed, at a cost the Répertoire was still counting. Marshal Kastellan used the breach to open a new phase of the siege, as he had intended from the day he took command. The Forge of Hephaestus had sent him nine regiments of Hephaestean sappers, and with them he meant to open a second front beneath the Cinder Flats. His plan was to drive galleries under the Archflamen's works, so that the Third's soldiers could come up behind or beneath strongpoints where their guns did not bear. By opening a front the Archflamen could not see, Kastellan hoped to take the strongest of his positions by surprise and win quick victories against them. He also judged that the Archflamen lacked the strength to match him underground and would be stretched by the attempt until he broke. If necessary, the sappers would dig beneath the Cinder Wall and the cone itself, under the ceiling-lances and the wards of the Acropolis, and come up inside. Kastellan expected the Archflamen to contest every metre of it, and counted on the Third's greater numbers and deeper stores to let him dig wherever he chose.

The war on the surface would continue as before, wearing down a defending army that could not replace its losses. It was beneath the ground, however, that Kastellan believed the Third held its greatest advantage, and where the reduction of the Arsenal would be decided.

The plan was not universally welcomed. The Third had never fought underground on any scale, and its colonels distrusted a form of war in which their soldiers would be out of reach of the relays and therefore of the vats. The Répertoire objected on its own terms. A soldier who died ninety metres down was a permanent loss, so a gallery could not be priced in transfers as a trench was priced, and a sapper lost below ground had to be found again from the Forge's rolls. Kastellan overruled both objections. He argued that the Third's losses on the surface were approaching the point at which a transfer and a null amounted to much the same thing, since a soldier who had gone up eight or nine times came back too slowed to fight, and that a mine which took a strongpoint for a few hundred sappers was cheaper in the end than a frontal assault which took it for a hundred thousand transfers and four thousand nulls. The Lord Marshal accepted his reasoning, and the Directorate, which had paid for the sappers, accepted it as well.

The nine regiments sent by the Forge were raised from the sapper establishments of the Forge's own holds and numbered about one hundred and eight thousand soldiers, organised in companies of six hundred. Each regiment answered to a Sapper-Prefect of the Forge, who took his orders from the Forge's commanders and only through them from Kastellan, an arrangement that preserved the Forge's control over its soldiers and caused some difficulty in practice. With them came forty boring engines, tracked machines able to cut a gallery three metres wide through basalt at forty metres a cycle, and the Forge's listening companies, whose geophone augurs were the most sensitive the Empire possessed. Kastellan placed sapper regiments behind every Line Phalanx on the inner belt, gave three to the Seventh Line Phalanx, where the main effort would fall, and held two in reserve under his own hand.

### 8.2 The Ground

The sapper commanders began with a survey of every regiment's sector. Small teams went forward into the front trenches and drilled deep bore-holes to establish how deep each layer of rock ran and whether it could be mined. The bore-holes found water, which the quartermasters distilled, and heat, which was a more serious matter. The tunnels would serve two purposes. Shallow saps would allow the Third's soldiers to move under cover for surprise attacks, while deep galleries would run beneath the Archflamen's works to end in mine chambers, packed with explosive and fired from below before an assault went in on the surface. On the two hundred and fiftieth cycle of Rotation Ten, the Hephaestean sappers went into the forward trenches with the Line Phalanxes and began to map the ground.

The Cinder Flats were poor ground for mining. The top thirty metres were cinder and ash over pumice, easy to trench and hopeless to tunnel, since the cinder ran and a gallery cut through it filled itself as fast as it was dug. The seep-storms drained straight down through it, so that a tunnel in the cinder became a stream within a cycle, and the wet cinder swelled and pressed upon the bracing from every side without warning. Below it the rock hardened into old lava, through which the seep had cut channels and caves running down toward the Phlegethon, and where the river's heat had entered the basalt there ran the same heat veins that had boiled the Third's trenches on the surface. This layer could be mined slowly, but every gallery cut in it risked breaking into a seep-river or a heat vein. From about eighty metres down lay the basalt of the spur itself, hard, dry and hot. Galleries cut there could be hewn with picks and needed little bracing, though progress was slow, the air was poor, and the old dykes of the shelf, black glass harder than the surrounding basalt, had to be blasted through at the risk of being heard on the surface.

The sappers had one advantage their enemies had not anticipated. The inner belt and much of the ground beneath it had been dug by the Third six centuries before, and the Third's archive on Quarterdeck Nineteen still held the plans of every gallery and shelter it had cut there, together with the surveys its engineers had made when the Arsenal was the Third's anchor against the Phlegethon. The plans were old, and the Archflamen's thralls had altered much of what they showed, but they gave the Hephaestean sappers a fuller map of the rock beneath an enemy's works than they had possessed in any earlier siege. The Arsenal's own plans of its outer vaults, held at Watchpoint Hades among the Répertoire's records of the forge-hold, supplied the rest.

At eighty metres, there was also nothing that could carry an upload.

### 8.3 The Deep List

A soldier's implant had seven seconds after death to reach a relay. In a trench on the surface a relay pylon was never more than a few hundred metres away, and seven seconds was ample. The implant's signal passed badly through twenty metres of basalt and not at all through eighty. The Hephaestean sappers had always known this. Sappers who died deep did not return, and the Forge's sapper regiments were raised from volunteers who accepted the fact, paid three times a trench soldier's rate, and buried their own dead underground where they fell. The Third's own small sapper establishment had known it for six hundred years. The Third's trench soldiers had never needed to fight underground, and had never considered it.

The Third's signallers attempted to run relays down the galleries behind the sappers. A relay in a gallery required a shielded line to the next relay and so on to the surface, as well as power, and the trench relays the Third possessed failed in the heat or were cut by collapses and counter-mines, leaving every soldier beyond the break without a relay. Nobody on the Flats had the craft to build a better one. The war beneath was therefore fought by soldiers who knew that death in it was final. The Répertoire kept the losses below ground on a separate list, which the sappers called the [[Deep List]]. By the end of the siege it held two hundred and twenty thousand names.

The Deep List changed the character of the Third's war in ways its commanders had not foreseen. For nine rotations the soldiers of the Third had fought in the knowledge that almost every death was temporary, and much of the Third's method, from the walk across no-man's land to the acceptance of the Répertoire's prices, rested upon that knowledge. Beneath the Flats it did not apply. The Hephaestean sappers accepted the condition as part of their trade. The trench soldiers later drafted into the galleries to carry spoil and fight off counter-mines did not, and the Third's provosts recorded refusals to go below ground in numbers they had never recorded for the walk over the parapet. Kastellan ordered that no trench soldier be sent below except as a volunteer, and was surprised to find volunteers coming forward in large numbers from among the [[Old Ladder|Old Ladders]], who had been up so many times that a final death held fewer terrors for them than another return through the vats.

The Forge treated its own losses on the Deep List as it had always treated them. Each sapper killed below ground was entered on the roll of the Forge's sapper hall with the name of the gallery where he fell, and his pay for the remainder of his engagement went to whoever he had named to receive it. The Third had no equivalent provision for its trench volunteers, whose deaths below ground were entered by the Répertoire as nulls like any other, and whose families received the same notice as those of soldiers lost to the Echo Mast. Kastellan petitioned the Directorate to extend the Forge's terms to them. The Directorate agreed in Rotation Twelve, after the petition had been before it for a rotation.

### 8.4 The Enemy Underground

The Archflamen did not lack miners of his own. Every vault of the Arsenal had been dug by thralls, the thrall gangs had been sinking heat-wells for eight thousand years, and thousands of the Archflamen's soldiers knew how to cut, brace and listen, with the Arsenal's own cutters and borers to do it with. He also had the Frost Masons of the [[Cocytus]], who had been building and breaking fortresses beneath the ice for longer than the Empire had held the Cocytus front and carried no implants to lose. They took command of the Archflamen's counter-mining as soon as they understood what the Third intended, and they were more skilled at it than any enemy the Third had fought. Once the Archflamen knew that galleries were coming, the Third could expect every one of them to be met.

The war beneath also shifted the balance of power inside the Arsenal. Until the Third went underground, the Archflamen's defence had been directed by [[Galba Ostorian|Prefect Ostorian]]'s garrison staff and by Dace, who commanded the Sworn, with the Legates of the deep rivers fighting beside them on terms of their own. The Frost Masons recognised no authority but their own Master of the Ice, and once the counter-mining was in their hands the ground beneath the Arsenal became a domain the Archflamen's commanders could neither see nor direct. The thrall miners who served under the Frost Masons were taken from Ostorian's cohorts without his consent, and the heat-well crews from the casting lines without the Steward's. The Archflamen approved every request. The Third's intelligence officers, reading the Arsenal's traffic where they could, noted that from Rotation Eleven onward Ostorian's name appeared less often in it and the Frost Masons' more, and concluded that the defence of the Arsenal was passing out of the hands of the soldiers who had begun it.

The Répertoire estimated the Archflamen's strength below ground at about sixty thousand miners drawn from the thrall gangs and the heat-well crews, with perhaps four thousand Frost Masons directing them. The figure for the Frost Masons was a guess. They had come up the river with the drowned and kept below ground for the most part, and no soldier of the Third had seen more than a few dozen of them together. They wore armour of grey ice that did not melt at Depth 40, worked without lamps, and could hear a pick through two hundred metres of basalt. Several of the Hephaestean sapper companies had met them before, at Hold Charybdis on the Cocytus under Kastellan's command, and regarded them as the most dangerous miners in Tartarus.

The method of the Hephaestean sappers was old and exacting. A sapper officer chose the line of a tunnel and his sappers sank a shaft to the required depth, either straight down with ladders or as a sloping incline down which heavier gear could be brought. Shaft heads were concealed behind false trench walls under the guard of trench sentries, and the larger inclines were sunk further back, behind cinder rises out of sight of the Archflamen's spotters. The sappers worked in squads of five, in which two cut at the face, one carried the spoil back for use as sandbags in the trenches above, one braced and one listened, seated in a niche in the gallery wall with a geophone augur pressed to the rock through every watch. Wet rock required heavier bracing and slowed the work, and as each tunnel lengthened, power lines, pumps and spoil winches followed it. Galleries were driven off the shaft foot under constant survey by the squad's optio, since a straight course was hard to hold underground. The first galleries were narrow, two sappers wide, but as the war beneath developed they became guides for wider ones cut by the Forge's boring engines.

### 8.5 The Silent Dark

The sappers faced many enemies before they met the Archflamen's. Every tunnel roof would fall in time under its own weight and had to be propped. The surveys usually found the seep-rivers and the galleries were routed around them, but the sappers often worked ankle-deep in hot water that the hand pumps could not clear. Deep in the basalt there was little oxygen and a number of gases without smell, so the sappers carried detectors and respirators and worked masked whenever the detectors showed red. In the lowest galleries, near the heat veins, the rock was hot to the touch, and the squads worked in shifts of an hour. Above all there was noise. Underground, sound was the only means of finding an enemy, and it carried well through rock. Any sound in a deep gallery could betray it and bring a counter-mine upon it. The war beneath was a war of silence, in which the sappers wrapped their picks in cloth, worked barefoot and spoke by hand signs.

The listening companies of the Forge were the sappers' principal defence. Their posts were sunk at intervals of a hundred metres along the front and linked by cable to a listening room beneath each Line Phalanx's command bunker, where officers plotted every reported sound on the gallery maps and ordered counter-mines against anything that could not be accounted for. By the middle of Rotation Eleven the listening net beneath the Flats was the densest the Forge had ever laid. The Frost Masons answered it by digging during the barrage, when the noise of the guns drowned everything else below ground, and the Third's listeners came to dread the hour of the heaviest bombardment more than any other.

An enemy tunnel, once found, could be destroyed by counter-mining. A team would dig toward it and set a collapse-charge against it, a long, narrow charge shaped to throw its blast into the rock, which the Third's sappers called a dart. The Hephaestean sappers carried them ready-made, the Archflamen's miners made their own from the Arsenal's blasting stores, and the Frost Masons made theirs from ice. A dart was bored into the gallery wall, walled in behind with sandbags and fired by cable from a safe distance, usually the surface, bringing the rock down upon anyone in the enemy gallery or sealing them behind a fall that could not be cleared before the air ran out. A dart set by a team that had found the enemy without being found gave no warning at all. A counter-mine team might also break into the enemy's galleries to seize them, and every such break-in ended in a short and savage fight in the dark by lamplight, with shotguns, pistols, grenades and knives.

The fighting under Leaf 54-47 on the four hundred and second cycle of Rotation Ten was typical of the many small actions of the war beneath. A squad of the Sixty-Sixth Sapper Company, mining beneath a bunker, located an enemy counter-mine above its own gallery and halted all work until the listeners could hear no digging in it. [[Ansel Rook|Sapper-Lieutenant Ansel Rook]] then led a hand-picked section through its floor and divided it to explore the enemy gallery in both directions. When six of the Archflamen's miners passed between the two halves of the section, Rook followed them and engaged them from behind as the other half came back toward them, and all six miners were killed in the crossfire. Reinforcements drawn by the firing then overran the two sappers left to guard the junction before they could finish preparing a charge to drop it, and both were killed ninety metres down. Rook, wounded, covered the section's withdrawal through the hole and had the dart in his own gallery fired as the pursuing miners came through after him, bringing the roof down upon them. The Sixty-Sixth Company sent two names to the Deep List that night and four wounded to the surface, Rook among them. With the enemy gallery located, it fired further darts and destroyed the rest of it. The Archflamen's miners answered raids of this kind with traps and stronger guards in their galleries. The Frost Masons began to fill abandoned galleries with ice that closed behind them and did not melt, and later to leave galleries open and empty for the Third's sappers to find, with darts already set in their walls.

### 8.6 The Northern Galleries (Rotation Eleven, Cycles 120 to 340)

While the Seventh and Eighth Line Phalanxes fought for the ground beneath Leaves 55 and 56, the sapper regiments in the north worked against the works between the inner belt and the Cinder Wall along the Mora Cut, where the ground was drier and the basalt lay nearer the surface. Their method there was the classic one of the Hephaestean schools, a series of small mines driven beneath the Archflamen's pillboxes and gun pits and fired together at the start of an attack on the surface. Between the hundred and twentieth and the three hundred and fortieth cycles of Rotation Eleven, the northern sappers fired forty-one mines on the front of the Second and Fifth Line Phalanxes. Twenty-nine destroyed their targets, eight were found and destroyed by counter-mines before they could be fired, and four were fired too late to help the attacks they had been dug for. On the leaves where the mines succeeded, the northern Phalanxes gained more than two kilometres at a cost in transfers less than a third of what the same ground would have cost on the surface alone. It was the clearest evidence of the siege that Kastellan's war beneath could succeed, and it put eleven thousand names on the Deep List.

### 8.7 Vault 9 (Rotation Eleven, Cycles 60 to 64)

The ground beneath Leaves 55 and 56 was the most heavily mined on the front, and the Seventh and Eighth Line Phalanxes employed their sappers there without pause. Their principal objective was [[Vault 9]], one of the Arsenal's outer vaults, cut into the basalt under the old inner belt two kilometres outside the Cinder Wall. The Archflamen still used it as a sheltered depot for stores and armour, and a rotation of Phaethon fire had achieved little against it, since its contents lay deep in the rock. The sappers' plan was to tunnel to it, break in by surprise and overrun it from below. The Frost Masons heard them coming and counter-mined the surrounding ground heavily, and a second war developed in the dark around Vault 9 as it became clear that the Archflamen understood the Third's objective and meant to deny it. A maze of shafts and galleries grew up beneath Leaves 55 and 56. With surprise lost, Kastellan committed the Forge's boring engines to speed the work.

The fighting underground there was as heavy as any on the surface, and the sapper companies lost more soldiers than the trench regiments above them, every one of whom went onto the Deep List. Galleries met and fought every cycle, sometimes for days at a time, as each side tried to seize the other's tunnels and push on from them. By weight of numbers the Hephaestean sappers gradually gained the upper hand and drove the Frost Masons back, and on the sixtieth cycle of Rotation Eleven they reported that they could break into the vault. Five sapper companies would make the break-in and hold the breach, with further companies behind them, stiffened by breachers in plate carrying meltas and demolition charges, to exploit it. The Forty-Ninth and Fiftieth Trench Regiments would make diversionary attacks on the surface on either side, pressed hard enough to draw in the Archflamen's cohorts, and the Fourth Ordnance Regiment would fire a box barrage over Vault 9 to seal it from reinforcement from above.

The Archflamen knew that the attack must come soon and had prepared for it. He sent reinforcements into the vault, among them a warband of the Cinderborn called the Ninefold Brand, who sought nothing more than close fighting from room to room. The Third's commanders did not know that most of the vault's stores and armour had already been withdrawn deeper into the spur, and that the objective was nearly empty. Inside, the defenders had blocked the main passages with barricades of slag drums, shell crates, sandbags and basalt blocks, each covered by heavy guns, so that every passage would have to be stormed and paid for. Two galleries had been cut beneath the vault, and in each a breaching charge of several hundred tonnes had been tamped in behind sandbag walls to drive its blast upward.

On the sixty-fourth cycle the charges were fired by Sapper-Captain Odile Marchand of the Twentieth Sapper Company. The blast was felt in the trenches ninety metres above. Inside the vault it brought down two storerooms, crushed or buried the defenders nearest them and filled the passages with smoke and dust, and the first sapper squads came up through the two breaches over rubble piled metres high. For some minutes they met no resistance. The defenders beyond the rubble held their fire until the shock had passed, and then opened up down every passage with their heavy guns. Sappers caught in the open were cut down where they stood, and every barricade the sappers reduced with grenades cost them dead and wounded, while each fallen gunner was replaced. Flame sections came forward and filled the passages with fire and fumes, and overhead the box barrage shook the ceilings. The first attack stalled with the sappers holding only a foothold in the vault.

Captain Marchand came forward and ordered her sappers to blow their own passages through the inner walls with melta-charges, so that they could enter rooms from directions the defenders were not covering, and authorised gas grenades to drive the thralls from their barricades. The tactic restored movement for a time, until a melta-charge opened a welded blast door at the foot of a stair behind which the Ninefold Brand were waiting. The Cinderborn charged through the breach as it cooled, singing a hymn of the Red Furnace, and Marchand was among the first killed. In the passages beyond, the Cinderborn killed sappers by the hundred. They were taller, faster and stronger than any soldier of the Third, the gas had no effect on them, and they moved through the vault at will while the sappers sealed their breaches against them and fought to hold what they had taken. On the second cycle the breachers were committed from the reserve in the hope that their plate would succeed where the sappers had failed. They suffered as the sappers had in the same fire-swept passages, though in places they cleared a barricade and turned it against its builders, only for the Cinderborn to counter-attack through their fire. Rifle rounds and grenades did little to them, and a Cinderborn would walk through a stream of keyed fire from a heavy flamer to kill the breacher who fired it. The Third's soldiers had no answer to an enemy that fire did not harm.

On the third cycle the Frost Masons, who had prepared their counter-mines in secret, brought down one of the Third's two approach galleries, burying a sapper squad on its way to the vault. The whole of the battle now depended upon a single gallery, and squads had to be withdrawn from the fighting to dig counter-mines and man additional listening posts, though under the box barrage the listeners could hear almost nothing. The five companies of the first wave, each six hundred strong at the outset, were by then mostly below half strength. The Twentieth Sapper Company numbered one hundred and eighty-seven. All of their dead went onto the Deep List. After four cycles without gains, with the Frost Masons digging toward the last supply gallery and a party sent to clear the first fall buried by a second dart, the attack was called off, and the sapper squads came back to the surface after four cycles in a battle without quarter. They had failed to take Vault 9.

The first battle for Vault 9 had cost the five assault companies more than two thousand names on the Deep List and the breacher cohorts committed after them some nine thousand transfers, for an objective that would have yielded little if it had been taken. Kastellan's report on it to Watchpoint Hades was candid. The sappers had been beaten by the Cinderborn, against whom the Third possessed no proven answer above or below ground, and he asked the Lord Marshal for help in finding one. None was available at the time. The report did establish a point the Third would act upon later. The Cinderborn were the Archflamen's best soldiers, and wherever they were found in strength the Third would have to meet them with something other than its trench regiments.

Three battles were fought for Vault 9 in all, before the Twenty-Second Trench Regiment overran it from the surface at the end of Rotation Eleven. The defenders trapped inside by the advance were given no quarter.
