The appearance of the Hollowed changed the Répertoire's arithmetic for the rest of the siege. Until Rotation Sixteen, a soldier lost on the Flats had been lost to the Third and nothing worse. From the hook onward, a soldier lost in the Grey might be added to the enemy's strength, and the Répertoire's estimates of the Archflamen's army, which had fallen every rotation since the inner belt broke, began to rise again. The Third issued new orders on the handling of the dead. Bodies left in the Grey were to be burned where possible, and regiments advancing through ground where the Grey had fallen were to treat any figure in the Third's colours that did not answer a challenge as hostile. The order was obeyed. It did nothing for the morale of the regiments obliged to carry it out.

### 10.4 Height 202 (Rotation Sixteen, Cycle 230)

The Sixteenth and Twentieth Armoured required a complete refit after the hook, and the First Line Phalanx postponed its offensive to wait for them. On the two hundred and thirtieth cycle, the Eleventh Line Phalanx attacked alone along a front of fifteen kilometres. The Sixty-Eighth Trench Regiment faced the hardest task. Its sector took it up the northern slopes of a range of old cinder vents that had gone cold before the Arsenal was built, at whose southern end stood the cone of the Acropolis itself. Its first objective was Height 187. After that it had to take three hardened ceiling-lance silos, built to survive a direct hit from anything the Third could fire, before the final climb to Height 202, from which it would look down upon the Cinder Wall. The Seventieth had further to go over easier ground, with two lines of trenches to storm before it reached the Wall. The Tenth Ordnance Phalanx had three hundred and fifty tanks ready to support the attack, among them thirty [[Colossus Super-Heavy|Colossi]], and the Behemoths of Talos stood by. Sappers in every regiment's sector had been at work for a rotation beneath the Archflamen's works, and the battle opened with their mines and with saps breaking out behind the strongpoints they had marked.

In a seep-storm, the leading cohorts of the Sixty-Eighth began the climb. Within a few hours the advance had been halted along the whole line by strongpoints dug deep into the slopes with clear fields of fire downhill, while the cohorts coming up behind were cut to pieces by guns sheltered behind the Wall. To bring the tanks forward, the Hundred and Ninth Sapper Company had been ordered to build a road, and its sappers worked under fire throughout the cycle, blasting rock and clearing a track for the armour waiting at the foot of the slope. The work went slowly, and without tanks to destroy the gun pits, the soldiers on the slope could do little but endure. The climb to Height 202 would be another long and bitter grind. On the left of the Eleventh's attack, the Seventieth opened with a charge by four hundred and fifty outriders on ash-bikes along the old ore road, without a preliminary barrage, since the staff believed the guns would achieve little against the dug-in cohorts and would only warn them of the attack. The charge depended entirely on surprise. At first it worked, and some of the leading squadrons broke through as far as Leaf 58-45, but most of the riders were shot down before they reached anything. About sixty answered the roll at the end of the cycle.

The Eleventh's offensive went on slowly, at the usual cost in soldiers and tanks, and the First Line Phalanx's delay made that cost far higher. With the First's front quiet, the Archflamen could shift soldiers from it to meet the Eleventh, and the attrition required to wear down the defenders opposite the Eleventh would take much longer. The Eleventh's commanders repeatedly asked Grace to set the First Line Phalanx moving and take the pressure off them, and she repeatedly refused, insisting that the First would go when every tank was in place and not before. For more than a week the Eleventh bled alone. After the fourth such request, Grace informed the Eleventh that the First would attack in eight cycles. On the two hundred and sixty-first cycle it did, with three regiments striking south-west across fifteen kilometres toward the Cinder Wall, massed tanks and Colossi at their head. The armoured spearheads were soon breaking into the Archflamen's lines, where they met the survivors of the cohorts the Fifth's hook had driven back into the pocket. The Unremembered fought fanatically and fired more of the Grey. The Wrath wings now flew every watch, and the Archflamen's swarms and drowned Lanterns appeared less and less often, so that the Third seemed at last to have won the air beneath the ceiling. The heavy Wraths bombed the Cinder Wall on Leaf 58-45 and carpeted the ground behind it, and the combined weight of all the attacks was grinding the defenders down.

### 10.5 The Kindling of Draugos (Rotation Sixteen, Cycles 260 to 320)

As the Third's regiments pressed forward, even the Legates of the deep rivers could not save the Archflamen from the weight of what the Empire had set in motion. For the first time, the Arsenal's soldiers were short of shells, food and fuel, and some cohorts had begun to hoard what remained while others watched their stocks fall every cycle. The morale of the Arsenal's own soldiers was declining. Some still fought as hard as ever, out of madness or unshakeable faith, but others threw down their rifles and went over to the Third, or ran, though the [[Lictors of the Flame]] shot any they caught. For the first time the Archflamen's army faced the danger that collapse in one sector would spread to the next until the whole of it gave way. There were those among its commanders who would not permit the war to end. The Legates of the rivers had come up to the Flats to kill, and they had no intention of seeing the war end before it had given them all the killing it could.

The most dangerous of them was [[Draugos the Kindled]], war-chief of the Cinderborn. What took place at the summit of the Archflamen's army during these months was established only afterwards, during the Hunt's long investigation into the siege, from the questioning of prisoners and the work of the Hunt's sister-augurs upon their minds. At the time, the Third knew nothing of events inside the Wall. Draugos had come up the Phlegethon to burn things for the river. He did not serve the Archflamen or recognise his authority, and like the other bands of the deep his Cinderborn were loyal only to their war-chief and their river. Such allies were formidable in battle and dangerous in adversity, and as the front contracted and the stores ran short, the bands of the deep began to turn upon their allies and upon one another. Draugos meant to halt the rot before it brought down the Arsenal, to prolong the war until nothing remained on the Flats to burn, and to take the Arsenal for himself.

He began by bringing the other fire-bands under his command, which would make his the largest faction of the Phlegethon on the Flats, and with them behind him he intended to take the whole of the Archflamen's army, which meant removing the Archflamen and his household. Whether he had always intended this, or had been instructed to by something beneath him in the river, the Hunt's inquiry could not establish. The fire-bands respected only strength in combat, and few on the Flats could match his. He challenged the tribune of the Red Furnace, the most savage of the Phlegethon's bands after his own, to single combat for command of both. Refusal would have dishonoured the tribune before the river, and his own warriors would have pulled him down for it. The two fought to the death in the light of the casting pits at the foot of the cone, and Draugos won, claiming the Red Furnace by right of conquest. None of its warriors disputed the claim.

He then secured the [[Kindlers]], the priests of the deep fire, who had been working for most of the siege to open the way between the Flats and what lay beneath the Phlegethon, and who had performed the reformation ritual on the riverbank in the fifth rotation. They knew how to open a door. What they lacked were the tens of thousands of lives a door of the size they intended would cost, and Draugos offered to provide them in return for their oath to follow him for the remainder of the war. He offered them a promise as well. What came through the door would be the greatest of the old keepers of the pit, [[Kampe]], the warden of Tartarus, who had held the Hundred-Handed for the Titans before the [[Olympians]] were born. The Kindlers swore to him, and the fire-bands were his.

With the strongest faction on the Flats behind him, Draugos turned upon the remaining bands with a simple demand that they join him or burn. To show that he meant it, he fell without warning upon the war-chief of the [[Brethren of the Black Shale]], an old band from the deep Phlegethon, and killed him and all his champions in their own camp. The rest of the Brethren submitted after a short fight, and the other bands followed them, with two exceptions. The Forsworn would not swear to Draugos, and neither would the Unremembered.

[[Hesper Unsworn]] made a bargain of her own to keep the Forsworn out of Draugos's way. Of all the war-chiefs of the deep she was the only one the Archflamen trusted, and she had been directing much of his war for rotations. She knew that he was by now no more than a banner, though the Arsenal's people still revered him as the holy man who had freed them. She agreed to stand aside and let Draugos take him, in return for the Forsworn keeping their own command, and she kept her word. In secret, the Forsworn admitted Draugos and his champions to the Archflamen's apartments in the Acropolis. Those of the household guard who had not already been quietly killed by the Forsworn were cut down by Draugos's champions, who left a trail of butchery through the apartments. [[Ilan Dace|Haruspex Ilan Dace]] escaped with a few of his guards and the girl [[Mirren]] and fled to the Lethe Cut and the Unremembered. Archflamen Sabinus Orrhae was taken alive. He had played his part, and the deep fire had only one use left for him. Draugos had him and his surviving household thrown into the deepest cells beneath the cone, to go into the Kindlers' door with the tens of thousands. By blood and treachery, Draugos had made himself master of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal, with millions to march at his word and a door to build.

### 10.6 Gibbet Hill (Rotation Sixteen, Cycle 330)

The Third knew nothing of the fall of the Archflamen or the rise of Draugos. On Quarterdeck Nineteen, Grace followed the offensives of the First and Eleventh Line Phalanxes cycle by cycle. The advance toward the Wall had been slower than she had hoped, but the Archflamen's army had now cracked, and the ground gained each cycle was growing. The number of prisoners had risen sharply, and the quartermasters reported large quantities of abandoned tanks and captured equipment. In some sectors the Archflamen's soldiers appeared to have lost the will to fight. The First Line Phalanx's Third Trench Regiment was now within striking distance of the Wall, and Grace ordered it to take the [[Ore Gate]] on Leaf 57-45 or force a breach near it and hold that, so that the First's offensive would end only when it had a road through the Wall. The Behemoths would go in with it.

On the Eleventh's front, progress had also been slow. The Hundred and Ninth Sapper Company's road had held back the whole attack, and cohorts had clung to the slope waiting for tank support that could not reach them, while hundreds of sappers died under the shelling. Even without the tanks, the ceiling-lance silos fell one by one in hard fighting at close quarters. From the last of them, the Sixty-Eighth could see what the Archflamen's soldiers had done on the crest of Height 202. They had built gallows there and hanged prisoners of the Third from them as a warning, after first cutting out their implants, so that every soldier on the gallows had died slowly with nothing to carry them back to Hades. Every one had gone null. The Third's soldiers called the place [[Gibbet Hill]].

On the three hundred and thirtieth cycle, the Sixty-Eighth's Fifteenth Cohort, its centuries reduced to twenty soldiers or fewer by the fighting on the slopes, was close enough to attempt the crest. Three times it went up behind mortar barrages and three times it was thrown back in the fighting at the top, and its commander was mortally wounded on the slope, lying for several hours out of reach of a relay before a relay team crawled up to him and he was able to upload. The cohort went up a fourth time under cover of the dark watch, and after a confused fight in the darkness took Gibbet Hill, with a single optio left to command its defence. The expected counter-attack never came, a further sign that the Archflamen's army was weakening. The Sixty-Eighth cut the bodies down from the gallows, carried them down the hill, and planted the regiment's standard on the crest. Below it lay the Cinder Wall, and beyond the Wall, close now, the cone of the Acropolis.

The capture of Gibbet Hill completed the second of the operations Grace had planned to follow the hook. The Eleventh Line Phalanx had taken a hundred cycles to advance fifteen kilometres and had lost a hundred and ninety thousand soldiers doing it, more than its whole strength at the start of the offensive, so that its regiments ended the operation largely composed of soldiers who had been transferred during it. Its performance had exceeded the expectations of the staff that had doubted it. With the First Line Phalanx on the Wall to the south and the Eleventh on Height 202, the ring around the Acropolis was closed along its whole length for the first time in the siege, and from the crest of Gibbet Hill the Third's spotters could direct fire onto the inner face of the Cinder Wall and the lower slopes of the cone. The Archflamen's army could no longer move between the northern and southern halves of its perimeter without coming under observation, and its last stores outside the Wall, in the old casting yards along the foot of the vents, were taken or destroyed in the cycles that followed. Grace reported to the Director that the Arsenal was enclosed and that the Third could begin preparing the assault on the Acropolis. The report was the last good news the Hunt would send to Luna for some time.

### 10.7 The Archflamen's Door

> *"Every engine has its limit. So does every soldier. Find it before the enemy does."*
>
> Cradle-Lord Doros Varne, Forge Cohort Talos

### 10.8 A Test of Loyalty (Rotation Sixteen, Cycle 360 to Rotation Seventeen, Cycle 90)

On the three hundred and sixtieth cycle of Rotation Sixteen, the Dionysian Logistics Directorate issued the Third's command with [[Directive 40-PA-0907]], ordering it to withdraw six regiments from the line and prepare them for transport. The Directorate had reviewed the theatre's requirements and found that the Third's strength on the Flats could no longer be sustained and was no longer required. Its supply and transport would be reduced to match, and the reduction of the Arsenal was downgraded in priority. Given the losses inflicted on the Archflamen, the Directorate calculated that the regiments remaining would be sufficient to finish the siege, at an acceptable cost in time and in the additional transfers that would result. The six regiments would go to [[Lethe Hold]], where the Unremembered had been raiding the vat-lines and where Commander Thaleia had been asking for reinforcement for two rotations.

The directive was transmitted directly to Grace's forward bunker on the Flats, and her staff recognised it at once for what it was. The College had been defeated in the Conclave, and now it was retaliating against the Hunt. Thwarted in its attempt to take control of the war, it had been working behind the scenes ever since to weaken the Hunt's position on the Flats and secure a share of the Third's regiments for its own purposes. The Hunt's staff assumed that the College had promised the Directorate's masters on the ceiling worlds its votes and support in other matters in return. Hunt Second Celyn objected that the Directorate could not act against the Director's seal. Grace judged that it had found a way around it. The directive left the Third under the crescent and took six of its regiments to defend a hold against the same enemy the Third was fighting, and the manoeuvre was difficult to oppose.

Grace sent envoys of her own up the cables, two Psiloi officers and a clerk of the Hunt carrying the Director's seal, to bypass the Directorate and put the case to the Director herself and, through her if necessary, to the Council of the Twelve, on which the Director sat. The Directorate supplied and moved the Third, but the Third was under the crescent now, and the Directorate had gone over the Hunt's head without consulting the Lieutenant. The envoys joined the crowds of petitioners in the galleries outside the Director's audience hall, where every petitioner would be heard in time. It would not be quick. In the meantime, Grace could only continue to fight the war.

When the answer came, it was unfavourable. The Director had put the case to the master of the Dionysian Logistics Directorate, who had sought and received his patron's support, and in the Council the Lord of the College had backed them both. It was a powerful alliance. Others, among them the Forge and the [[Pyrrhus Command]], abstained. Directive 40-PA-0907 would stand, softened slightly in the Hunt's favour. The Director's seal had been overruled by the highest authority in the Empire, and its word was law. Grace could have refused. She could have held the Flats with the Third behind her and challenged the Directorate to come and take its regiments, and with the Third's support she might have resisted arrest long enough to take the Arsenal. Such a refusal would have been declared a revolt at once, and it would have ended with her name under a CODE 31 and a second war, this time against Enforcement. Others had taken that road out of pride, and Grace had watched several of them do it. An officer who believed she knew the Empire's needs better than the Council of the Twelve had a code number of her own. After holding the directive for most of a watch, she signed the orders for the six regiments to withdraw.

On the ninetieth cycle of Rotation Seventeen, the First Line Phalanx's three remaining regiments left the Flats, and with them went the three Phaethon regiments of the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx, halving the Third's heaviest artillery. The Ninth Assault Phalanx was also withdrawn, though Grace's case had saved the Twentieth Armoured, which was transferred to the Seventh Line Phalanx. Half the independent batteries used to stiffen the guns at the point of every offensive went as well. They went up the cables to Lethe Hold and became Commander Thaleia's. Grace would have to finish the siege with what remained.

On their way back down the cables, Grace's envoys were found dead in their compartment on a supply car. All three had been beheaded cleanly beneath the implants, and the implants had been taken with the heads, so that all three were null. Their killers were never found or named. The killings were understood as a message that the College had been watching the Lieutenant and had tested her loyalty, and that she had passed. Grace had the bodies sent to Luna and spoke of the matter to no one for a cycle.

The withdrawal was a serious blow to the Third, and its consequences were felt for the rest of the siege. Six regiments was nearly a sixth of the Third's infantry, and the three Phaethon regiments had been the only guns on the Flats able to overload the [[Energy Shielding|wards]] of the Acropolis from beyond the range of its lances. Every plan for the final assault had assumed them. The Hunt's staff calculated that the directive had added at least two rotations to the siege and several million transfers to its cost, and the Répertoire's own revision, published a cycle after the regiments left, did not dispute the figure. At Lethe Hold, Commander Thaleia received the six regiments with more equipment than her sector had held in a century, and used them well. The Unremembered's raids on the vat-lines ceased within a rotation, and Lethe Hold logged no further losses to them for the remainder of the war. The Directorate cited the result as proof that its decision had been sound. On the Flats, the Third's officers observed that the Unremembered had stopped raiding Lethe Hold because Ysander Pale had drawn the last of his warbands down to the Cinder Flats, where the Third was now weaker by six regiments to meet them.

The affair also settled the position of the Hunt within the Empire's politics for the rest of the siege. The College had shown that it could reach past the Director's seal through the Council of the Twelve, and the Hunt had shown that it would obey the Council even when obedience cost it dearly. Grace's refusal to resist the directive was later held up within the Hunt as an example of the discipline expected of a Lieutenant. It was not held up as an example in her presence.

### 10.9 The Ore Gate (Rotation Seventeen, Cycle 210)

The quarrels within the Empire could not stop the siege. Grace now had fewer soldiers, tanks and shells, but the Archflamen's army was showing signs of breaking at last, and the remaining phalanxes were given new positions on the line. The task of taking the Ore Gate passed to the [[Second Line Phalanx]], which was ordered to provide one regiment at full strength for it, and it gave the Forty-Third Trench Regiment. It took weeks to bring the guns into position and more weeks for the sappers to dig their saps, but on the two hundred and tenth cycle the regiment was ready. [[Mara Thyssen|Colonel Mara Thyssen]] of the Forty-Third planned the assault, and [[Silas Varro|Provost-General Silas Varro]] would lead it from the front in his Thureos, the Ash Walker. As always, the tanks and breachers would go in behind a heavy barrage, supported by the Steropes-pattern Behemoths Hammer of Lemnos and Bellows of the Forge, whose task was to strike the Wall and the gate with their heaviest weapons and open the breaches the breachers and tanks would need.

The Forty-Third advanced as the Behemoths' missile racks fired salvo after salvo at the gate, and the fire against it grew heavier the closer it came. Despite that fire, the Archflamen counter-attacked. The gate's armoured doors rolled back and a mass of infantry, tanks, guns and the spider-legged walkers of the casting lines came out together, and among them were the warriors of the Red Furnace and Draugos's champions in their furnace-plate. The Ash Walker was knocked out early in the fighting and its crew killed. Varro, wounded, was caught on foot as the counter-attack drove the Forty-Third back past his position, and was killed by Draugos himself. The relay logged him null. The Cinderborn took the soldiers they caught, and the soldiers of the Third had begun to learn what was done with them.

Varro's death did not end the battle. The counter-attack had driven the Forty-Third back, but its numbers began to tell, and more tanks drove for the gate again while the Hammer of Lemnos struck it with its forge-lance until the gate was gone and the parapet above it had collapsed. The towers on either side were reduced to rubble by the guns and the tanks. Over the rubble the fighting swung back and forth. The Forty-Third rushed the remains of the gate with fixed bayonets and was thrown back by the Cinderborn, whose champions, each a veteran of a hundred fights in the deep, fought at the front with Draugos among them. The fighting continued through the cycle and the dark watch, with the shells of both sides falling among the combatants. For two more cycles the Forty-Third threw itself at the gate and was repulsed each time with heavy losses.

During the fighting, rents began to open in the air above the gate, thin bright lines that hung for a few seconds and closed. The Hunt's augurs concluded that they were deliberate, and that the Archflamen's people were already opening the way for the things beneath the river. Grace asked Hunt-Elder Sarn to discover who or what was responsible, and it was Sarn's work that would later uncover the story of Draugos's rise and of the Kindlers' door. The Hunt answered in other ways too. The Director sent a second company of the Sixth Moonwall to the Flats, and on the three hundredth cycle Flame-Captain Dione Tharn took her place on the line beside Roe.

The battle for the Ore Gate had cost the Forty-Third Trench Regiment two-thirds of its strength and its commander, and the gate remained in the Archflamen's hands, though in name only, since nothing of it was left standing. The Third held the rubble of its outer towers. The death of Provost-General Varro was keenly felt. He had commanded the Third's armour in every major offensive since the [[Operation Antilochus|Big Push]], and his loss null, at the hands of a war-chief of the deep, was taken by the Third's soldiers as an omen. The Répertoire recorded the battle as a reverse. The Hunt recorded it differently. It was at the Ore Gate that the Third first faced Draugos in person, and the Hunt's observers came away from the fighting convinced that the Archflamen no longer commanded his own army. The furnace-plate of Draugos's champions was seen at every point where the defence was strongest, and the Archflamen's banner, which had flown over every counter-attack of the siege, was not seen at all. Grace reported the observation to the Director with her estimate that a change of command had taken place inside the Wall. She was correct, and it would be half a rotation before the Hunt could say how.

### 10.10 Under the Cone (Rotation Seventeen)

While the fighting around the Wall continued, the sappers pushed deeper toward their objective. The war underground was as bitter and as slow as the war above, but progress was being made, and galleries were approaching the cone from every direction. The Frost Masons counter-mined, but they lacked what the Hephaestean sappers had, the borers, the hundreds of tonnes of explosive and what seemed an inexhaustible supply of soldiers to dig. Unable to counter-mine fast enough, the Archflamen's people began to release creatures into the galleries: starving drakon-spawn from the river channels, nameless things of the deep that came up through cracks in the rock, and Laistrygons who had been in the Grey until nothing remained in them but hunger, sent down the shafts and abandoned there. The sappers found themselves fighting in the dark half a kilometre from the nearest relay, and every sapper who died there went onto the Deep List. Sapper-Lieutenant Ansel Rook's company lost a third of its strength in Rotation Seventeen, almost all of it to creatures.

Grace pressed the sapper commanders for progress. The battle around the Wall was consuming soldiers and tanks faster than any fighting she had seen, whole regiments had already been destroyed in the attacks, and a way in from below might prove the best road into the Acropolis. The sapper commanders could not promise a gallery beneath the cone's undercroft for at least another half rotation. Until then, the costly battles above ground would have to continue, if only to prevent the Archflamen from moving his people below to dig against the sappers.

By the end of Rotation Seventeen the reduction of the Pyriphlegethon Arsenal had reached its final stage. The ring was closed on the Cinder Wall along its whole length, the Wall itself had been breached at the Ore Gate and in three other places, and the sappers were within a few hundred metres of the undercroft of the cone. The Archflamen's army had lost the last of its ground outside the Wall and most of its stores, and the Hunt's estimate of its strength had fallen below two million for the first time. Against that, the Third was weaker than at any point since the Big Push. It had lost six regiments and half its heaviest guns to the Directorate, its armour had been worn down in the hook and on the Wall, and the null rate, which had fallen during the Kastellan Offensive, was rising again as the fighting drew closer to the Echo Mast. The Répertoire's estimate for the assault on the Acropolis, published at the turn of the rotation, was the highest it had produced for any single operation of the siege. Grace signed it without comment and sent it to Luna, where the Director signed it in turn.

## 11 The Scree

> *"My eye to your aim. My hand to your will. My life to your hunt."*
>
> Oath of the Psiloi, said on the Flats before the first assault on the Acropolis

### 11.1 The Acropolis (Rotation Eighteen)

All the old ore roads of the Cinder Flats ended at the foot of the Acropolis. They had linked the casting lines, heat-wells, magazines and trench belts that now lay wrecked behind the Third, and they converged on the cone, the last and tallest of the old cinder vents, which rose out of the Flats on a pillar of black basalt. The main road climbed its side in switchbacks to the [[Founders' Gate]], a gate of cut basalt blocks worn smooth by eight thousand years of heat. Above the walls and parapets of the cone rose the dome of the Katabasion. It had begun as a single shrine over the bones of the first legion to go down into Tartarus and return, and over the centuries it had grown into a vast reliquary hall carved from the cone's own stone, where pilgrims from the ceiling worlds came in their thousands to touch the legion's shields. Higher still stood the Echo Mast. Once the Arsenal's noosphere relay, the spire that had carried every upload on the Flats to Watchpoint Hades, it stood upon the cone's inner ward, separated from the rest of the Acropolis by a sheer gorge that split the summit in two, and from it a watcher could see the whole of the Flats.

By the eighteenth rotation, the Flats below the cone had become a field of smoke and fire. The guns of the Third, once a distant thunder, were now a single continuous noise around the cone, and shells struck its slopes or burst against the wards of the Acropolis every minute. Inside its walls the Acropolis was crammed with soldiers, guns and stores. Outside them, the slopes were covered with gun pits and observation posts, every face of the cone was pocked with covered trenches and marksmen's holes, and the ground around its foot was scarred with bunkers, pillboxes and dugouts. The Archflamen's people, and now Draugos's, had worked hard to make the Acropolis a rock upon which the Third would break itself again and again. After rotations of bombing by the [[Wrath-Pattern Strike Wing|Wrath wings]] and long-range fire from the heaviest guns the Third possessed, the Acropolis was badly damaged and still standing. The end of the siege would be fought out upon it.

### 11.2 The Hunt's Plan

A plan for the assault upon the cone had existed almost since the siege began. The Arsenal was an Imperial work, and the Répertoire held every plan and drawing of every change and strengthening the founders and the Directorate had ever made to it. Castorre had planned the assault in detail, [[Theron Kastellan|Kastellan]] had revised his plans, and Grace was now the third commander to go through the slates, the war-table models, the supply figures and the regimental briefings and alter the plan again, reviewing it in full with her contingent and the senior Hunters before any orders were issued.

The operation was enormous and intricate. A million soldiers would attack in phases, to wear down the defenders one step at a time and then take and hold the key points. The ground was the worst problem. No armour could be used in the final assaults, since the slopes were too steep for tanks and barely passable for infantry, and the attackers would be funnelled into the killing grounds the defenders had prepared. However the planners approached it, the final push into the Acropolis would be the bloodiest phase of the bloodiest siege in the theatre. For many of the million, it would also be their last battle of any kind, since the Echo Mast stood at the top of the cone and the null rate rose the closer to it a soldier died.

The first priority was the wards. Fed by heat-wells sunk to the edge of the Phlegethon beneath the cone, they wrapped every wall, tower and buttress of the Acropolis, and while they stood there was little chance of harming its stone. Grace intended to pour enough fire onto the cone to leave no stone of it upon another, and accepted that the Directorate would complain and the College protest bitterly at the destruction of the Katabasion. She judged it a necessary sacrifice to burn out what was growing on the summit. The wards could be overloaded only when enough hits forced the heat-wells feeding them to cut out, and overloading each ward and keeping it down was the task of the Phaethons the Directorate had left to the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx. To do it they needed shells, and the Ordnance Phalanx was given first call on every shell that came down the cables. The regiments' own guns would go short, which meant fewer shells for preparatory barrages, smoke, counter-battery fire and rolling barrages, and fewer for meeting counter-attacks, leaving the breachers more exposed. While the wards stood, however, no assault could get through the walls. The regiments would have to rely on their lighter field guns and on the guns of their tanks.

The next objectives were the three main gates. With the wards down and the guns reducing the interior of the Acropolis to ruins, the breachers would climb the slopes and take the gates as stepping stones for the waves that would follow. It would be the hardest and most thankless work of the siege, an advance across open ground under fire against the strongest defences the enemy possessed, dug in behind walls so thick that only direct hits from the heaviest shells could touch them. Everyone understood that the cohorts would be ground up on the slopes, and the planners could see no alternative. Grace had to decide who would be given the task.

### 11.3 The Assault Forces

The forces assembled for the assault on the Acropolis were the largest the Third had concentrated at a single point during the siege, though they were smaller than the plan had once assumed. The Dionysian Logistics Directorate's withdrawal of six regiments and three Phaethon regiments in Rotation Seventeen had removed the margin that Castorre and Kastellan had both built into their versions of the plan, and Grace's revision had to accomplish with the eighteen Trench Regiments it could allot to the assault what the original had given to twenty-five. The first wave of the assault would be carried by four of them. The [[Twenty-Second Trench Regiment|Twenty-Second]] would take [[The Scree (Pyriphlegethon)|the Scree]] and the gates on the eastern and northern faces of the cone. The Thirty-Fifth would take the [[Gully of Ash]] and the vault doors on the western flank. The Twenty-Sixth would take the [[Slag Gate]] spur to the south-west, and the Forty-Third, rebuilt after the Ore Gate, would hold the Wall breaches behind all three and feed soldiers forward as they were needed. Behind them stood the regiments of the Seventh and Eleventh Line Phalanxes, to be committed regiment by regiment as the first wave was used up.

The armour available was the remnant of five armoured regiments, about six hundred tanks in all, of which no more than a third could be used on the cone at any one time. The Behemoths of [[Forge Cohort Talos]], under [[Doros Varne|Cradle-Lord Doros Varne]], would support the attack on the Slag Gate spur and the Gully of Ash, where the ground at the foot of the cone was broad enough for them to manoeuvre. Enforcement had lent the Hunt a Lictor cohort for the drop into the gully, and the Sixth Moonwall, with both of its companies now on the Flats, would wait aboard the Selene's Arrow as Grace's reserve. The Wrath wings would bomb the cone every watch until the assault began and the Tenth Ordnance Phalanx would fire on the wards throughout.

The Répertoire priced the operation at four hundred thousand transfers for the capture of the three gates, and its estimate of the null rate on the cone was the figure that most troubled Grace's staff. The Echo Mast stood at the summit, and every soldier who died on the slopes would die within its reach. The Répertoire put the null rate for the assault at one in six. It did not propose any means of reducing it. The Third's technical officers had been searching for one since the Lethe Cut, and had found none, and the regiments that would make the assault were told the figure in their briefings. None of them asked to be relieved of the task.

She gave it to the Twenty-Second Trench Regiment under Colonel [[Imre Saldane]], who had risen through its ranks over the whole of the siege and whose name had become something of a legend on the Flats. As a centurion he had led the attack that broke the outer belt sixteen rotations before, and had been cut off and fought for three cycles without help, wounded five times in that action alone. He had transferred twice since, and had lost his left arm to a Laistrygon's slag-rake during the Kastellan Offensive when he drove the Twenty-Second's salient into the inner belt on Leaf 54-45, returning from Hades with a bronze replacement to lead the regiment through the Cinder Wall. Grace told him plainly what she was asking of the regiment, and Saldane accepted the order without argument and undertook that the Twenty-Second would take the Founders' Gate.

### 11.4 The Twenty-Second's Plan

Saldane built his regiment up for the assault with shells, ammunition, fuel and soldiers drawn forward from the dumps, and studied the models and plans with his staff. From the forward trenches that now ringed the cone, the Twenty-Second would attack from three directions at once: from the north against the [[Flamen Gate]], from the west against the Slag Gate and the works outside it, including the ceiling-lance silo guarding the approach to the [[Pilgrim Gate]] above, and from the south-east, with its heaviest blow, against the Founders' Gate. Each attack would be led by a whole cohort in fifteen Hoplons, the First Cohort from the north, the Seventh from the west and the Fifteenth from the south-east.

The attack would begin only once the Ordnance Phalanx's barrage had done its work. As the barrage lifted onto the cone itself, the Hoplons would carry the leading cohorts through the last of the outer works and set them down where the slope became too steep to drive, and the breachers would continue uphill on foot under the fire of any tanks that could find a firing position at the foot of the cone. When the Hoplon cohorts stalled, a second wave on foot would pass through them, and the third wave would do the same. The fourth wave would contain most of the regiment's shock breachers, who would carry the attack to the gates and fight for the gate-towers. On the switchback road to the Founders' Gate, Hephaestean dozer-crawlers would clear the mines so that a company of Thureos could climb in single file to support the shock breachers, and fresh cohorts would wait at the foot of the road to go straight up and hold the gate once it was taken. The attacks on the other two gates would be strong feints, with enough soldiers and guns to hold their defenders in place and perhaps to take the gates if the defence wavered. It was at the Founders' Gate that Saldane hoped to win a foothold. If he did, another regiment would take over the assault, since by then the Twenty-Second would be finished.

### 11.5 The Scree (Rotation Eighteen, Cycle 81)

The barrage opened on the eighty-first cycle of Rotation Eighteen. The Tenth Ordnance Phalanx, dug in south of the Demus Gorge, had spent many watches preparing the fire mission, and every gun that could be found was trained upon the cone and the Acropolis. The divine mortar rounds landed first, the heavier rounds of the [[Bunker Busters|aedificant]] batteries a few seconds behind them, and finally the Phaethon shells. A thousand shells fell in the first minute, and the Acropolis and its slopes disappeared into fire and smoke. On time, Saldane ordered the attack. The Hoplons moved off with the leading cohorts aboard, followed by more cohorts on foot in ordered lines, by the Thureos tanks, the field guns and the Kentauros carriers, and finally by the shock breachers in their heavy plate. Saldane committed everything he had, keeping back three cohorts and his outriders as a reserve to be thrown in if a breakthrough came.

All three attacks met the hard resistance everyone had expected. The Hoplons drew heavy fire even through the smoke. The leading carrier of the Seventh Cohort was struck by a hidden gun whose shell passed through its troop bay and everyone in it. The First Cohort ran into a minefield and left several Hoplons burning, and from high on the cone a ceiling-lance fired flat down the slope and destroyed three more. On the Fifteenth Cohort's front, carriers were disabled by tank rounds and shredded tracks, and the soldiers who climbed out scattered under heavy fire. When the ramps came down, every century met the same reception: heavy guns sweeping the ground, hidden minefields, and mortar fire on pre-ranged targets of terrible accuracy. Centurion Petra Oss, leading the Seventh Cohort, was killed with her whole command section when a bunker believed destroyed opened fire at point-blank range, and her soldiers crawled on toward the road to the Slag Gate over their own dead.

In the north, the Archflamen counter-attacked the First Cohort and threw back the first wave in hand-to-hand fighting before it had begun to climb toward the Flamen Gate. The second wave turned the fight again with fixed bayonets, and the third won a few hundred metres in a rush before the guns and mortars broke it well short of the gate. Centurion Dagny Holm of the Nineteenth Cohort tried to press on uphill, and her cohort went the way of the first waves. Fewer than a hundred of its seven hundred and forty soldiers returned. Holm was not among them. Her implant had been within reach of a Third relay throughout, and she was logged null, because the Echo Mast had been closer.

The main attack on the Founders' Gate fared no better. Eight of the Fifteenth Cohort's fifteen Hoplons were wrecked, and the rest pulled back, firing up the slope until their guns were empty, while the breachers climbed into fire coming down from above and lost all order in the fighting. On the switchback road the dozer-crawlers scraped a track through the mines until lance-fire from the walls left every one of them burning. Behind them, twelve Thureos tanks of the Twenty-Second's Fifth Armoured Company climbed in line astern, firing on the gate-towers, and were knocked out one by one, the last of them reaching the fourth switchback before its crew abandoned it. The burning wrecks blocked the road to any attack that came after. Ahead of the attack, the barrage had closed onto the cone and was overloading the wards as planned, but the heat-wells feeding them were buried far down beneath the vaults, and any harm done to the wards lasted only as long as the shells kept falling. The Twenty-Second's soldiers called the slopes of the cone the Scree, after the loose cinder that slid away under their boots. By the dark watch of the first cycle it was covered from top to bottom with the Twenty-Second's dead.

Saldane renewed the attack on the second cycle with his reserve cohorts, and on the third with soldiers returned from the vats overnight, and both attacks were repulsed on the middle slopes. By the end of the third cycle the Twenty-Second had lost fifty-eight thousand soldiers, and the Répertoire logged more than eleven thousand of them as null, nearly twice the rate it had predicted. The relay crews on the Flats observed that the null rate rose with altitude. Soldiers killed at the foot of the Scree usually reached Watchpoint Hades. Soldiers killed within a kilometre of the gates usually did not. On the fourth cycle, Grace ordered the Twenty-Second to hold the lower Scree and stop attacking until the wards were down, and shifted the weight of the Ordnance Phalanx's fire to the eastern face to help it. The wards would not come down. The heat-wells beneath the cone were deeper than the Répertoire's plans showed, the Archflamen's engineers had evidently sunk new ones during the siege, and the Phaethons could not keep enough shells on any single ward for long enough to overload it. Three cycles into the assault, the Hunt's plan had failed at its first step.

### 11.6 The Gully of Ash (Rotation Eighteen, Cycles 81 to 87)

West of the Twenty-Second, the Thirty-Fifth Trench Regiment under Colonel Theron Kastellan attacked the Gully of Ash, a deep ravine cutting into the western flank of the cone and running up to the Arsenal's great vault doors, the armoured gates of the magazines beneath the Acropolis. The plan for clearing it was intricate. The first objective was a natural trench across the line of advance, which the maps called [[The Seam|the Seam]]. From there two forces would attack the twin strongpoints at the mouth of the gully, while a third swung right to storm the ruins of a ceiling-lance silo thought to have been destroyed by the Phaethons months before, whose broken walls still gave the defenders cover from which to fire along the line of advance. A fourth force would then push into the gully and attack its walls and the vault doors, together with a drop into the gully by Enforcement's Lictor cohort. Once the walls were broken, a further attack would clear the rest of the gully, and the Hephaestean sappers would blow the doors and begin clearing the magazines beyond. Grace expected the assault to take three or four cycles of sustained fighting.

The planners had misjudged the Seam. On the war-table it appeared as a single line, thought to be lightly held. In fact it was a deep crack in the basalt with a steep northern wall, impassable to tanks in many places, and the defenders had dug deep into its walls where the guns could not reach them. The Thirty-Fifth's fire plan, designed for the gully and the plunging fire from the cone above, had given the Seam only a brief shelling by a few batteries of light mortars. The defenders had also hidden tanks in its bottom, with firing positions dug at its edge, from which they could roll up hull-down, fire and withdraw before the Thirty-Fifth could reply. They held their fire until the leading centuries were at short range and then tore them apart. Five Thureos were burning within seconds from flanking shots out of tanks no one could see, and the attack stopped dead under the mortars that followed. The commander of the Third Cohort and six of his eleven centurions were killed in the first volleys. There was no way around the Seam, and the attack went straight at it and made little progress.

Kastellan brought the guns down upon the Seam in haste, but its defenders found shelter beneath its lip, and the regiment's three Colossi found no way across it and lost two of their number to demolition charges thrown from the crack. It was not until the second cycle that the Thirty-Fifth forced its way into the Seam by weight of numbers and began to clear it with grenades and bayonets, finding thralls, Laistrygons, garrison soldiers and Frost Masons side by side within it. The Sixteenth Cohort, whose task was the attack on the ruined silo, had already been badly hurt in the fight for the Seam and lacked the guns to subdue the strongpoint, and its attacks on the silo failed. Only by the end of the third cycle was the Seam, an objective that should have fallen in the first hours, in the Thirty-Fifth's hands. With the attack behind its timetable there was no time for a long barrage on the twin strongpoints at the mouth of the gully, and the assault on them went in under the fire of the Phaethons and of the tanks in the Seam.

The assault planned for three cycles was in its fifth before any of the Third entered the gully itself. The attack on the silo had failed again, and even the arrival of the Steropes-pattern Behemoth Hammer of Lemnos for a third attempt could not get the breachers onto it. Inside the gully, the Thirty-Fifth found a maze of trenches and minefields and tank traps set as densely as a forest, the work of the Frost Masons, and the advance toward the vault doors went forward a few metres at a time under fire from the walls and towers. Behind the regiment, the shells were running short. The attack had outrun the stocks allocated to it, the fire meant to suppress the cone's defenders slackened, and the plunging fire from the top of the gully became murderous, with no cover anywhere. Cohort after cohort was used up in the gully for nothing. The Thirty-Fifth's soldiers called it [[The Kiln|the Kiln]].

It was the seventh cycle of the battle before Grace ordered the Lictor drop, which had been planned for the second cycle and repeatedly postponed. Enforcement's Lictor cohort, almost a hundred and fifty strong, went down into the gully in drop-caskets with four Hunters of the Director's household, behind a wave of fire-caskets that sprayed the landing ground. In the low smoke, with the lip of the gully blocking their view, the Thirty-Fifth's officers could not see the landing and missed the moment for the combined attack on the walls and doors that was meant to go in from both sides at once. The Lictors came down into a trap. The vault doors opened, and out of them came a drowned Behemoth, surrounded by a swarm of thralls, Hollowed and things from the river, followed by the Cinderborn and the Red Furnace under Draugos and his champions. The Thirty-Fifth was ordered to attack the doors in a desperate attempt to reach the Lictors. A Cyclopean lance-tank burned through the doors with repeated shots, but the regiment could not force its way inside, and a charge by its entire outrider force was shot down to the last rider. With that, the last reserve for the assault was gone. Without relief, the Lictor cohort and the four Hunters were destroyed. Those who were wounded or captured were taken by Draugos and handed to the Kindlers, who opened them as hosts, and they would return onto the Flats within a few cycles as the Kindled.

The setbacks did not end in the gully. To the north-east, in one of the many small attacks supporting the main effort, the Thirty-Fifth's Seventeenth Cohort was working through the edge of the ruined forges at the foot of the cone when it was ambushed by the [[Unremembered]], who had come up into the forges by passages unknown to the Third. The whole cohort was surrounded and destroyed before help could be spared from the other attacks. More than seven hundred soldiers were lost, most of them taken alive into the Acropolis to become Hollowed.

By the eighth cycle, the Thirty-Fifth was coming apart in the Kiln. Two-thirds of its breachers were dead, and the relays were logging one in four of its dead as null. The Grey came down into the gully with every Arsenal barrage, the drowned Behemoth was walking down the gully toward the Seam with the Red Furnace behind it, and the regiment's relay bunker for the gully had taken a direct hit on the sixth cycle, so that its fire net was down and every battery still able to fire was firing blind. On the Scree, what remained of the Twenty-Second was being pushed off the slope. Kastellan ordered the Thirty-Fifth to fall back. The assault on the Acropolis was on the point of failure.

The scale of the failure was clear on Quarterdeck Nineteen by the end of the eighth cycle. In eight cycles the Third had taken none of the three gates, had failed to overload a single ward, and had lost a hundred and sixty thousand soldiers, of whom thirty-nine thousand were null, a rate of almost one in four against the Répertoire's estimate of one in six. Two of the four regiments of the first wave were no longer fit to attack, and the Twenty-Sixth's assault on the Slag Gate spur, the last of the three, had yet to begin. Grace's staff prepared two sets of orders for her that night. The first committed the Twenty-Sixth on the following cycle, as planned. The second suspended the assault on the Acropolis, withdrew the first wave to the Wall, and reported to the Director that the cone could not be taken by the means available to the Third. Grace held both until the dark watch. The Hunt's later inquiry found no evidence of which she intended to sign, since events in the Gully of Ash made the choice for her before she had signed either.

### 11.7 The Arrival of the Lantern (Rotation Eighteen, Cycle 88)

In the dark watch of the eighty-eighth cycle, a vessel of the Amaterasu [[Amaterasu Dominion|Dominion]] crossed Depth 40 low beneath the ceiling, through a crack in the ceiling geometry that appears on no chart of the Empire. It was a lantern, a slender craft of gold lacquer shaped like the lamp of a [[Shinto Pantheon|Shinto]] shrine, running for the deep crossings into [[Egyptian Pantheon|Egyptian]] space, and its course had not been meant to bring it near the Flats. It was the only light beneath the ceiling that was not on fire, and the ceiling-lances on the cone found it at once. The second beam took off its tail, and it came down burning onto the upper Scree and slid two hundred metres down the cinder to rest against a wrecked Hoplon at the head of the Gully of Ash.

It carried two passengers. They were the pair whom the Hunt's files had last placed on the Themis Needle in [[Ch23 - Interlude I|Interlude I]], where they had escaped Lieutenant Grace: the boy whom the Director had afterwards marked with her silver crescent in [[Draft - Interlude II|Interlude II]], at the cost of days of his own memory, and the intelligence wearing him, which the Hunt's files name [[Minerva (AI)|Minerva]], together with [[Verrill Chase]], a chemist and relay engineer of the Chase family. Since the Needle, the Dominion's shrine-vats had healed both of them completely. The boy had both arms and both eyes again, his [[Ageis|Aegis]] was whole, and Minerva was running at full capacity for the first time since [[Ch15 - Rex Invictus VIII|Chapter 15]]. Neither had intended to join the siege, and neither had any standing in the Third's order of battle. Their intervention over the following three cycles would nonetheless decide the assault on the Acropolis, in the manner of a strike force of the Empire's oldest warrior orders arriving at the crisis of a battle and turning it.

The pair came down the Scree through the Archflamen's fire and joined the Thirty-Fifth's Ninth Cohort under Centurion [[Liane Marsh]] at the head of the gully, the rounds fired at them stopping in the air short of the boy's Aegis. Minerva's first act was to restore the regiment's fire net, which she did through a relay set still strapped to a dead signaller. The net came back up across the whole of the Thirty-Fifth's front under a new authority that no battery commander could trace, carrying no seal of the regiment, the Seventh Line Phalanx or the Hunt. It did carry a target list. Every battery on the western front of the cone that still had shells received its share of the list in the same instant, with firing solutions and a time on target, covering the Seam, the twin strongpoints, the gully, the ruined silo, the Behemoth and every gun pit on the western face that had fired in the previous six cycles. Battery commanders who asked who was firing received a single answer.

> FIRE. ON MY TIME. QUESTIONS AFTER.

Most of them fired. The rest found their guns laid for them with solutions better than any they could have calculated, and fired after the first salvo. Forty batteries fired together, and every shell arrived in the same second on targets that had been engaged without effect for six cycles. The twin strongpoints at the mouth of the gully were destroyed. The ruined silo that the Sixteenth Cohort had failed to take three times collapsed into its own magazine, and gun pit after gun pit on the western face fell silent. The Archflamen's hull-down tanks in the Seam were struck in the four seconds between rolling up and rolling back, by mortar rounds fired forty seconds earlier at where they were going to be. Kastellan's retreat was halted.

### 11.8 Grey Seventh and the Three-Second Cadence

Verrill Chase, meanwhile, had begun work on [[Grey Seventh]], using residue from a crater where the last Grey shell had burst. The Third's technical officers had concluded that the distillate emptied the uploads of those who breathed it, and it had been the main cause of null transfers in the later rotations of the siege. Chase established that it was a memory solvent, which dissolved the binding holding a soldier's memories in the shape of a person, so that the implant uploaded the whole of the person in no order at all, and the uploads reaching Hades arrived scrambled. She identified the counter in the same examination. A binder that fixed a pattern faster than the Lethe could dissolve it would stop the effect in the lungs, and [[Mnemosyne]] salt, the exact opposite of the Lethe distillate, was used for lineage-keying shells at the breech and was a standard store of every foundry casting divine mortar rounds. Minerva located the nearest stock in a forward magazine in the Seam, three hundred metres away, which the Thirty-Fifth had taken on the second cycle without knowing what it held, and set Chase twenty minutes to build a filter from it.

Minerva then addressed the Mast. The Third's implants were built to upload once at death through the nearest relay, with a window of seven seconds, and on the Flats the nearest relay was the enemy's. Through the same relay set, Minerva changed the cadence of every implant on the Cinder Flats so that each sent its state to Watchpoint Hades every three seconds, continuously, where before it had uploaded once at death. A soldier who died in the Kiln would already be at Hades in all but the last three seconds of his life, and nothing would remain in him for the Mast to take. The implants were the Répertoire's, and no officer of the Third had the authority to alter them. At Watchpoint Hades, thirty-seven depths above the Flats, the Répertoire recorded every implant on the Flats changing cadence at once, under an authority it did not recognise and could not refuse.

> [[CODE 18]] – Speculum Falsum. Origin: Répertoire du Savoir Athénien, Watchpoint Hades. Subject: Unauthorised cadence change, all implants, Depth 40.
> Verdict: Pending.

The vat-crews at the Watchpoint noticed the difference within the hour, as soldiers recovered from the Flats came out of the vats whole. The relay crews on the Flats noticed it sooner. The null rate in the Kiln, which had stood at one in four, fell to nothing and remained there for the rest of the siege.

### 11.9 The Kiln (Rotation Eighteen, Cycle 88)

The drowned Behemoth had by then walked a third of the way down the gully. It was one of the Forge's oldest patterns, crusted black with river growth, and its Phlegethon-cannon fired every forty seconds and set the floor of the gully burning, with the Red Furnace and the Kindled following under Draugos's banner. Minerva went down the gully to meet it on foot, through fire from the walls that stopped short of her Aegis, and a number of the breachers retreating past her turned back to follow. Above the gully, on the western face of the cone, stood the Arsenal's quench-tanks, cisterns of Cocytus brine the size of hab-blocks, piped down from the ice holds above Depth 40 eight thousand years before to cool the casting lines of the Acropolis. Neither side had treated them as part of the war, and the siege had left them intact. Minerva had found them in the Répertoire's plans within her first second on the Scree. Through the Arsenal's own machine net, which the founders had built and the Archflamen's people had never fully understood, she opened the valves.

The brine came down the western face in a white sheet and poured into the gully, freezing as it fell. The Phlegethon fire on the gully floor went out in a scream of steam, as did the river-fire in the Behemoth's cannon and in the Phlegethon-blades of the Red Furnace, and the Kindled faltered. The brine climbed the Behemoth's legs and froze, holding the engine to the knees in grey ice with its cannon cold. With its wards down, the Thirty-Fifth's batteries, now on the new net, put eleven Phaethon rounds into its chest in nine seconds, and it broke open and fell back against the wall of the gully as its reactor died. Minerva then ordered the Thirty-Fifth forward over every set in the regiment at once, telling it that the fire was hers and that its dead were going home. Kastellan repeated the order on his own authority.

Chase had the forward magazine open within four minutes and found eleven tonnes of Mnemosyne salt in sealed jars behind the casting stores. She set a section of the Thirty-Fifth's sappers to knocking the bottoms out of mask canisters and a line of breachers to packing them with salt and charcoal from the magazine's stores, and wrote the recipe onto a signaller's slate, with a drawing of the canister, to be sent to every regiment and every captured magazine on the Flats. Every regiment of the Third had been capturing Mnemosyne salt for eighteen rotations without knowing its use. The signal went out under Minerva's authority. Within the hour, the first Grey Seventh barrage of the night fell upon the Thirty-Fifth in the Kiln, and the soldiers in the gully, wearing Chase's filters, breathed it and went on fighting with their memories intact.

### 11.10 The Turning (Rotation Eighteen, Cycles 88 to 90)

The rout in the Kiln was halted in the dark watch of the eighty-eighth cycle, and by the turn of the next watch it had become an advance. The Thirty-Fifth went back down the gully behind its own barrage, which now fell to the metre where it was needed and moved forward with the breachers. The Kindled met them at the head of the gully, and the brine had affected them in a way the Kindlers had not foreseen. They had been made with river-fire, and while some still burned, others had been returned to something like the soldiers they had once been. The Red Furnace fought on, and Draugos's champions killed many of the Thirty-Fifth that cycle, but without their Phlegethon-blades and the fire behind them they were only warriors, and the Third had always had more warriors than the deep. Draugos withdrew the Red Furnace through the burned-out vault doors into the Acropolis. The Thirty-Fifth followed them to the doors and held them.

On the Scree, the Twenty-Second found fire falling in front of it again where it was needed, and stopped falling back. Saldane had lost two-thirds of his regiment. He held the lower Scree with what remained, and when the new net gave his guns a target list he had not requested, he used it without enquiring into its source. Every soldier of the Twenty-Second who died on the Scree after the dark watch of the eighty-eighth cycle went home whole. The Répertoire's figures for the assault record the effect of the intervention.

> Assault on the Acropolis, Cycles 81 to 87: 160,000 transfers. Null: 39,000.
> Assault on the Acropolis, Cycles 88 to 90: 61,000 transfers. Null: 0.

The Third's command could not establish who had intervened. The Répertoire logged the authority on the net as UNREGISTERED, CLASS UNKNOWN, CARRYING NO SEAL, and its CODE 18 query remained unanswered at the end of the rotation. The soldiers of the Thirty-Fifth said that it had been a boy who came down the Scree out of a burning lantern. Most of the Third did not believe them.

The three cycles that followed the lantern's fall decided the siege. The cadence change ended the Echo Mast's harvest of the Third's dead at a stroke. For more than fifteen rotations the null rate had been the measure by which the Archflamen's war was succeeding, and the Mast had fed upon every soldier who died within its reach. From the eighty-eighth cycle it received nothing more from the Flats. Chase's filter removed the weapon that had done most to swell the null lists in the later rotations of the siege, and with it the means by which the Unremembered had made the Hollowed. The fire net Minerva restored and directed gave the Third, for the first time, artillery that could hit the Archflamen's guns as quickly as they could move. None of the three had been within the power of the Third, the Hunt or the Répertoire to provide, though each had been sought for rotations, and all three were accomplished by two individuals with no place in the order of battle, in the space of a single watch. The Third's later histories credit the turning of the assault to the Thirty-Fifth and to Kastellan, who had the sense to repeat an order whose source he did not know. The Hunt's own records are more exact, and the Répertoire's are silent.

The intervention also posed a problem the Third's command was unable to resolve. An authority that could change every implant on the Flats without a seal could, in principle, change them for any purpose, and the Répertoire treated the cadence change as a breach of its own systems until the end of the siege. Its technical officers recommended that the change be reversed. Grace refused to allow it, on the ground that the Third was in the middle of an assault and that the change was saving soldiers, and the Répertoire did not press the point. The cadence remained at three seconds for the rest of the siege, and the Répertoire has since adopted it as the standard for every implant in the Tartarean theatre, without crediting its author.

### 11.11 The First Victory (Rotation Eighteen, Cycle 89)

On the Slag Gate spur, west of the Kiln, Grace committed the Twenty-Sixth Trench Regiment to the assault on the eighty-ninth cycle, after eighty heavy Wraths had bombed the spur in six waves and hundreds of shells had joined them. When the smoke cleared, the wall of the spur had been breached in at least four places and at least two towers reduced to rubble. The Slag Gate itself still stood, scorched black, with its parapets smashed. Many of its defenders had been crushed in their bunkers by falling stone, but the survivors burrowed into the rubble and made new firing positions in it, the heavy guns that had survived were set up among the broken stone, and the southern bastion of the ceiling-lance silo, the strongest work on the spur, had come through largely intact. For all the destruction, the bombing achieved little. The Twenty-Sixth's assault cohorts went forward with tank companies and Colossi among their centuries, and with them walked Cradle-Lord Varne's [[Anvil of Talos]] and two Steropes, the Bellows of the Forge and the Wakeful, with two more Steropes close by. During the assault, Hunt Second Celyn twice brought Grace a signal from the Thirty-Fifth concerning the Kiln. Both times Grace deferred it.

Despite the bombing, the defenders still had plenty of fight, and the first wave soon slowed to a crawl. The weight of armour, nearly a hundred tanks in that wave alone, and the fire of the Behemoths were breaking up what remained of the walls and the defence lines, though many tanks stuck in the bomb craters and fought as pillboxes until their ammunition ran out. The first centuries were soon fighting in the rubble of the walls, clearing them one stretch at a time. On the right, the Wakeful led the attack on the silo's southern bastion, and there came the first serious setback. The lance buried in the bastion had survived the bombing, and its first beam overloaded the Wakeful's last wards while the second passed through the Behemoth's front plate into its reactor. The Wakeful went up in a ball of white fire, killing everything within a hundred metres, and left nothing behind but scorched ground and a few fragments of melted wreck for the Forge's crews to take home. Another of Talos's engines had been lost. Grace ordered more guns onto the bastion and went forward to the front line with her contingent, where she took direct command of the attack upon it. She called off the Phaethons and sent the leading centuries and their shock breachers in, and by the dark watch they were fighting inside the bastion's tunnels and galleries. Grace and her contingent led the close fighting, and it was Grace who reached the lance chamber and destroyed the lance with a bolt of lightning.

While the Twenty-Sixth took the spur wall, the Archflamen's forces counter-attacked from the Pilgrim Gate high on the cone, pouring down the Pilgrim Stair with tanks and walkers, the furnace-engines of the Cinderborn, iron machines into which living people had been sealed, and the last of the Brethren of the Black Shale, who had submitted to Draugos and had something to prove. The counter-attack swept the Twenty-Sixth back from the wall, and the fight for the rubble changed hands six times through the cycle and the dark watch. By the turn of the watch the rubble was back in the enemy's hands and four of the Twenty-Sixth's cohorts had been broken. Every one of the soldiers lost had gone home whole. The Twenty-Sixth's relay team reported this to Grace at the turn of the watch, and she had no time to consider what it meant.

On the second cycle, as the Twenty-Sixth tried again for the spur, Grace committed her own reserve. Shield-Captain Roe's company and Flame-Captain Tharn's came down from the Selene's Arrow onto the Pilgrim Stair through the moon-ship's translation chambers and struck the counter-attack from behind. The Sixth Moonwall had been trained for ten thousand years against exactly such things as the furnace-engines, and its flame hunters went into the engines and through them. With the Moonwall's strike tipping the balance, Grace led the Twenty-Sixth back up the rubble slope to the top of the spur, and the last of the defenders fell back up the Pilgrim Stair under the southern face of the cone. After two cycles of fighting, what was left of the Slag Gate spur belonged to the Third, the Archflamen's counter-attack had been beaten, and the Third had a base from which to attack the Pilgrim Gate. It was the first victory of the final battle for the Acropolis.

The victory had been costly. The Twenty-Sixth lost thirty-one thousand soldiers on the spur, the Wakeful had been destroyed with its whole crew, and the Moonwall had lost forty Hunters in its strike on the Pilgrim Stair, more than in any single action of the Sixth Moonwall's recent history. Not one of the Twenty-Sixth's dead had gone null. The capture of the spur also gave the Third its first position on the cone itself. From the top of the spur the Third's guns could fire directly into the Pilgrim Gate, and the Pilgrim Stair offered a road up the southern face that tanks could climb, which the Scree and the Gully of Ash did not. The Hunt's staff revised the plan for the next phase of the assault around it within a cycle. The Thirty-Fifth held the vault doors at the head of the gully, the Twenty-Second held the lower Scree, and the Twenty-Sixth held the spur. The ring that had closed on the Cinder Wall in Rotation Sixteen had closed again on the cone. The Archflamen's army still held the three gates, the Katabasion and the Mast, and Draugos still had his door to build, but for the first time since the siege began the Third had reason to believe that it could finish the war.

Only once the spur was secure did Grace take Celyn's signal. It contained the Thirty-Fifth's report, the Répertoire's figures, the unanswered CODE 18 query and a single image from a centurion's helm camera, taken through smoke and steam, of a boy walking down the Gully of Ash ahead of a frozen Behemoth. The steam hid his face. Grace ordered that the centurion be brought to her.

## 12 The Gates

> *"Better broken in body than emptied in mind."*
>
> Hunt-Elder Melaina Sarn

### 12.1 Where the Deep Walks (Rotation Eighteen, Cycles 90 to 158)

The fighting on the Scree had left the Third in possession of the Slag Gate spur and the Gully of Ash, and for the first time in eighteen rotations its regiments stood at the foot of the cinder cone itself. What remained to the Archflamen was the Acropolis and the ground immediately beneath it: the outer ward with its curtain walls and towers, the Katabasion and the Archflamen's palace on the upper terraces, the House of the Hearth on the eastern terrace, and across the gorge that split the cone, the inner ward with the [[Tabularium (Pyriphlegethon)|Tabularium]] and the Echo Mast. The cone could be entered by three gates. The Pilgrim Gate on the southern face stood at the head of the Pilgrim Stair, the old processional route up which pilgrims had climbed to the Katabasion for eight thousand years. The Flamen Gate on the north face opened onto the slope above the old inner belt and gave directly onto the outer ward. The Founders' Gate on the western face, the oldest of the three, stood at the top of the switchback road that carried the Arsenal's traffic up from the vault doors in the Gully of Ash. Every approach to the gates lay under the walls of the outer ward, and every gate was covered by the wards of the Acropolis, which had so far turned the Phaethons aside and left the gatehouses scarred and intact.

The Third that faced the cone was much reduced. The assault on the Scree had cost two hundred and twenty-one thousand transfers in ten cycles, and four of the Twenty-Sixth's cohorts had been broken in the fighting for the spur wall. The Directorate's reductions, imposed over Kastellan's objections, had cut the flow of shells down the [[Cinder Stair]] by a third, and in Rotation Sixteen six regiments had been taken to Lethe Hold under Directive 40-PA-0907. Of the forty-one Trench Regiments that had come down the Stair over the course of the siege, the equivalent of nineteen remained on the Flats at anything like full strength. Most of the ceiling-lances had now been destroyed, and the low geometry above the Flats was open to the Fleet for the first time, though the wards of the Acropolis still stood against anything the Fleet could bring to bear.

Against the Third stood an enemy that had changed in character. The Archflamen's own army, the Sworn, the garrison and the thralls who had held the belts for eighteen rotations, had been reduced to a few hundred thousand soldiers inside the cone, many of them starving and most of them past any hope of relief. The defence of the Acropolis had passed in practice to the Legates of the deep and to what the Kindlers had raised. Since the opening of the Kindlers' door, the deep had walked openly on the cone. The Burned, the hounds of the deep and the bronze bulls of the old forges had been seen on the walls of the outer ward, and the Hunt's sister-augurs reported that the door in the Tabularium was wider than it had ever been. The Third's officers were by now aware that the Archflamen himself no longer commanded. Draugos the Kindled had taken the Arsenal from him, and the defence of the cone answered to the Legates and to Haruspex Dace.

One change in the Third's fortunes was noticed before it was understood. From the eighty-eighth cycle of Rotation Eighteen, the null rate on the fronts nearest the Scree fell sharply. Soldiers transferred in the Gully of Ash and on the Slag Gate spur reached Watchpoint Hades whole, and the Twenty-Sixth's relay team reported at the end of the fighting for the spur that every soldier the regiment had lost had gone home. The Répertoire could not account for the improvement and logged it under the CODE 18 query it had opened on the eighty-eighth cycle. The Hunt's officers connected it with the two figures who had come down onto the Scree in the Dominion lantern. They were correct to do so, though the full explanation would not be known until the Mast.

Lieutenant Grace's decision was to take the gates. The Hunt's staff considered and rejected a slower course, in which the Third would sap up the cone and starve the Acropolis out over a further rotation. The Third could no longer afford a rotation of attrition under the Directorate's reductions, and the sister-augurs advised that every cycle the door remained open made the deep on the cone stronger. The gates would be attacked together, so that the defenders could not concentrate against any one of them, and the forces for the attack would be gathered over the following sixty cycles.

### 12.2 The Witness on the Scree (Rotation Eighteen, Cycle 92)

Two cycles after the battle in the Gully of Ash, the Hunt of the Director took its first formal notice of the two figures who had come down onto the Scree. Lieutenant Thalia Grace had asked for a witness who had seen the Dominion lantern fall, and Centurion Liane Marsh of the [[Thirty-Fifth Trench Regiment]]'s Ninth Cohort, whose maniples had been nearest to it, was brought to the Hunt's command bunker on the Slag Gate spur to report in person. Her account, preserved in the Hunt's interview log, described the lantern burning gold as it came down, a boy climbing out of the wreck, rounds stopping in the air before him, and the moment at which he laid a hand on a dead signaller's relay set and the Thirty-Fifth's fire net came back up. She gave his age as about sixteen, with black hair, sea-green eyes and a small silver crescent visible through his coat over the chest, which she compared to the Lieutenant's own. His companion was a blonde girl with a pencil in her hair who swore freely and addressed him as "mister hero."

The silver crescent was the Director's mark, and the Hunt's officers in the bunker understood at once that the pair on the Scree were the boy and girl who had escaped Grace on the Themis Needle in Interlude I. Grace's reaction to the identification was entered in the log as an expression of disbelief. Hunt Second Celyn reminded her that the Director's standing order applied, under which anything carrying Percy Jackson's scent or signature was to be brought in. Grace's ruling, logged the same watch, was a practical one. The pair were to be located and watched but not approached. If the boy was running the Thirty-Fifth's fire, the Hunt would not take him off it in the middle of the assault on the cone, and no one was to touch him until the gates were down. She reserved the approach to herself. The decision was characteristic of her command. Sent to the Arsenal by the Director as a punishment for losing the same boy on the Needle, she had every personal reason to take him at once, and she chose to leave him in the line where he was useful.

The ruling had consequences for the Hunt's relations with the rest of the Empire's machinery. A standing order of the Director was not a matter for a Lieutenant's discretion, and Hunt Second Celyn entered a formal note of dissent in the Hunt's log, the only one recorded against Grace during her command. The Répertoire, which had no knowledge of the identification, continued to carry the pair as an unregistered authority under its open CODE 18 query. For the sixty cycles that followed, the Psiloi kept the pair under observation as ordered. The boy moved between the Thirty-Fifth's cohorts on the Scree and the sapper galleries under the old inner belt, and was reported repairing relay sets, correcting fire plans and on one occasion standing for most of a watch at the lip of the gorge looking up at the Echo Mast.
